Kaplansky Test Problems for R R -Modules in ZFC Thanks: The second author’s research has been supported by a grant from IPM (No. 1400030417). The third author’s research has been partially supported by Israel Science Foundation (ISF) grant no: 1838/19. This is publication 421 of third author.
Mohsen Asgharzadeh, Mohammad Golshani, Saharon Shelah

Abstract
. Fix a ring R R and look at the class of left R R -modules and naturally we restrict ourselves to the case of rings such that this class is not too similar to the case R R is a field. We shall solve Kaplansky test problems, all three of which say that we do not have decomposition theory, e.g., if the square of one module is isomorphic to the cube of another it does not follows that they are isomorphic. Our results are in the ordinary set theory, ZFC. For this we look at bimodules, i.e., the structures which are simultaneously left R R -modules and right S S -modules with reasonable associativity, over a commutative ring T T included in the centers of R R and of S S . Eventually we shall choose S S to help solve each of the test questions. But first we analyze what can be the smallest endomorphism ring of an ( R , S ) (R,S) -bimodule. We construct such bimodules by using a simple version of the black box.



2010 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary: 20K30; 03C60; 16D20; 03C75; Secondary: 03C45; 13L05

0.
Introduction

Throughout this paper
{\bf R}
is an associative ring with
1=1_{{\bf R}}
, which is not necessarily commutative nor Noetherian. An

{\bf R}
-module is a left
{\bf R}
-module, unless otherwise specified. We shall prove here
for example (see Theorem
6.5
):

Theorem 0.1
.

For every ring
{\bf R}
, one of the following conditions hold:

(a)

all
{\bf R}
-modules are direct sum of countably generated
indecomposable
{\bf R}
-modules (such rings are called left pure semisimple
rings), moreover such a representation is unique up to isomorphism,

or

(b)

for every cardinal
\lambda>|{\bf R}|
satisfying

\lambda=\left(\mu^{\aleph_{0}}\right)^{+}
, and
0<m(\ast),\ell(\ast)<\omega
,
there is an
{\bf R}
-module
{\mathbb{M}}

of cardinality

\lambda

such that

M\cong M^{n}\ \Leftrightarrow\ n\in\{m(\ast)k+\ell(\ast):k<\omega\}.

We shall prove this theorem in
ZFC
, the Zermelo–Fraenkel set theory with the axiom of choice.
Theorem
0.1
is new even if we assume Jensen’s combinatorial principle

diamond
.

Corollary 0.2
.

Let
0<m_{1}<m_{2}-1
and assume that
{\bf R}
is not pure semisimple. Then there is an
{\bf R}
-module of cardinality
\lambda
such
that:

{\mathbb{M}}^{n^{1}}\cong{\mathbb{M}}^{n^{2}}\mbox{ iff }\quad m_{1}<n^{1}\ \&\ m_{1}\leq n^{2}\ \&\ [(m_{2}-m_{1})|(n^{1}-n^{2})]

(
|
means divides).

This result extends several known statements starting with Corner
[
2
]
, and a special case of it, when
{\bf R}
is the ring of integers, was obtained recently
by Göbel-Herden-Shelah

[
21
, Corollary 9.1(iii)]
.

Given an algebraic theory, one of the major objectives is to find some structure theorems for the objects under consideration under
various invariants, say for example up to isomorphism.
In 1954, Kaplansky formulated three test problems (see
[
28
, page 12]
), where in his opinion a structure theory can be satisfactory
only if it can solve these problems. He formulated his questions
in the context of Abelian groups as follows:

(I)

If
{\bf G}
is isomorphic to a direct summand of
{\bf H}
and
{\bf H}
is
isomorphic to a direct summand of
{\bf G}
, are
{\bf G}
and
{\bf H}
necessarily
isomorphic?

(II)

If
{\bf G}\oplus{\bf G}
and
{\bf H}\oplus{\bf H}
are isomorphic, are
{\bf G}
and
{\bf H}

isomorphic?

(III)

If
{\bf F}
is finitely generated and
{\bf F}\oplus{\bf G}
is isomorphic to

{\bf F}\oplus{\bf H}
, are
{\bf G}
and
{\bf H}
isomorphic?

He also noted that
these problems can be formulated for very general mathematical
systems, and he also
mentioned that problem (I) has an affirmative answer in set theory, namely the
Cantor-Schröder-Bernstein theorem.
It may be worth to note that the first two problems were asked earlier, in 1948, independently by Sikorski
[
48
]
and Tarski
[
49
]
in the context of Boolean algebras, where they answered these problems affirmatively
for countably complete Boolean algebras.
Also, Sierpinski asked
the
cube problem
(see below for its statement) in the context of linear orders, see
[
47
]
.

There is a great deal of work on Kaplansky’s test problems over different algebraic structures.
In the sequel we summarize some of them.

For Boolean algebras, the first negative result was proved by Kinoshita
[
30
]
, who answered test question (I)
in negative for countable Boolean algebras. Hanf
[
25
]
answered question (II) in negative for the same class of structures
and
Ketonen
[
29
]
solved, among many other interesting things, the Tarski cube problem by producing a
countable Boolean algebra isomorphic to its cube but not its square.
A solution to the Schroeder-Bernstein problem for Banach spaces
is the subject of
[
24
]
by Gowers.
Very recently, Garrett
[
18
]
proved that every linear order which is isomorphic to its cube, is isomorphic to its square as well,
thus answering Sierpinski’s cube problem for linear orders.

It is known that the problems have positive answer for many classes of Abelian groups, like finitely generated groups, free groups, divisible groups and so on, see
[
17
]
. Jónsson
[
26
]

gave negative answers to test problems (I) and (II) for countable centerless non-commutative groups
and then in
[
27
]
, he gave a negative answer to problem (II)
for the class of torsion-free Abelian groups of rank 2. In 1961 Sasiada
[
35
]
answered the first problem in negative for the class
of torsion-free groups of rank
2^{\aleph_{0}}
.
Corner, in his breakthrough work
[
2
]
, proved that any countable torsion-free
reduced ring can be realized as an endomorphism ring of a torsion-free Abelian
group and deduced from this a negative answer to the Kaplansky’s test problems (I) and (II) for countable torsion-free
reduced groups.
Later, Corner
[
3
]
constructed a countable torsion-free Abelian
group
{\bf G}
, which is isomorphic to
{\bf G}^{3}
but not to
{\bf G}^{2}
, thereby giving a negative answer to the cube problem, which asks if
{\bf G}
is isomorphic to
{\bf G}^{3}
, does it follows that
{\bf G}
is isomorphic to
{\bf G}^{2}
?
As another contribution, Corner proved that for any positive integer
r
there exists a countable torsion-free Abelian
group
{\bf G}
such that
{\bf G}^{m}\cong{\bf G}^{n}
if and only if
m\equiv_{r}n
. This property of
{\bf G}
is called the

Corner pathology.
In particular, the ring of integers has a pathological object.
For countable separable
p
-groups, Elm’s theorem provides an affirmative solution to Kaplansky problems (I) and (II), and
Crawley
[
6
]
, in 1965, showed that this does not extend to the uncountable case by giving a negative answer to these problems for the case of uncountable separable
p
-groups.
Given any
n\geq 2,
Eklof and Shelah
[
16
]
have constructed a locally free Abelian group
G
of cardinality
2^{\aleph_{0}}
such that
G\oplus\mathbb{Z}^{n}\cong G
. Very recently, Richard
[
34
]
has presented more friendly examples of Abelian groups
equipped with the pathological property.
Corner’s ideas were used by a number of mathematicians, to give negative answers to the first two test questions for some classes of Abelian groups. For instance, see
see
[
9
]
,
[
10
]
,
[
11
]

[
15
]
,
[
34
]
, and
[
13
]
.

Shelah’s work
[
46
]

deals with the first test problem of Kaplansky in the category
of modules over a general ring,
however his results were obtained under set theoretic assumptions beyond ZFC.
In fact, Shelah applied
Jensen’s diamond principle, a kind of prediction principle whose truth is independent of ZFC, to present rigid-like modules which violate Kaplansky test problem.

Thomé
[
50
]
and Eklof-Shelah

[
15
]
constructed an
\aleph_{1}
-separable Abelian
group
{\mathbb{M}}
in ZFC such that the Corner’s ring is algebraically
closed in
{\rm End}({\mathbb{M}})
. Consequently
{\mathbb{M}}
breaks down the Kaplansky’s test problem,
i.e., it is isomorphic to its cube but not to its square.

In sum, Kaplansky test problems remained widely open for the category of modules over general rings.

In this paper, we are interested in the category
of modules over a general ring which is not necessarily commutative nor countable.
Working in ZFC, we answer all three
test problems.
This continues
[
46
]
, but can be read independently. Let us stress that all of our
results are in ZFC (without any extra set theoretic axioms), and that we obtain even stronger results than those of
[
46
]
. For example, see Theorem
0.1
.
We will do this by using a simple case of the “
Shelah’s black
box
”, see Lemma
3.43
and Theorem
3.44
.

The black boxes were introduced by Shelah in
[
43
]
and in a more general form in
[
44
]
,
where he showed,
following some ideas from
[
38
, Ch.VII]
, that they follow from ZFC.
We can consider black boxes
as a general method to generate a class of diamond-like principles provable in ZFC.

In
[
36
]
Shelah proved that any ring
{\bf R}
satisfies one of the following two possibilities:

(i)

All modules are direct sums of countably generated modules, or

(ii)

For any cardinal
\lambda>|{\bf R}|
there is an
{\bf R}
-module of cardinality
\lambda
which is not the direct sum of
{\bf R}
-modules of cardinality
\leq\mu
for some
\mu<\lambda
.

Shelah’s work in
[
46
]
extends (ii) in terms of endomorphism algebras. From this and in
{\bf V}={\bf L}
, he constructed an
{\bf R}
-module
{\mathbb{M}}
with prescribed endomorphisms modulo an ideal of small endomorphisms.
Then Shelah found a connection from (ii) to the Kaplansky’s test problems.

Here, we remove the extra assumption
{\bf V}={\bf L}
and show that it is possible to get clause
(b)
of Theorem
0.1
from the condition (ii).

Here, we lose the
\lambda
-freeness (this is unavoidable, even for
Abelian groups- see Magidor and Shelah
[
31
]
).
In particular we prove here
that for each
m(\ast)
, there is an
{\bf R}
-module
{\mathbb{M}}
,
such that
{\mathbb{M}}\cong{\mathbb{M}}^{n}
if and only if

n
divides
m(\ast)
and we also answer the other Kaplansky’s test problems which were
promised
in
[
46
]
. Also here we prove explicitly that the theorems apply to
elementary (= first order) classes of modules which are not superstable.

In the course of the proof of Theorem
0.1
, we develop general methods that allow us to prove the following
results in ZFC. These results provide negative solutions to the Kaplansky’s test problems (I) and (II).

Theorem 0.3
.

Let
{\bf R}
be a ring which is not pure semisimple and let
\lambda>|{\bf R}|
be a regular cardinal of the form
\left(\mu^{\aleph_{0}}\right)^{+}
.
Then there are
{\bf R}
-modules
{\mathbb{M}}_{1}

and
{\mathbb{M}}_{2}
of cardinality
\lambda
such that:

i)

{\mathbb{M}}_{1}
,
{\mathbb{M}}_{2}
are not isomorphic,

ii)

{\mathbb{M}}_{1}
is isomorphic to a direct summand of
{\mathbb{M}}_{2}
,

iii)

{\mathbb{M}}_{2}
is isomorphic to a direct summand of
{\mathbb{M}}_{1}
.

Theorem 0.4
.

Let
{\bf R}
be a ring which is not pure semisimple and let

\lambda=\left(\mu^{\aleph_{0}}\right)^{+}>|{\bf R}|
be a regular cardinal.
Then there are

{\bf R}
-modules
{\mathbb{M}}
,

{\mathbb{M}}_{1}
,
{\mathbb{M}}_{2}
of cardinality
\lambda
such that

i)

{\mathbb{M}}\oplus{\mathbb{M}}_{1}\cong{\mathbb{M}}\oplus{\mathbb{M}}_{2}
,

ii)

{\mathbb{M}}_{1}\not\cong{\mathbb{M}}_{2}
.

In particular, theorems
0.3
and
0.4
improve the results from
[
46
]
, by removing the
use of diamond principle.

As Kaplansky test problems and also our results are related to the indecomposability of modules, let us also
review some background in this direction.
Fuchs
[
17
]
, with some help of Corner, proved, by induction on
\lambda
, the existence of an indecomposable
Abelian group in many cardinals
\lambda
(e.g., up to the first strongly
inaccessible cardinal), and even of a rigid system of
2^{\lambda}
Abelian
groups of power
\lambda
.
It was
conjectured at that time that for some “large cardinal”
(e.g., supercompact) this may fail.
Corner
[
4
]

reduced the number of primes to five and
later, Göbel and May
[
20
]

to four, in the following sense.
Suppose
R
is an algebra over a commutative ring
A
,

\lambda
is an infinite cardinal, and assume that
R
, as an
A
-algebra, can be generated by no more than
\lambda

elements. Let
M=\oplus_{\lambda}R
and suppose in addition that there is an embedding
R\hookrightarrow{\rm End}_{A}(M)
by scalar multiplication. Göbel and May introduced
four
A
-submodules
M_{0},M_{1},M_{2},M_{3}
of
M
such that

R=\{\varphi\in{\rm End}_{A}(M):\varphi(M_{i})\subset M_{i}\emph{forall}0\leq i\leq 3\}.

Shelah
[
39
]
then proved the existence in every
\lambda
(using
stationary sets) of an indecomposable Abelian groups of cardinality
\lambda

(and even a rigid system of
2^{\lambda}
ones).
Lately, Göbel and Ziegler
[
23
]
generalized this to

{\bf R}
–modules for “
{\bf R}
with five ideals”.
Answering a
question of Pierce, Shelah
[
40
]
constructed
essentially decomposable
1
1

1

{\bf G}
is essentially decomposable if
{\bf G}={\bf G}_{1}\oplus{\bf G}_{2}
implies
{\bf G}_{1}

or
{\bf G}_{2}
is bounded.

Abelian

p
-groups for any cardinal
\lambda
, which is strong
limit of uncountable cofinality.

Eklof and Mekler
[
12
]
, using diamond on some
non-reflecting stationary set of ordinals
<\lambda
of cofinality
\aleph_{0}
,
got a
\lambda
-free indecomposable Abelian
group of power
\lambda
. Shelah
[
42
]
continued this where he showed that the diamond can be
replaced by weak diamond on a non-reflecting stationary subset of

S^{\lambda}_{\aleph_{0}}:=\{\delta<\lambda:\mathop{\mathrm{cf}}(\delta)=\aleph_{0}\}

(so for
\lambda=\aleph_{1}
,
2^{\aleph_{0}}<2^{\aleph_{1}}
suffices).

Dugas
[
8
]
continuing
[
12
]
proved, under the assumption
{\bf V}={\bf L}
, the existence of a strongly

\kappa
-free Abelian group with endomorphism ring
{\mathbb{Z}}
and then, by using
p
-adic rings, Göbel
[
19
]
realized to a larger family of
rings.

Dugas and Göbel
[
9
]
, continuing
[
8
]
,
[
19
]
and

[
42
]
, proved that for a Dedekind domain
{\bf R}
which is not a field,
there are arbitrary large indecomposable
{\bf R}
-modules, there are arbitrarily large
{\bf R}
-modules which do not satisfying the Krull-Schmidt cancellation property and also related their work to the Kaplansky’s test problems,
by showing that
{\bf R}
is not a complete discrete valuation ring if and only if there are

{\bf R}
-modules of arbitrary high rank which do not satisfy Kaplansky’s test problems.
They also showed that every cotorsion-free ring is the endomorphism ring of some Abelian group.
Then in
[
10
]
, they
characterized
the rings which can be represented as
{\rm End}({\mathbb{M}})
modulo “the small
endomorphism” for some Abelian
p
-group, but as it continues

[
40
]

(which dealt with the case when we want the smallest such ring) the
representation of a ring
{\bf R}
is by an Abelian group
{\mathbb{M}}
of a
power strong
limit cardinal of cofinality
>|{\bf R}|
. The situation is similar in Dugas and
Göbel
[
11
]
where the results of
[
9
]
and more are obtained in
such cardinals.

Black box enables us to get the results of
[
9
]
,

[
10
]
in more and smaller cardinals, e.g.,
\lambda=\left(|{\bf R}|^{\aleph_{0}}\right)^{+}
.
Corner and Göbel
[
5
]
continued this, and using ideas of Shelah have presented a detailed treatment of construction of Abelian groups (or modules) having preassigned endomorphism rings, and satisfying additional constraints.

For more information,
see
the books Eklof-Mekler
[
14
]
and Göbel-Trlifaj

[
22
]
. The books of Prest
[
32
]
and
[
33
]
talk about
model theory of modules, and in particular on pure semisimple rings.

1.
Outline of the proof of Theorem
0.1

In this section we give an overview of the proof of Theorem
0.1
.
To this end, we deal with the problem of choosing a ring
{\bf S}

(essentially
the ring of
endomorphisms
we would like),
and try to build an

{\bf R}
-module which “has the endomorphisms for

s\in{\bf S}
but not many more”.

In Section
3
, we develop the setting of our construction and to explain it, let us
first present a simple case. Let
{\bf R}
be a ring (with unit

1_{{\bf R}}
) which is
not left purely semisimple (i.e., as in Theorem
0.1
(b)).
Then we consider formulas
\varphi(x)
of the form

(\exists y_{0},\ldots,y_{k_{m}})[\bigwedge\limits_{m<m(\ast)}a_{m}x=\sum\limits_{i<k_{m}}b_{m,i}y_{i}]

where
\{a_{m},b_{m,i}\}

are elements of the ring
{\bf R}
. For
any left
{\bf R}
–module
{\mathbb{M}}
the set
\varphi({\mathbb{M}})=\{x:{\mathbb{M}}\models\varphi(x)\}
is not
necessarily a sub-module (as
{\bf R}
is not
necessarily commutative), but it is an additive subgroup of
{\mathbb{M}}
.
Now, our assumption implies that for some sequence
\bar{\varphi}=\langle\varphi_{n}(x):n<\omega\rangle
, each
\varphi_{n}
is as
above. For every
n
and
{\mathbb{M}}
we have

\varphi_{n+1}({\mathbb{M}})\subseteq\varphi_{n}({\mathbb{M}})
, and for some

{\mathbb{M}}
, the sequence

\langle\varphi_{m}({\mathbb{M}}):m<\omega\rangle

is strictly increasing. Without loss of generality
~k_{n}<k_{n+1}
and:

\varphi_{n}(x)=(\exists\;y_{0},\ldots y_{k_{n-1}-1})(\bigwedge\limits_{m<n}a_{m}x=\sum\limits_{i<k_{m}}b_{m,i}y_{i}).

Let
{\mathbb{N}}_{n}
be the
{\bf R}
–module generated by
x^{n},y^{n}_{i}
(
i<k_{m}
) freely,
except the following relations

a_{m}x^{n}=\sum\limits_{i<k_{n}}b_{m,i}y^{n}_{i}\quad\forall m<n.

Let
g_{n}
be the homomorphism from
{\mathbb{N}}_{n}
into

{\mathbb{N}}_{n+1}
mapping

x^{n}
to
x^{n+1}
and
y^{n}_{i}
to
y^{n+1}_{i}
(for
i<k_{n}
).
Let

{\mathfrak{e}}=\langle{\mathbb{N}}_{n},x_{n},g_{n}:n<\omega\rangle=\langle{\mathbb{N}}^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n},x^{{\mathfrak{e}}}_{n},g^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n}:n<\omega\rangle.

Next, we define the following subgroups of
{\mathbb{M}}
:

\varphi_{n}({\mathbb{M}})=\varphi^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n}({\mathbb{M}}):=\{f(x):f\mbox{ is a homomorphism from }{\mathbb{N}}_{n}\mbox{ to }{\mathbb{M}}\}.

In Definition

3.33
we will define the notion of “semi-nice construction”, which in particular gives a sequence
\bar{{\mathbb{M}}}
of
{\bf R}
-modules, and in Theorem

3.44

we show that the existence of a semi-nice construction follows from
a suitable version of
black boxs. It gives us
an
{\bf R}
–module
{\mathbb{M}}=\bigcup\limits_{\alpha}{\mathbb{M}}_{\alpha}

such that every endomorphism
{{\mathbf{f}}}

of
{\mathbb{M}}
is in a suitable sense trivial: on
general
grounds,
{{\mathbf{f}}}

maps
\varphi_{n}({\mathbb{M}})
into itself, hence
it maps
\varphi^{\mathfrak{e}}_{\omega}({\mathbb{M}})=:\bigcap\limits_{n<\omega}\varphi_{n}({\mathbb{M}})
into itself. So it
induces an additive endomorphism

\hat{{\mathbf{f}}}_{n}={{\mathbf{f}}}\restriction(\varphi_{n}({\mathbb{M}})/\varphi^{\mathfrak{e}}_{\omega}({\mathbb{M}}))
of the Abelian group

\varphi_{n}({\mathbb{M}})/\varphi^{\mathfrak{e}}_{\omega}({\mathbb{M}})
.
By the construction, for some
n=n({\mathbf{f}})
,
the mapping

\hat{{\mathbf{f}}}_{n}

is of the “unavoidable” kind: multiplication by some

a
from the center of
{\bf R}
.

The point is that for undesirable
{{\mathbf{f}}}
,
we will be able to find
x_{n}
and
x

such that
x-x_{n}\in\varphi_{n}({\mathbb{M}})
but for no
y\in{\mathbb{M}}
do we have:

n<\omega\quad\Rightarrow\quad y-{{\mathbf{f}}}(x_{n})\in\varphi_{n}({\mathbb{M}}).

So, omitting countable types helps.
But, we would like to have more: not
to generalize “the Abelian group

{\mathbb{M}}
has no
endomorphism
except multiplication by
a\in{\mathbb{Z}}
”, but to generalize
“the Abelian group
{\mathbb{M}}
has a
prescribed endomorphism ring
{\bf S}
”. For this, we consider a fixed pair

({\bf R},{\bf S})
of
rings and a commutative subring
{\bf T}
of the
center of
{\bf R}
and
the center of
{\bf S}
, and work with

({\bf R},{\bf S})
-bimodules, where by an
({\bf R},{\bf S})
-bimodule, we mean a left
{\bf R}
-module
{\mathbb{M}}
which is
a right
{\bf S}
-module and satisfies the following equations:

\forall r\in{\bf R}
,
\forall s\in{\bf S}
,
(rx)s=r(xs)

and
\forall t\in{\bf T}
,
tx=xt
. We
would like to
build a bimodule
{\mathbb{M}}
such that all of its

{\bf R}
–endomorphisms (i.e., endomorphisms as an
{\bf R}
–module) are,
in a sense,
equal to left multiplication by a member of
{\bf S}
. To be able to
construct such an
{\mathbb{M}}
, we
need to have
{\mathfrak{e}}=\langle{\mathbb{N}}^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n},x^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n},g^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n}:n<\omega\rangle
, but now

{\mathbb{N}}^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n}
is a
bimodule,
x^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n}
and
g^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n}
is a
bimodule homomorphism from
{\mathbb{N}}^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n}
to

{\mathbb{N}}^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n+1}

mapping
x^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n}
to
x^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n+1}
. As a first
approximation, let
\varphi^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n}({\mathbb{M}})
be

\{x:\mbox{ there is an ${\bf R}$-homomorphism from }{\mathbb{N}}^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n}\mbox{ to
}{\mathbb{M}}\mbox{ mapping }x^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n}\mbox{ to }x\}.

Of course,
\varphi^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n}({\mathbb{M}})
is an additive subgroup of

{\mathbb{M}}
. We define

\psi^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n}({\mathbb{M}})
similarly but using bimodule homomorphism.
In fact, we consider a set
{\mathfrak{E}}
of such

{\mathfrak{e}}
’s.

However,
{\mathbb{N}}^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n}
is not necessarily
finitely generated as an

{\bf R}
-module. So let us restrict ourselves to bimodules such that, locally
they look like direct sum of
{\bf R}
-modules from some class
{\mathcal{K}}
. This property is
denoted by
0\leq_{\aleph_{0}}{\mathbb{M}}
(essentially it can be represented
as being

{\mathbb{L}}_{\infty,\aleph_{0}}
-equivalent to such a sum; more fully
0\leq^{{\rm ads}}_{{\mathcal{K}},\aleph_{0}}{\mathbb{M}}
, see Definition
3.15
).
Now, if
0\leq_{\aleph_{0}}{\mathbb{M}}
, we let

\begin{array}[]{r}\varphi^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n}({\mathbb{M}}):=\{x\in{\mathbb{M}}:\mbox{ there is an
${\bf R}$--homomorphism from ${\mathbb{N}}^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n}$ into ${\mathbb{N}}$ }\\
\mbox{mapping $x^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n}$ to $x$}\},\end{array}

and

\begin{array}[]{r}\psi^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n}({\mathbb{M}}):=\{x:\mbox{ there is a bimodule homomorphism from
${\mathbb{N}}^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n}$ into ${\mathbb{N}}$ }\\
\mbox{mapping $x^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n}$ to $x$}\}.\end{array}

Also our complicated set
{\mathfrak{e}}
makes us to define the set

{\mathbb{L}}^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n}
of elements of
{\mathbb{N}}^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n}
whose
image under
bimodule homomorphism is determined by the image of
x^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n}
.

In addition, we
would like to include in our framework
the class of
{\bf R}
-modules of
a fix
first order complete theory
T
;
this is fine for
T
not superstable, but
we need to replace the requirement

0\leq_{\aleph_{0}}{\mathbb{M}}
by “
{\mathbb{M}}^{\ast}\leq_{\aleph_{0}}{\mathbb{M}}
” and
choose
{\mathcal{K}}
and
{\mathbb{M}}^{\ast}

appropriate for
T
. For example,
{\mathbb{M}}^{\ast}

can be any
\aleph_{1}
-saturated model of

T
and

{\mathcal{K}}:=\{{\mathbb{N}}:{\mathbb{N}}\mbox{ is an ${\bf R}$--module such that
}{\mathbb{M}}^{\ast}\prec_{{\mathcal{L}}(\tau_{\bf R})}{\mathbb{M}}^{\ast}\oplus{\mathbb{N}}\},

where
{\mathcal{L}}(\tau_{\bf R})
is the language of
{\bf R}
-modules (see Definition
2.5
).
Also,
{\mathbb{N}}
is not too large,
e.g. it has size at most
\|{\bf R}\|+\|{\bf S}\|.

Let
\bar{\mathbb{M}}=\langle{\mathbb{M}}_{\alpha}:\alpha\leq\kappa\rangle
be a semi-nice construction
and let
{\mathbb{M}}_{\kappa}
be the module defined by
\bar{\mathbb{M}}
.
For any
{\bf f}:{\mathbb{M}}_{\kappa}\to{\mathbb{M}}_{\kappa}
,
\alpha<\kappa

and
n<\omega
, we consider the following principle:
For any bimodule homomorphism

{\bf h}
from

{\mathbb{N}}^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n}
into
{\mathbb{M}}_{\kappa}
, and for
every
\ell<\omega
we have

{\bf f}({\bf h}(x_{n}^{\mathfrak{e}}))\in{\mathbb{M}}_{\alpha}+\varphi^{\mathfrak{e}}_{\ell}({\mathbb{M}}_{\kappa})+\mathop{\mathrm{Rang}}({\bf h}).

We refer to this property by saying that the statement

({\mathord{\mathrm{Pr}}})^{n}_{\alpha}{[}{\mathbf{f}},{\mathfrak{e}}{]}
holds.
In Lemma
4.2
we prove that every

{\bf R}
-endomorphism
{{\mathbf{f}}}

of the module

{\mathbb{M}}_{\kappa}
constructed in Section
3
is “somewhat definable” and
specifically satisfies

({\mathord{\mathrm{Pr}}})^{n(\ast)}_{\alpha}[{{\mathbf{f}}},{\mathfrak{e}}]

(for some

\alpha<\kappa
,
n(\ast)<\omega
and for all the
\mathfrak{e}
’s we have taken care
of). We show that
({\mathord{\mathrm{Pr}}})^{n(\ast)}_{\alpha}[{{\mathbf{f}}},{\mathfrak{e}}]
implies a stronger version, denoted by

({\mathord{\mathrm{Pr}}}1)^{n(\ast)}_{\alpha,z}[{{\mathbf{f}}},{\mathfrak{e}}]

where

z\in{\mathbb{L}}^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n}
.
More explicitly, if
h
is a bimodule homomorphism
from
{\mathbb{N}}^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n}
into
{\mathbb{M}}_{\kappa}
,
({\mathord{\mathrm{Pr}}}1)^{n(\ast)}_{\alpha,z}[{{\mathbf{f}}},{\mathfrak{e}}]
implies that

{\bf f}(h(x^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n}))-h(z)\in{\mathbb{M}}_{\alpha}+\varphi^{\mathfrak{e}}_{\omega}({\mathbb{M}}_{\kappa}).

This is the subject of Lemma
4.7
.

In Section
5

we try to say more: in
{\mathbb{M}}_{\kappa}
every endomorphism is in some suitable
sense equal to one in a ring
{\bf{dE}}
, whose definition is given in Lemma
5.24
(iii). Here, we
remark that
the “in some sense equal” means: for
each
n
we restrict
{{\mathbf{f}}}

to an additive sub-group

\varphi^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n}({\mathbb{M}}_{\kappa})

(closed under
{{\mathbf{f}}}
),
divide by another

\varphi^{\mathfrak{e}}_{\omega}({\mathbb{M}}_{\kappa})

and take direct
limit;
on the top of this we have an “error term”: we have to divide by a
“small” sub-module of
{\mathbb{M}}_{\kappa}
, which means of cardinality

<\lambda=\|{\mathbb{M}}_{\kappa}\|
. A better result is: we divide the ring of such
endomorphisms by the ideal of those with “small” range and then even
“compact ones” which are essentially of cardinality

\leq\mathop{\mathrm{sup}}\{\|{\mathbb{M}}\|:{\mathbb{M}}\in{\mathcal{K}}\}
.
We close
Section
5
by proving the following result.

Corollary 1.1
.

Suppose
{\bf S}
is a ring extending
\mathbb{Z}
such that
({\bf S},+)
is free
and let
{\bf R}
be a ring which is not pure semisimple. Let
{\bf D}
be a
field such that
p:=\operatorname{char}({\bf D})|\operatorname{char}({\bf R})
and set

{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}:={\mathbb{Z}}/p{\mathbb{Z}}
if
p>0

and
{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}:={\mathbb{Z}}
otherwise.
Let
\Sigma
be a set of
equations over
{\bf S}
not solvable in
{\dot{\bf D}}\mathop{{\otimes}}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}}({\bf S}/p{\bf S})
. Then for
{\mathbb{M}}
which is strongly nicely constructed,

\Sigma
is not
solvable in
{\rm End}({\mathbb{M}})
.

Let us now explain how the above constructions can be applied to prove Theorem
0.1

(for more details see Section
6
). We first introduce
a ring
{\bf S}_{0}
, it is incredibly easy compared to
{\bf S}
.
To this end, let
{\bf T}
be the subring of

{\bf R}
which 1
generates. Let
{\bf S}_{0}
be the ring extending
{\bf T}

generated by
\{{\mathcal{X}}_{0},\ldots,{\mathcal{X}}_{m(\ast)-1},{\mathcal{W}},{\mathcal{Z}}\}
freely except:

(\ast)_{1}
:

{\mathcal{X}}^{2}_{\ell}={\mathcal{X}}_{\ell}
,

{\mathcal{X}}_{\ell}{\mathcal{X}}_{m}=0
(
\ell\neq m
),

1={\mathcal{X}}_{0}+\ldots+{\mathcal{X}}_{m(\ast)-1}
,

{\mathcal{X}}_{\ell}{\mathcal{W}}{\mathcal{X}}_{m}=0
for
\ell+1\neq m\ \mod\ m(\ast)
,

{\mathcal{W}}^{m(\ast)}=1
,

{\mathcal{Z}}^{2}=1
,

{\mathcal{X}}_{0}{\mathcal{Z}}(1-X_{0})={\mathcal{X}}_{0}{\mathcal{Z}}
,

(1-{\mathcal{X}}_{0}){\mathcal{Z}}{\mathcal{X}}_{0}=(1-{\mathcal{X}}_{0}){\mathcal{Z}}
.

For an integer
m
let
[-\infty,m):=\{n:n\mbox{ is an integer and }~n<m\}
. To each

\eta\in{}^{[-\infty,m)}\omega
we assign
\eta\restriction k:=\eta\restriction[-\infty,\mathop{\mathrm{min}}\{m,k\})
.
We look at

\begin{array}[]{lll}W_{0}:=&\big\{\eta:&\eta\mbox{ is a function with domain of the form }[-\infty,n)\\
&&\mbox{and range a subset of }\{1,\ldots,m(\ast)-1\}\mbox{ such that}\\
&&\mbox{for every small enough }m\in{\mathbb{Z}},\ \eta(m)=1\big\}\end{array}

and
W_{1}:=W_{0}\times\{0,\ldots,m(\ast)-1\}
.
Let
\bf D
be a field such that

{\bf T}\mbox{ is finite }\quad\Rightarrow\quad\operatorname{char}({\bf D})\mbox{
divides }|{\bf T}|.

So,
{\bf D}\otimes{\bf S}
is the ring extending
\bf D
by adding

{\bf D},{\mathcal{X}}_{0},\ldots,{\mathcal{X}}_{m(\ast)-1},{\mathcal{W}},{\mathcal{Z}}

as non commuting variables freely except satisfying the equations in
(\ast)_{1}
.
Let
{\mathbb{M}}^{\ast}={}_{\bf D}{\mathbb{M}}^{\ast}
be the left

{\bf D}
-module freely generated by

\{x_{\eta,\ell}:\eta\in W_{0},\ \ell<m(\ast)\}.

We make
{}_{\bf D}{\mathbb{M}}^{\ast}
to a right

({\bf D}\mathop{{\otimes}}{\bf S}_{0})
-module by defining
xz
for
x\in{}_{{\bf D}}{\mathbb{M}}^{*}
and
z\in{\bf S}_{0}
, so it is enough to deal with

z\in\{{\mathcal{X}}_{m}:m<m(*)\}\cup\{{\mathcal{Z}},{\mathcal{W}}\}
. Let
x=\sum\limits_{\eta,\ell}a_{\eta,\ell}x_{\eta,\ell}
where

(\eta,\ell)
vary on

W_{0}
and
a_{\eta,\ell}\in{\bf D}
and
\{(\eta,\ell):a_{\eta,\ell}\neq 0\}

is finite and we let
\sum\limits_{\eta,\ell}a_{\eta,\ell}x_{\eta,\ell})z=\sum\limits_{\eta,\ell}a_{\eta,\ell}(x_{\eta,\ell}z),
where

\begin{array}[]{rcl}x_{\eta,\ell}{\mathcal{X}}_{m}&:=&\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}x_{\eta,\ell}&\mbox{if }\ell=m,\\
0&\mbox{if }\ell\neq m,\end{array}\right.\\
\\
x_{\eta,\ell}{\mathcal{Z}}&:=&\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}x_{\eta{}^{\frown}\!\langle\ell\rangle,0}&\mbox{if }\ell>0,\\
x_{\eta\restriction[-\infty,n-1),\eta(n-1)}&\mbox{if }\ell=0,\mbox{
and }(-\infty,n)=\mathop{\mathrm{Dom}}(\eta),\end{array}\right.\\
and\\
x_{\eta,\ell}{\mathcal{W}}&:=&x_{\eta,m}\ \mbox{ when }m=\ell+1\ \mod\ m(\ast).\end{array}

We get a
({\bf D},{\bf S})
-bimodule. Let
{}_{\bf D}{\mathbb{M}}^{\ast}_{\ell}=\{\sum\limits_{\eta}d_{\eta,\ell}x_{\eta,\ell}:\ \eta\in W_{0}
and
d_{\eta,\ell}\in{\bf D}\}
, so
{}_{\bf D}{\mathbb{M}}^{\ast}=\mathop{{\bigoplus}}\limits_{\ell=0}^{m(\ast)-1}{}_{\bf D}{\mathbb{M}}^{\ast}_{\ell}
.

Now we are ready to define
{\bf S}
, but in addition
\sigma=0

when
{\mathbb{M}}^{\ast}_{{\bf D}}\sigma=0

for every
{\bf D}
and every
({\bf D},{\bf S}_{0})
-bimodule
{}_{\bf D}{\mathbb{M}}^{*}
as
defined above.

We will prove that
{\bf S}

is a free
{\bf T}
-module. This allow us to apply Corollary
1.1
.
Look at
{\mathbb{P}}
, the bimodule induced by the semi-nice construction equip with the strong property presented in above. Let
{\mathbb{P}}_{\ell}={\mathbb{P}}{\mathcal{X}}_{\ell}
. It follows easily that
\mathop{{\bigoplus}}\limits^{m(\ast)-1}_{\ell=1}{}_{\bf R}{\mathbb{P}}_{\ell}\cong({}_{\bf R}{\mathbb{P}}_{0})^{m(\ast)-1}.
It is enough to show
{}_{\bf R}{\mathbb{P}}^{k}_{0}\not\cong{}_{{\bf R}}{\mathbb{P}}_{0}
for all
1<k<m(\ast)-1
.
Assume
k
is a counterexample.
We apply Corollary
1.1
to find
a field
{\bf D}
and
{\mathcal{Y}}\in{\bf D}\mathop{{\otimes}}\limits_{\bf T}{\bf S}
satisfying the
following equations:

(*)_{2}
:

{\mathcal{Y}}\restriction{}_{\bf D}{\mathbb{M}}^{\ast}_{0}
is an
isomorphism from

{}_{\bf D}{\mathbb{M}}^{\ast}_{0}
onto
\mathop{{\bigoplus}}\limits_{\ell=1}^{k}{}_{\bf D}{\mathbb{M}}^{\ast}_{\ell}
,

{\mathcal{Y}}\restriction\mathop{{\bigoplus}}\limits_{\ell=1}^{k}{}_{\bf D}{\mathbb{M}}^{\ast}_{\ell}
is an
isomorphism from
\mathop{{\bigoplus}}\limits_{\ell=1}^{k}{}_{\bf D}{\mathbb{M}}^{\ast}_{\ell}
onto
{}_{\bf D}{\mathbb{M}}^{\ast}_{0}
,

{\mathcal{Y}}\restriction\mathop{{\bigoplus}}\limits^{m(\ast)}_{\ell=k+1}{}_{\bf D}{\mathbb{M}}^{\ast}_{\ell}
is the identity

{\mathcal{Y}}^{2}=1
.

Then, we look at the following sets:

\begin{array}[]{ll}w_{\eta,\ell}&:=\{(\nu,m)|(\nu,m)=(\eta,\ell)\mbox{ or }\eta^{\frown}\langle\ell\rangle\trianglelefteq\nu~\mbox{and}~m<m(*)\}\\
u_{\ell}&:=\{(\eta,m)|m=\ell,\ \eta\in w_{0}\}\\
w^{m}_{\eta,\ell}&:=\ w_{\eta,\ell}\cap u_{m}\\
w^{[1,n]}_{\eta,\ell}&:=w_{\eta,\ell}\cap\bigcup\limits_{m\in[1,n]}u_{m}.\end{array}

For any
u\subseteq\{(\eta,\ell):\eta\in W_{0},\ \ell<m(\ast)\}
, we let
{\mathbb{N}}_{u}
be
the sub
{\bf D}
-module of
{}_{\bf D}{\mathbb{M}}^{\ast}
generated by

\{x_{\eta,\ell}:(\eta,\ell)\in u\}
. For every large enough finite subset
v\subseteq w_{\eta,\ell}
,
we show the following is well defined:

{\bf n}_{\eta,\ell}:=\dim\left(\frac{{\mathbb{N}}_{w^{0}_{\eta,\ell}}}{{\mathbb{N}}_{w^{0}_{\eta,\ell}\setminus v}}\right)-\dim\left(\frac{{\mathbb{N}}_{w^{[1,k]}_{\eta,\ell}}}{{\mathbb{N}}_{w^{0}_{\eta,\ell}\setminus v}{\mathcal{Y}}}\right).

These integers are independent from the first factor: Suppose
\eta
,
\nu\in w_{0}
and
\ell\in\{0,1,\ldots,m(\ast)-1\}
then
{\bf n}_{\eta,\ell}={\bf n}_{\nu,\ell}
so we shall write

{\bf n}_{\ell}
.
These numbers are such that they satisfy the following equations:

{\bf n}_{\ell}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}0&\mbox{if }\ell\in[1,k]\\
{\bf n}_{0}+{\bf n}_{1}+\ldots+{\bf n}_{m(\ast)-1}&\mbox{if }\ell\in[k+1,m(\ast))\emph{or}\ell=0\end{array}\right.

Finally, we use this equation to derive the following contradiction

\sum^{m(\ast)-1}_{\ell=1}{\bf n}_{\ell}=\frac{k}{m(\ast)-1}.

2.
Preliminaries from algebra and logic

In this section we provide some preliminaries from algebra and
logic that are needed for the rest of the paper. Let us start
by fixing some notations that we will use through the paper.
Recall that
{\bf R}
is a ring, not necessary commutative, with
1=1_{\bf R}
.

Convention 2.1
.

By
\mathop{\mathrm{Cent}}(-)
we mean the center of a ring
(-)
.
The rings

{\bf S}
and
{\bf R}
are with
1
, and we always assume that
{\bf T}:=\mathop{\mathrm{Cent}}({\bf R})\cap\mathop{\mathrm{Cent}}({\bf S})
is a commutative
ring.
2
2

2

Indeed, in our applications we have
{\bf T}={\bf R}\cap{\bf S}
(see Section 6, below).

Definition 2.2
.

An
({\bf R},{\bf S})
-bimodule
{\mathbb{M}}
is a left
{\bf R}
-module and right

{\bf S}
-module such that for all
r\in{\bf R},x\in{\mathbb{M}}
and
~s\in{\bf S}
we have

(rx)s=r(xs)
and that
tx=xt
for all
t\in{\bf T}
. When the pair
({\bf R},{\bf S})
is clear from the context, we refer to
{\mathbb{M}}
as a bimodule.

Convention 2.3
.

We use
{\mathbb{K}}
,
{\mathbb{M}}
,

{\mathbb{N}}
and
{\mathbb{P}}

to denote bimodules (or left

{\bf R}
-modules).

Definition 2.4
.

Let
{\bf f}:{\mathbb{M}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{N}}
be a bimodule
homomorphism.

(1)

The kernel,

\mathop{\mathrm{Ker}}({\bf f}):=\{x\in{\mathbb{M}}:{\bf f}(x)=0\}

is a sub-bimodule of
{\mathbb{M}}
.

(2)

The image,

\mathop{\mathrm{Rang}}({\bf f}):=\{{\bf f}(x):x\in{\mathbb{M}}\}

is a sub-bimodule of
{\mathbb{N}}
.

(3)

If
{\mathbb{N}}
is a sub-bimodule of
{\mathbb{M}}
then

{\mathbb{M}}/{\mathbb{N}}:=\{x+{\mathbb{N}}:x\in{\mathbb{M}}\}

is a homomorphic image of
{\mathbb{M}}
. The mapping
x\mapsto x+{\mathbb{N}}
is a
homomorphism with kernel
{\mathbb{N}}
.

(4)

For a bimodule
{\mathbb{M}},{\rm End}_{\bf R}({\mathbb{M}})

is the endomorphism ring of

{\mathbb{M}}
as a
left
{\bf R}
-module.

In this paper we also consider modules and bimodules as logical structures, so let us fix a language for them. We only sketch the basic
definitions and results which are needed here, and refer to
[
1
]
and
[
7
]
for further information.

Definition 2.5
.

(1)

An
{\bf R}
-module
{\mathbb{M}}
is considered as a
\tau_{\bf R}
-structure
where
\tau_{\bf R}=\{0,+,-\}\cup\{{}_{r}H:r\in{\bf R}\},
where the universe
is the set
of elements of
{\mathbb{M}}
, and
0,+,-

are interpreted naturally and
{}_{r}H
is
interpreted as (left) multiplication by
r
(i.e.,
{}_{r}H(x):=rx
).
Then

(a)

Terms of
\tau_{\bf R}
are expressions of the form
\sum_{i<m}r_{i}x_{i}
, where
m<\omega,

r_{i}
is in
{\bf R}

and
x_{i}
is a variable.

(b)

Atomic formulas of
\tau_{\bf R}
are equations of the form
t_{1}=t_{2}
, where
t_{1},t_{2}
are terms.

(2)

An
({\bf R},{\bf S})
-bimodule
is similarly interpreted as a
\tau_{({\bf R},{\bf S})}
- structure
where

\tau_{({\bf R},{\bf S})}=\{0,+,-\}\cup\{{}_{r}H:r\in{\bf R}\}\cup\{H_{s}:s\in{\bf S}\}

and
{}_{r}H,H_{s}
for
r\in{\bf R},s\in{\bf S}

are unary function symbol, which will be interpreted as
follows:
{}_{r}H
as left multiplication by
r
(i.e.,
{}_{r}H(x):=rx
)
and
H_{s}
as
right multiplication by
s
(i.e.,
H_{s}(x)=xs
).
Then

(a)

Terms of
\tau_{({\bf R},{\bf S})}
are expressions of the form

\sum_{i<d}\sum_{h<h_{d}}r_{i,h}x_{i}s_{i,h}

where
d,h_{d}<\omega,

r_{i,h}\in{\bf R}

s_{i,h}\in{\bf S}

and
x_{i}
is a variable.

(b)

Atomic formulas of
\tau_{({\bf R},{\bf S})}
are equations of the form
t_{1}=t_{2}
, where
t_{1},t_{2}
are terms.

The next lemma is trivial.

Lemma 2.6
.

The class of
({\bf R},{\bf S})
-bimodules is a variety, i.e., there are equations in the language of
\tau_{({\bf R},{\bf S})}
such that a

\tau_{({\bf R},{\bf S})}
-structure is an
({\bf R},{\bf S})
-bimodule iff it satisfies these equations.

Let us now introduce the infinitary languages for modules and bimodules. They will play essential roles in the sequel.

Definition 2.7
.

Suppose
\kappa
and
\mu

are infinite cardinals, which we allow to be
\infty
, and let
\tau

be one of
\tau_{{\bf R}}
or
\tau_{({\bf R},{\bf S})}
. The infinitary language
\mathcal{L}_{\mu,\kappa}(\tau)

is defined so as its vocabulary is the same as
\tau,
it has the same terms and atomic formulas as in
\tau,
but we also allow conjunction and disjunction of length less than
\mu
(i.e., if
\phi_{j},
for
j<\beta<\mu
are formulas, then so are
\bigvee_{j<\beta}\phi_{j}
and
\bigwedge_{j<\beta}\phi_{j}
) and quantification over less than
\kappa
many variables (i.e., if
\phi=\phi((v_{i})_{i<\alpha})
, where
\alpha<\kappa
, is a formula, then so are
\forall_{i<\alpha}v_{i}\phi
and
\exists_{i<\alpha}v_{i}\phi
).

Note that
\mathcal{L}_{\omega,\omega}(\tau)
is just the first order logic with vocabulary
\tau.

Given
\kappa
,
\mu
and
\tau
as above, we are sometimes interested in some special formulas from
\mathcal{L}_{\mu,\kappa}(\tau)
.

Definition 2.8
.

(1)

\mathcal{L}^{\text{cpe}}_{\mu,\kappa}(\tau)
, the class of conjunctive positive existential formulas, consists of those formulas of
\mathcal{L}_{\mu,\kappa}(\tau)
which in their formulation
only
\wedge,\bigwedge_{j<\beta},\exists x
and
\exists_{i<\alpha}v_{i}
are used (where
\beta<\mu
and
\alpha<\kappa
).

(2)

\mathcal{L}^{\text{pe}}_{\mu,\kappa}(\tau)
, the class of positive existential formulas, is defined similarly where we also allow
\vee
and
\bigvee_{j<\beta}

(3)

\mathcal{L}^{\text{p}}_{\mu,\kappa}(\tau)
, the class of positive formulas, is defined similarly where we allow
\vee
,
\bigvee_{j<\beta}
and also
the universal quantifiers
\forall x
and
\forall_{i<\alpha}v_{i}
.

(4)

By a simple formula of
\mathcal{L}_{\mu,\kappa}(\tau)
, we mean a formula of the form

\phi=\exists_{i<\alpha}v_{i}[\bigwedge_{j<\beta}\phi_{j}],

where each
\phi_{j}=\phi_{j}((v_{i})_{i<\alpha})
is an atomic formula.

Lemma 2.9
.

The following assertions are valid:

(1)

Suppose
{\bf f}:{\mathbb{M}}\to{\mathbb{N}}
is an
({\bf R},{\bf S})
-bimodule homomorphism. Then
{\bf f}
preserves
\mathcal{L}_{\infty,\infty}^{\text{pe}}(\tau_{({\bf R},{\bf S})})
-formulas.

(2)

Suppose in addition to 1) that
f
is surjective. Then
{\bf f}
preserves
\mathcal{L}_{\infty,\infty}^{\text{p}}(\tau_{({\bf R},{\bf S})})
-formulas.

Proof.

(1). Suppose
\phi((v_{i})_{i<\alpha})\in\mathcal{L}_{\infty,\infty}^{\text{pe}}(\tau_{({\bf R},{\bf S})})
and let
a_{i}\in{\mathbb{M}},
where
i<\alpha.
We need to show:

{\mathbb{M}}\models\phi((a_{i})_{i<\alpha})\Rightarrow{\mathbb{N}}\models\phi(({\bf f}(a_{i}))_{i<\alpha}).

This can be proved by induction on the complexity of the formulas, and we leave its routine check to the reader.

(2). This can be proved in a similar way; let us only consider the case of the universal formula to show how the surjectivity of the function
\bf f

is used. Thus suppose that
\phi((v_{i})_{i<\alpha})=\forall x\psi((v_{i})_{i<\alpha},x)
is in
\mathcal{L}_{\infty,\infty}^{\text{pe}}(\tau_{({\bf R},{\bf S})})
and let
a_{i}\in{\mathbb{M}},
where
i<\alpha.
We may assume that the lemma holds for
\psi.
Suppose
{\mathbb{M}}\models\phi((a_{i})_{i<\alpha})
. We show that
{\mathbb{N}}\models\phi(({\bf f}(a_{i}))_{i<\alpha})
. Thus let
b\in{\mathbb{N}}
. As
\bf f
is surjective, there exists
a\in{\mathbb{M}}
such that
{\bf f}(a)=b
and by the assumption,
{\mathbb{M}}\models\psi((a_{i})_{i<\alpha},a)
. By the induction hypothesis,
{\mathbb{N}}\models\psi(({\bf f}(a_{i}))_{i<\alpha},{\bf f}(a))
, i.e.,
{\mathbb{N}}\models\psi(({\bf f}(a_{i}))_{i<\alpha},b)
. Since
b
was arbitrary we have
{\mathbb{N}}\models\phi(({\bf f}(a_{i}))_{i<\alpha})
.
∎

Remark 2.10
.

Note that there is no need for
{\bf f}
to preserve
\neg
formulas. But if
{\bf f}
is an isomorphism, then it preserves all formulas.

The next lemma shows that under suitable conditions, we can replace
\rm cpe
-formulas by simple formulas.

Lemma 2.11
.

Let
\tau
be either
\tau_{{\bf R}}
or
\tau_{({\bf R},{\bf S})}
, and suppose
\mu_{1}\geq\mu,\kappa
is
regular.
If
\varphi(\bar{x})\in\mathcal{L}^{\rm cpe}_{\mu,\kappa}(\tau)
, then
we can find as equivalent simple formula in

\mathcal{L}_{\mu_{1},\mu_{1}}(\tau)
.

We are also interested in definable subsets of (bi)modules.

Definition 2.12
.

Let
\tau

be one of
\tau_{{\bf R}}
or
\tau_{({\bf R},{\bf S})}
. Given a
\tau
-structure
{\mathbb{M}}
and a formula
\phi(x_{0},\cdots,x_{n-1})
in
\mathcal{L}_{\infty,\infty}(\tau)
, let

\phi({\mathbb{M}})=\{\langle a_{0},\cdots,a_{n-1}\rangle\in~^{n}{{\mathbb{M}}}:M\models\phi(a_{0},\cdots,a_{n-1})\}.

Here, by
\mathop{\rm\ell g}(-)
we mean the length function.

Lemma 2.13
.

Let
\tau
be either
\tau_{{\bf R}}
or
\tau_{({\bf R},{\bf S})}
, and let

\varphi(\bar{x})\in\mathcal{L}_{\mu,\kappa}^{\rm p}(\tau)
.
Suppose
\bar{z}_{\ell}\in{}^{\mathop{\rm\ell g}(\bar{x})}{\mathbb{M}}_{\ell}
, for
\ell=1,2
and

\bar{z}_{\ell}=\langle z^{\ell}_{i}:i<\mathop{\rm\ell g}(\bar{x})\rangle
,

{\mathbb{M}}={\mathbb{M}}_{1}\oplus{\mathbb{M}}_{2}
,
and
\bar{z}=\langle z_{i}:i<\mathop{\rm\ell g}(\bar{x})\rangle
where

{\mathbb{M}}\models z_{i}=z^{1}_{i}+z^{2}_{i}
.
Then

{\mathbb{M}}\models\varphi(\bar{z})\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\bigwedge\limits_{\ell=1}^{2}{\mathbb{M}}_{\ell}\models\varphi(\bar{z}_{\ell}).

Furthermore, if
for
\ell=1,2
and
i<\mathop{\rm\ell g}(\bar{x}),z^{\ell}_{i}=0_{M_{\ell}}
, then
{\mathbb{M}}_{\ell}\models\varphi(\bar{z}_{\ell})

and

\bar{z}=\bar{0}_{\mathop{\rm\ell g}(\bar{z})}
.

Proof.

The desired claim follows by an easy induction on the complexity of the formula

\varphi(\bar{x})
.
∎

The above lemma implies if
\varphi(x)\in\mathcal{L}_{\mu,\kappa}^{\rm cpe}(\tau),
where

\tau
is
\tau_{{\bf R}}
or
\tau_{({\bf R},{\bf S})}
and if
{\mathbb{M}}={\mathbb{M}}_{1}\oplus{\mathbb{M}}_{2}
, then

\varphi({\mathbb{M}})=\varphi({\mathbb{M}}_{1})\oplus\varphi({\mathbb{M}}_{2})
.

Lemma 2.14
.

For each bimodule
{\mathbb{M}}
, the following assertions hold:

(1)

If
\varphi(x)\in\mathcal{L}^{{\rm cpe}}_{\infty,\infty}(\tau_{({\bf R},{\bf S})})
, then
\varphi({\mathbb{M}})

is a subgroup of

{\mathbb{M}}
.

(2)

If

\varphi(x)\in\mathcal{L}^{{\rm cpe}}_{\infty,\infty}(\tau_{{\bf R}})
,
then
\varphi({\mathbb{M}})
is a sub-right

{\bf S}
-module, but not
necessarily
a sub-
{\bf R}
-module. Furthermore, if
{\bf R}
is commutative, then
it is a sub-
{\bf R}
-module as well.

Proof.

This follows by induction on the complexity
of
\varphi(x).
The straightforward details are leave to the reader.
∎

Another notion from model theory which is of importance for us is the notion of omitting types:

Discussion 2.15
.

Suppose
\tau
is a countable languages
and suppose
\mathcal{M}
is a
\tau
-structure.

(1)

Given
A\subseteq\mathcal{M},
by an
n
-type over
A
we mean a set

p(v_{1},\cdots,v_{n})
of formulas, whose free variables are
v_{1},\cdots,v_{n}
such that every finite
p_{0}\subseteq p

is realized, i.e., there are
x_{1},\cdots,x_{n}
in
\mathcal{M}
such that for all
\varphi\in p_{0},~\mathcal{M}\models\varphi(x_{1},\cdots,x_{n})
.

(2)

A type is complete if it is maximal under inclusion. By the axiom of choice each type can be extended into a complete type.

(3)

The type
p
is isolated by some formula
\psi(v_{1},\cdots,v_{n})\in p
if for every
\varphi\in p,

\mathcal{M}\models\forall x_{1}\cdots\forall x_{n}(\psi(x_{1},\cdots x_{n})\rightarrow\varphi(x_{1},\cdots,x_{n})).

It is clear that if
p
is isolated by some formula
\psi(v_{1},\cdots,v_{n})\in p
, then it is realized. The omitting types theorem says that the converse is also true.

Lemma 2.16
.

Let
\tau
be a first order countable vocabulary and let
T
be a complete
\tau
-theory.
If
p
is a complete type which is not isolated, then there is a countable
\tau
-structure
\mathcal{M}\models T
which omits (i.e., does not realize)
p
.

Definition 2.17
.

Suppose that
{\mathbb{M}}_{0},{\mathbb{M}}_{1}
and
{\mathbb{M}}_{2}
are
(bi)modules and
\mathbb{g}_{i}:{\mathbb{M}}_{0}\to{\mathbb{M}}_{i}
are (bi)module homomorphisms
for
i=1,2
. The amalgamation of
{\mathbb{M}}_{1}
and
{\mathbb{M}}_{2}
along
{\mathbb{M}}_{0}

is
{\mathbb{M}}:=\frac{{\mathbb{M}}_{1}\oplus{\mathbb{M}}_{2}}{(\mathbb{g}_{1}(m),-\mathbb{g}_{2}(m):m\in{\mathbb{M}}_{0})}
.
There are natural maps
\mathbb{f}_{i}:{\mathbb{M}}_{i}\to{\mathbb{M}}
such that the following diagram is commutative:

It may be nice to note that the common notation for this is
{\mathbb{M}}_{1}+_{{\mathbb{M}}_{0}}{\mathbb{M}}_{2}
.

Definition 2.18
.

Let
{\mathbb{M}}
be an
{\bf R}
-module and
{\bf S}

be any subring of
{\rm End}_{\bf R}({\mathbb{M}})
. We say that
{\bf S}
is algebraically
closed in
{\rm End}_{\bf R}({\mathbb{M}})
if every finite system of equations in several variables over
{\bf S}

which has a solution in
{\rm End}_{\bf R}({\mathbb{M}})
, also
has a solution in
{\bf S}

Let us recall the definition of pure semisimple rings.

Definition 2.19
.

A ring
{\bf R}
is left pure semisimple if every left
{\bf R}
-module is pure-injective.

The next theorem gives several equivalent formulations for the above notion.

Theorem 2.20
.

(See
[
33
, Theorem 4.5.7]
)
The following conditions on a ring
{\bf R}
are equivalent:

(a)

{\bf R}
is left pure semisimple;

(b)

every left
{\bf R}
-module is a direct sum of indecomposable modules;

(c)

there is a cardinal number
\kappa
such that every left
{\bf R}
-module is a direct sum
of modules, each of which is of cardinality less than
\kappa
;

(d)

there is a cardinal number
\kappa
such that every left
{\bf R}
-module is a pure
submodule of a direct sum of modules each of cardinality less than
\kappa
.

The following result of Shelah gives a model theoretic criteria for rings which are not pure semisimple,
and plays an important role in this paper.

Theorem 2.21
.

(Shelah,
[
36
, 8.7]
)

Suppose
{\bf R}
is not left pure semisimple. Then for some bimodule
{\mathbb{M}}
and a sequence
\bar{\varphi}=\langle\varphi_{n}(x):n<\omega\rangle
, each
\varphi_{n}
is
a conjunctive positive existential formula and the sequence
\langle\varphi_{n}({\mathbb{M}}):n<\omega\rangle

is strictly decreasing.

3.
Developing the framework of the construction

In this section we develop some part of the theory that we need for our construction. The main result of this section is
Theorem
3.44
, which gives, in
ZFC
, a semi-nice construction, that plays an essential role in the next sections.

Definition 3.1
.

(1)

Let
{\mathfrak{E}}_{({\bf R},{\bf S})}={\mathfrak{E}}{({\bf R},{\bf S})}

be the class of all

{\mathfrak{e}}=\langle{\mathbb{N}}_{n},x_{n},g_{n}:n<\omega\rangle

such that:

(a)

{\mathbb{N}}_{n}
is an
({\bf R},{\bf S})
-bimodule,

(b)

x_{n}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{n}
,

(c)

g_{n}
is a bimodule homomorphism from
{\mathbb{N}}_{n}
to

{\mathbb{N}}_{n+1}
mapping
x_{n}
to
x_{n+1}
.

Given
{\mathfrak{e}}\in{\mathfrak{E}}_{({\bf R},{\bf S})}
we denote it by
{\mathfrak{e}}=\langle{\mathbb{N}}_{n}^{{\mathfrak{e}}},x_{n}^{{\mathfrak{e}}},g_{n}^{{\mathfrak{e}}}:n<\omega\rangle
.

(2)

We call
{\mathfrak{e}}\in{\mathfrak{E}}_{({\bf R},{\bf S})}

non-trivial
if for every
n
there is no homomorphism from

{\mathbb{N}}^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n+1}
to
{\mathbb{N}}^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n}
as
{\bf R}
-modules
mapping
x^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n+1}
to
x^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n}
.

(3)

Let
{\mathfrak{e}}=\langle{\mathbb{N}}_{n},x_{n},g_{n}:n<\omega\rangle\in{\mathfrak{E}}_{({\bf R},{\bf S})}
. For each
n\leq m
, we define:

g_{n,m}:=g_{n}\circ g_{n+1}\circ\ldots\circ g_{m-1}.

We set
g_{n,n}:={\rm id}_{{\mathbb{N}}_{n}}
and note that

n_{0}\leq n_{1}\leq n_{2}
implies
g_{n_{1},n_{2}}\circ g_{n_{0},n_{1}}=g_{n_{0},n_{2}}
.
We denote
g_{n,m}
by
g^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n,m}.

(4)

For
{\mathfrak{e}}\in{\mathfrak{E}}_{({\bf R},{\bf S})}
and an infinite

{\mathcal{U}}\subseteq\omega
, let
{\mathfrak{e}}^{\prime}=:{\mathfrak{e}}\restriction{\mathcal{U}}
,
the restriction of
{\mathfrak{e}}
to
{\mathcal{U}},
be defined by

{\mathbb{N}}^{{\mathfrak{e}}^{\prime}}_{\ell}=:{\mathbb{N}}^{\mathfrak{e}}_{m(\ell)},\quad x^{{\mathfrak{e}}^{\prime}}_{\ell}=:x^{\mathfrak{e}}_{m(\ell)},\quad g^{{\mathfrak{e}}^{\prime}}_{\ell}=g^{\mathfrak{e}}_{m(\ell),m(\ell+1)},

where
m(\ell)
is the
\ell
–th member of
\mathcal{U}
. Clearly
{\mathfrak{e}}^{\prime}
is in

{\mathfrak{E}}_{({\bf R},{\bf S})}
.

Let us define a special sub-class of
{\mathfrak{E}}_{({\bf R},{\bf S})}
.

Definition 3.2
.

Let
{{\mathfrak{E}}}_{\mu,\kappa}
be the class
of
\mathfrak{e}\in{\mathfrak{E}}_{({\bf R},{\bf S})}
such
that for each
n<\omega
,
{\mathbb{N}}^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n}
, as a bimodule, is generated
by

<\kappa

elements freely except satisfying
<\mu
equations.
This means, there are
(x_{i})_{i<\alpha<\kappa}
and atomic formulas
t_{j}=0,
for
j<\beta<\mu
, such that

{\mathbb{N}}^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n}=(\bigoplus_{i<\alpha}{\bf R}x_{i}{\bf S})/{\mathbb{K}}
, where
{\mathbb{K}}
is the bimodule generated by
\langle t_{j}:j<\beta\rangle
.

We now define what it means for a sequence of formulas to be adequate with respect to an element of
{\mathfrak{E}}_{({\bf R},{\bf S})}
:

Definition 3.3
.

Let
{\mathfrak{e}}:=\langle{\mathbb{N}}_{n},x_{n},g_{n}:n<\omega\rangle
and
\bar{\varphi}:=\langle\varphi_{n}:n<\omega\rangle
.

(1)

The sequence
\bar{\varphi}
is called
(\mu,\kappa)
-adequate with respect to
{\mathfrak{e}}

if the following conditions satisfied:

(\alpha)

\varphi_{n}=\varphi_{n}(x)
is a formula from
\mathcal{L}^{\rm cpe}_{\mu,\kappa}(\tau_{{\bf R}})
,

(\beta)

\varphi_{n+1}(x)\vdash\varphi_{n}(x)
(for the class of

{\bf R}
-modules)
3
3

3

we can restrict ourselves
to those we actually use, i.e.
the ones from
\mathop{\mathrm{cl}}({\mathcal{K}})
.
,

(\gamma)

{\mathbb{N}}_{n}\models\varphi_{n}(x_{n})\ \&\ \neg\varphi_{n+1}(x_{n})
.

Also, we say
{\mathfrak{e}}
is
(\mu,\kappa)
-adequate with respect to
{\mathfrak{e}}
.

(2)

We say
\mathfrak{e}
is explicitly
(\mu,\kappa
)-adequate with respect to
{\mathfrak{e}}
if

\mathfrak{e}
is
(\mu,\kappa)
-adequate with respect to
{\mathfrak{e}}
and

\mathfrak{e}\in{\mathfrak{E}}_{\mu,\kappa}
.

(3)

For simplicity,
\kappa
-adequate means
(\infty,\kappa)
-adequate with respect to
{\mathfrak{e}}
. Also, adequate is referred to

\aleph_{0}
-adequate with respect to
{\mathfrak{e}}
.

(4)

We say that
\bar{\varphi}
is
(\mu,\kappa)
-adequate
if
\bar{\varphi}
is
(\mu,\kappa)
-adequate with respect to some
{\mathfrak{e}}\in{\mathfrak{E}}_{({\bf R},{\bf S})}
.

(5)

We say
\bar{\varphi}
is adequate
with respect to
{\mathfrak{E}}\subseteq{\mathfrak{E}}_{({\bf R},{\bf S})}
if it is adequate with respect to some

{\mathfrak{e}}\in{\mathfrak{E}}
.

It follows from Lemma
2.9
that any
(\mu,\kappa)
-adequate
{\mathfrak{e}}\in{\mathfrak{E}}_{({\bf R},{\bf S})}
is non-trivial.

Definition 3.4
.

Let
{\mathfrak{e}}\in{{\mathfrak{E}}}
,
n<\omega
and
\kappa
be given. We define

(1)

\varphi^{{\mathfrak{e}},\kappa}_{n}:=\bigwedge\left\{\varphi(x):\varphi\in\mathcal{L}^{{\rm cpe}}_{\infty,\kappa}(\tau_{{\bf R}})~{\rm and~}{\mathbb{N}}^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n}\models\varphi(x^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n})\right\}
4
4

4

This is not
a formula being a conjunction on a class, not a set, but
when we deal with an
{\bf R}
-module
{\mathbb{M}}

it is enough to
restrict ourselves to
\mathcal{L}^{{\rm cpe}}_{\lambda,\kappa}
for

\lambda=(||{\mathbb{M}}||^{<\kappa})^{+}
.
,

(2)

\psi^{{\mathfrak{e}},\kappa}_{n}:=\bigwedge\left\{\psi(x):\psi\in\mathcal{L}^{{\rm cpe}}_{\infty,\kappa}(\tau_{({\bf R},{\bf S})})~{\rm and~}{\mathbb{N}}^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n}\models\psi(x^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n})\right\}
,

(3)

\bar{\varphi}^{{\mathfrak{e}},\kappa}:=\langle\varphi^{{\mathfrak{e}},\kappa}_{n}:n<\omega\rangle
,

(4)

\bar{\psi}^{{\mathfrak{e}},\kappa}:=\langle\psi^{{\mathfrak{e}},\kappa}_{n}:n<\omega\rangle,

(5)

\varphi^{{\mathfrak{e}},\kappa}_{\omega}(x):=\bigwedge\limits_{n<\omega}\varphi^{{\mathfrak{e}},\kappa}_{n}(x)
,

(6)

\psi^{{\mathfrak{e}},\kappa}_{\omega}(x):=\bigwedge\limits_{n<\omega}\psi^{{\mathfrak{e}},\kappa}_{n}(x)
.

Remark 3.5
.

In Definition
3.4
, we omit the index
\kappa
, when it is
\kappa(\mathfrak{e})
for
\psi
or
\kappa_{{\bf R}}(\mathfrak{e})
for
\varphi
, where
\kappa(\mathfrak{e})
and
\kappa_{{\bf R}}(\mathfrak{e})
are defined in Definition
3.7
, see below.

The next lemma shows the relation between different
\varphi^{{\mathfrak{e}},\kappa}_{n}
’s and
\psi^{{\mathfrak{e}},\kappa}_{n}
’s.

Lemma 3.6
.

For
n\geq m
,
\varphi^{{\mathfrak{e}},\kappa}_{n}(x)\vdash\varphi^{{\mathfrak{e}},\kappa}_{m}(x)
,
\psi^{{\mathfrak{e}},\kappa}_{n}(x)\vdash\psi^{{\mathfrak{e}},\kappa}_{m}(x)
and
\psi^{{\mathfrak{e}},\kappa}_{n}(x)\vdash\varphi^{{\mathfrak{e}},\kappa}_{n}(x)
.

Proof.

The lemma follows easily by applying Lemma
2.9
to
g^{\mathfrak{e}}_{m,n}
.
∎

Definition 3.7
.

(1)

For
{\mathfrak{e}}\in{\mathfrak{E}}_{{\bf R},{\bf S}}
we define:

(a)

\kappa({\mathfrak{e}})
as the first infinite cardinal
\kappa
such that for each

n<\omega
, the bimodule
{\mathbb{N}}^{{\mathfrak{e}}}_{n}
is generated by a
set of
<\kappa
elements.

(b)

\kappa_{{\bf R}}({\mathfrak{e}})
be the first infinite cardinal
\kappa
such that for each

n<\omega
, the bimodule
{\mathbb{N}}^{{\mathfrak{e}}}_{n}

as an
{\bf R}
-module is generated by a
set of
<\kappa
elements.

(2)

For each
{\mathfrak{E}}\subseteq{\mathfrak{E}}_{({\bf R},{\bf S})}
, we let

(a)

\kappa({\mathfrak{E}}):=\mathop{\mathrm{sup}}\{\kappa({\mathfrak{e}}):{\mathfrak{e}}\in{\mathfrak{E}}\}

and

(b)

\kappa({\mathfrak{E}})_{{\bf R}}:=\mathop{\mathrm{sup}}\{\kappa_{{\bf R}}({\mathfrak{e}}):{\mathfrak{e}}\in{\mathfrak{E}}\}
.

We frequently use the following lemma.

Lemma 3.8
.

(1)

Assume
{\mathbb{N}}
is a bimodule (resp.
{\bf R}
-module) generated by
<\kappa

members freely except satisfying
<\mu
equations and
x\in{\mathbb{N}}
.
Then there is a simple formula
\varphi\in\mathcal{L}_{\mu,\kappa}(\tau_{({\bf R},{\bf S})})
(resp.
\varphi\in\mathcal{L}_{\mu,\kappa}(\tau_{{\bf R}})
)
such that for any bimodule
(resp.
{\bf R}
-module)
{\mathbb{M}}
and
y\in{\mathbb{M}}
, the following are
equivalent:

(a)

{\mathbb{M}}\models\varphi(y)
,

(b)

for some homomorphism
{\bf f}
from
{\mathbb{N}}

into
{\mathbb{M}}
,
{\bf f}(x)=y

(if
{\mathbb{N}}
is an
{\bf R}
-module this means
{\bf f}
is an

{\bf R}
-homomorphism).

(c)

for some
{\mathbb{N}}^{\prime}
extending
{\mathbb{N}}
and a homomorphism

{\bf f}
from

{\mathbb{N}}^{\prime}
into
{\mathbb{M}}
we have
{\bf f}(x)=y
.

(2)

If
{\mathbb{N}}={\mathbb{N}}^{\prime}\oplus{\mathbb{N}}^{\prime\prime}

and
x\in N^{\prime}
,
then it suffices that

{\mathbb{N}}^{\prime}
is generated by
<\kappa
members freely except satisfying

<\mu
equations.

(3)

Clause (1) applies if
{\mathbb{N}}={\mathbb{N}}^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n}
,
x=x_{n}^{\mathfrak{e}}
and

\varphi=\psi^{{\mathfrak{e}},\kappa}_{n}
, when
{\mathbb{N}}^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n}
is generated
by

<\kappa
members freely except satisfying
<\mu
equations (as a bimodule). Similarly for

\varphi=\varphi^{{\mathfrak{e}},\kappa}_{n}
for
{\bf R}
-modules.

Proof.

(1). We prove the lemma for the case of bimodules, exactly the same proof works for
{\bf R}
-modules. Suppose
{\mathbb{N}}
is a bimodule generated by
<\kappa

members freely except satisfying
<\mu
equations and
x\in{\mathbb{N}}
. So it has the form
{\mathbb{N}}=\bigoplus_{i<\alpha}{\bf R}x_{i}{\bf S}/{\mathbb{K}}
,
where
{\mathbb{K}}
is the bimodule generated by
\langle t_{j}:j<\beta\rangle
, where each
t_{j}=0
is an atomic formula,
\alpha<\kappa
and
\beta<\mu
. Then

•

x=\sum_{i<d}\sum_{h<h_{d}}r_{i,h}x_{i}s_{i,h}
, where
d,h_{d}<\omega,

r_{i,h}\in{\bf R}
and

s_{i,h}\in{\bf S}
. We should remark that for all but finitely many of them we have
r_{i,h}x_{i}s_{i,h}=0.

•

t_{j}=\sum_{i<d}\sum_{h<h_{d}}r^{j}_{i,h}x_{i}s^{j}_{i,h},
where
r^{j}_{i,h}\in{\bf R}
and
s^{j}_{i,h}\in{\bf S}
. Again, for all but finitely many of them we have
r^{j}_{i,h}x_{i}s^{j}_{i,h}=0.

Now consider the formula

\varphi(v)=\exists_{i<\alpha}v_{i}[\bigwedge_{j<\beta}\sum_{i<\alpha}\sum_{h<h_{d}}r^{j}_{i,h}v_{i}s^{j}_{i,h}=0\wedge v=\sum_{i<\alpha}\sum_{h<h_{d}}r_{i,h}v_{i}s_{i,h}].

It is clearly a simple formula in
\mathcal{L}_{\mu,\kappa}(\tau_{({\bf R},{\bf S})})
. Now suppose
{\mathbb{M}}\models\varphi(y)
. Thus we can find

y_{i},
for
i<\alpha
, such that in
M
,

\sum_{i<\alpha}\sum_{h<h_{d}}r^{j}_{i,h}y_{i}s^{j}_{i,h}=0,

and

y=\sum_{i<\alpha}\sum_{h<h_{d}}r_{i,h}y_{i}s_{i,h}.

Define
{\bf f}:{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{M}}
so that for each
i<\alpha,f(x_{i})=y_{i}.
Then
f
is as required.

(2). This is clear and (3) follows from the fact that
\psi^{{\mathfrak{e}},\kappa}_{n}\vdash\varphi
(because
{\mathbb{N}}^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n}\models\varphi(x_{n}^{\mathfrak{e}})
).
∎

The next lemma shows that each non-trivial
{\mathfrak{e}}\in{{\mathfrak{E}}}_{\mu,\kappa}
has a canonical adequate
sequence.

Lemma 3.9
.

If
{\mathfrak{e}}\in{{\mathfrak{E}}}_{\mu,\kappa}
is non-trivial and

\mu\geq\kappa\geq\aleph_{0}

then
\bar{\varphi}^{{\mathfrak{e}},\kappa}
is
(\mu,\kappa)
-adequate with respect to
\mathfrak{e}
.

Proof.

It is easily seen, by the structure of each
{\mathbb{N}}_{n}^{\mathfrak{e}}
that
\varphi^{\mathfrak{e},\kappa}_{n}
is equivalent to
a formula of
\mathcal{L}^{{\rm cpe}}_{\infty,\kappa}(\tau_{{\bf R}})
. According to Lemma
3.6

\varphi^{\mathfrak{e},\kappa}_{n+1}\vdash\varphi^{\mathfrak{e},\kappa}_{n}.

By the definition of
\varphi^{\mathfrak{e},\kappa}_{n}
, we have
{\mathbb{N}}_{n}^{\mathfrak{e}}\models\varphi^{\mathfrak{e},\kappa}_{n}(x_{n}^{\mathfrak{e}})
. It remains to show that

{\mathbb{N}}_{n}^{\mathfrak{e}}\models\neg\varphi^{\mathfrak{e},\kappa}_{n+1}(x_{n}^{\mathfrak{e}})
. Suppose not. It follows from Lemma

3.8
(3) that there is a bimodule homomorphism
{\bf h}:{\mathbb{N}}_{n+1}^{\mathfrak{e}}\to{\mathbb{N}}_{n}^{\mathfrak{e}}

with
{\bf h}(x_{n+1}^{\mathfrak{e}})=x_{n}^{\mathfrak{e}},
contradicting the non-triviality of

\mathfrak{e}
.
∎

Definition 3.10
.

A
\lambda
-
context
is a tuple

{\mathfrak{m}}:=({\mathcal{K}},{\mathbb{M}}_{\ast},{\mathfrak{E}},{\bf R},{\bf S},{\bf T})=({\mathcal{K}}^{\mathfrak{m}},{\mathbb{M}}_{\ast}^{\mathfrak{m}},{\mathfrak{E}}^{\mathfrak{m}},{\bf R}^{\mathfrak{m}},{\bf S}^{\mathfrak{m}},{\bf T}^{\mathfrak{m}})

where

(1)

{\bf R},{\bf S}
and
{\bf T}
are as usual.

(2)

{\mathcal{K}}
is a set
of
({\bf R},{\bf S})
-bimodules.

(3)

{{\mathfrak{E}}}
is a subset of

{{\mathfrak{E}}}_{\infty,\lambda}

closed under restrictions
(i.e., if
{\mathfrak{e}}\in{{\mathfrak{E}}}

and
{\mathcal{U}}\subseteq\omega
is
infinite then

{\mathfrak{e}}\restriction{\mathcal{U}}\in{{\mathfrak{E}}}
).

(4)

If
{\mathfrak{e}}\in{{\mathfrak{E}}}
, then

\ {\mathbb{N}}^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n}\in{\mathcal{K}}

for each
n<\omega
.

(5)

{\mathbb{M}}_{\ast}

is a bimodule.
If
{\mathbb{M}}_{\ast}

is omitted we mean the zero bimodule.

Let
\chi({\mathcal{K}})
be
the minimal cardinal
\chi\geq\aleph_{0}
such that
every
{\mathbb{N}}\in{\mathcal{K}}
is generated, as a bimodule, by a set of
<\chi

members and set

||{\mathfrak{m}}||=\sum\{\|{\mathbb{N}}\|:{\mathbb{N}}\in{\mathcal{K}}\}+\|{\mathfrak{E}}\|+||{\mathbb{M}}_{\ast}||||{\bf R}||+||{\bf S}||+\aleph_{0}.

Remark 3.11
.

We omit
\lambda
from the
\lambda
-context, if it is clear from the context and then we mean for the minimal such
\lambda
.

Convention 3.12
.

From now on suppose that

{{\mathfrak{m}}}=({\mathcal{K}},{\mathbb{M}}_{\ast},{{\mathfrak{E}}},{\bf R},{\bf S},{\bf T})

is a
context.

Definition 3.13
.

(1)

Given a context
{\mathfrak{m}}=({\mathcal{K}},{\mathbb{M}}_{\ast},{\mathfrak{E}},{\bf R},{\bf S},{\bf T})
, we set:

•

c\ell_{\rm is}({\mathcal{K}})
be the
class of bimodules isomorphic to
some
{\mathbb{K}}\in{\mathcal{K}}
.

•

c\ell^{\kappa}_{\rm is}({\mathcal{K}})
be the
class of bimodules of the form

{\mathbb{M}}=\bigoplus\limits_{i<j}{\mathbb{M}}_{i}
, where

j<\kappa
, and for
i<j,{\mathbb{M}}_{i}\in c\ell_{\rm is}({\mathcal{K}})
.

•

c\ell({\mathcal{K}})=c\ell_{\rm ds}({\mathcal{K}})
be the class of
bimodules isomorphic to a direct sum of bimodules from

c\ell_{\rm is}({\mathcal{K}})

(so
c\ell_{\rm is}(c\ell({\mathcal{K}}))=c\ell({\mathcal{K}})
).

•

\kappa({\mathfrak{m}})=\kappa({\mathfrak{E}}^{\mathfrak{m}})
.

•

\kappa_{\bf R}({\mathfrak{m}})=\kappa_{\bf R}({\mathfrak{E}}^{\mathfrak{m}})
.

(2)

Following Definition
3.3
, let us say that

\bar{\varphi}

is adequate
for a context
{\mathfrak{m}}
if it is adequate with respect to
{\mathfrak{E}}^{\mathfrak{m}}
.

Definition 3.14
.

By a
{\mathcal{K}}
-bimodule we mean a bimodule from
c\ell_{\rm is}({\mathcal{K}})
. Also, we say
{\mathbb{M}}_{1}
is a
{\mathcal{K}}
-direct summand of

{\mathbb{M}}_{2}
if

{\mathbb{M}}_{2}={\mathbb{M}}_{1}\oplus{\mathbb{K}}

for some

{\mathbb{K}}\in c\ell_{\rm ds}({\mathcal{K}})
.

Definition 3.15
.

We say
{\mathbb{M}}_{1}
is an almost direct
{\mathcal{K}}
-summand of

{\mathbb{M}}_{2}
with respect to
\kappa

denoted
{\mathbb{M}}_{1}\leq^{\rm ads}_{{\mathcal{K}},\kappa}{\mathbb{M}}_{2}
,
if player II has a winning strategy in the following game
\Game_{{\mathcal{K}},\kappa}^{{\mathbb{M}}_{1},{\mathbb{M}}_{2}}
of length
\omega
:
in the
n^{\rm th}
move player I chooses a subset
A_{n}\subseteq{\mathbb{M}}_{2}
of
cardinality
<\kappa
, and then player II chooses a sub-bimodule
{\mathbb{K}}_{n}\subseteq{\mathbb{M}}_{2}.

Player II wins iff:

(a)

A_{n}\subseteq{\mathbb{M}}_{1}+\sum\limits_{\ell\leq n}{\mathbb{K}}_{\ell}
,

(b)

{\mathbb{K}}_{n}
is in
c\ell^{\kappa}_{\rm is}({\mathcal{K}})
,

(c)

{\mathbb{M}}_{1}+\sum\limits_{\ell<\omega}{\mathbb{K}}_{\ell}={\mathbb{M}}_{1}\oplus\bigoplus\limits_{\ell<\omega}{\mathbb{K}}_{\ell}
.

We usually write
\leq_{\kappa}
or
\leq^{\rm ads}_{\kappa}

instead of
\leq^{\rm ads}_{{\mathcal{K}},\kappa}
if

{\mathcal{K}}
is clear. We also
may write
\leq_{{{\mathfrak{m}}},\kappa}

or
\leq^{\rm ads}_{{{\mathfrak{m}}},\kappa}
, when
\mathcal{K}=\mathcal{K}^{{\mathfrak{m}}}
.

We now define another order between bimodules and then connect it to the above defined notion.

Definition 3.16
.

Let
{\mathbb{M}}_{1}
and
{\mathbb{M}}_{2}
be two bimodules.

(1)

{\mathbb{M}}_{1}\leq^{\rm pr}_{\varphi}{\mathbb{M}}_{2}
means that

{\mathbb{M}}_{1}\subseteq{\mathbb{M}}_{2}
and
if
\psi=\psi(\bar{x})
is a sub-formula of
\varphi
and

\bar{z}\in{}^{\mathop{\rm\ell g}(\bar{x})}{\mathbb{M}}_{1}
then

{\mathbb{M}}_{1}\models\psi(\bar{z})\iff{\mathbb{M}}_{2}\models\psi(\bar{z}).

(2)

By
{\mathbb{M}}_{1}\leq^{\rm qr}_{\varphi}{\mathbb{M}}_{2}
we mean that

{\mathbb{M}}_{1}\subseteq{\mathbb{M}}_{2}

and if
\psi=\psi(\bar{x})
is a sub-formula of

\varphi
and
\bar{z}\in{}^{(\mathop{\rm\ell g}\bar{x})}{\mathbb{M}}_{1}
, then

{\mathbb{M}}_{2}\models\varphi(\bar{z})\Rightarrow{\mathbb{M}}_{1}\models\psi(\bar{z}).

(3)

Assume
\mathcal{F}
is a set of formulas. The notation
{\mathbb{M}}_{1}\leq^{\rm pr}_{\mathcal{F}}{\mathbb{M}}_{2}
(resp.
{\mathbb{M}}_{1}\leq^{\rm qr}_{\mathcal{F}}{\mathbb{M}}_{2}
) means

{\mathbb{M}}_{1}\leq^{\rm pr}_{\varphi}{\mathbb{M}}_{2}
(resp.
{\mathbb{M}}_{1}\leq^{\rm qr}_{\varphi}{\mathbb{M}}_{2}
) for all
\varphi\in\mathcal{F}.
In the special case

\bar{\varphi}:=\langle\varphi_{n}:n<\omega\rangle
, the property

{\mathbb{M}}_{1}\leq^{\rm pr}_{\bar{\varphi}}{\mathbb{M}}_{2}
holds iff

{\mathbb{M}}_{1}\leq^{\rm pr}_{\varphi_{n}}{\mathbb{M}}_{2}
for each
n
.

For instance, let
\varphi
be the following simple formula

\varphi=\varphi(x)=(\exists y_{0},\ldots,y_{i}\ldots)_{i<\alpha}\bigwedge\limits_{j<\beta}[a_{j}x=\sum\limits_{i<\alpha}b_{j,i}y_{i}]

where
a_{j},b_{j,i}\in{\bf R}
. Then

{\mathbb{M}}_{1}\leq^{\rm pr}_{\varphi}{\mathbb{M}}_{2}
means that
if for some
x\in{\mathbb{M}}_{1}
and
y_{i}\in{\mathbb{M}}_{2}

we have

a_{j}x-\sum_{i<\alpha}b_{j,i}y_{i}=0\quad\forall j<\beta

then there are
y^{\prime}_{i}\in{\mathbb{M}}_{1}

such that

a_{j}x-\sum\limits_{i<\alpha}b_{j,i}y^{\prime}_{i}=0\quad\forall j<\beta.

Remark 3.17
.

Note that if
\varphi
is existential (e.g. a simple formula), then

\leq^{\rm qr}_{\varphi}

is equal to
\leq^{{\mathord{\mathrm{pr}}}}_{\varphi}
.

The next lemma connects the above defined relations
to each other.

Lemma 3.18
.

Let
{\mathbb{M}}_{1}
and
{\mathbb{M}}_{2}
be two bimodules such that
{\mathbb{M}}_{1}
is
{\mathcal{K}}
-nice. The following assertions are true:

(1)

{\mathbb{M}}_{1}\leq_{\kappa}{\mathbb{M}}_{2}
iff

{\mathbb{M}}_{1}\leq^{\rm pr}_{\mathcal{L}_{\infty,\kappa}(\tau_{({\bf R},{\bf S})})}{\mathbb{M}}_{2}
.

(2)

If
{\mathbb{M}}_{1}\leq_{\kappa}{\mathbb{M}}_{2}
and
\varphi(x)\in\mathcal{L}^{\rm cpe}_{\infty,\kappa}(\tau_{({\bf R},{\bf S})})
,
then
\varphi({\mathbb{M}}_{1})={\mathbb{M}}_{1}\cap\varphi({\mathbb{M}}_{2})
.

Proof.

Clause (2) follows from (1) and the definition of
\leq^{\rm pr}
, so let us prove (1). Let
{\mathbb{M}}_{0}

witness
{\mathbb{M}}_{1}
is
\mathcal{K}
-nice.

Suppose
{\mathbb{M}}_{1}\leq_{\kappa}{\mathbb{M}}_{2}
. We prove, by induction on the complexity of the formula
\varphi\in\mathcal{L}_{\infty,\kappa}(\tau_{({\bf R},{\bf S})}),
that
{\mathbb{M}}_{1}\leq^{\rm pr}_{\varphi}{\mathbb{M}}_{2}
.

The only non-trivial case where the assumption
{\mathbb{M}}_{1}\leq_{\kappa}{\mathbb{M}}_{2}
is used is the case of existential quantifier
5
5

5

The case of universal quantifier then follows easily as
\forall\equiv\neg\exists\neg.
, so let us suppose
\varphi(v_{i})_{i<\alpha}
is of the form

\exists_{j<\beta}w_{j}[\psi((w_{j})_{j<\beta},(v_{i})_{i<\alpha})],

the claim holds for
\psi=\psi((w_{j})_{j<\beta},(v_{i})_{i<\alpha})
and suppose
(x_{i})_{i<\alpha}\in{\mathbb{M}}_{1}
. We want to show that

{\mathbb{M}}_{1}\models\varphi((x_{i})_{i<\alpha})\iff{\mathbb{M}}_{2}\models\varphi((x_{i})_{i<\alpha}).

As
{\mathbb{M}}_{1}
is a submodule of
{\mathbb{M}}_{2},
by the induction hypothesis, if
{\mathbb{M}}_{1}\models\varphi((x_{i})_{i<\alpha})
, then
{\mathbb{M}}_{2}\models\varphi((x_{i})_{i<\alpha}).

Conversely suppose that
{\mathbb{M}}_{2}\models\varphi((x_{i})_{i<\alpha}).
Then we can find
(y_{j})_{j<\beta}\in{\mathbb{M}}_{2}
such that

{\mathbb{M}}_{2}\models\psi((y_{j})_{j<\beta},(x_{i})_{i<\alpha}).

Now consider the game
\Game_{\mathcal{K},\kappa}^{{\mathbb{M}}_{1},{\mathbb{M}}_{2}}
,
in which player I plays
A_{n}=\{y_{j}:j<\beta\}
at each step
n
. At the end, we have bimodules
\{{\mathbb{K}}_{n}:n<\omega\}

such that:

•

A_{n}\subseteq{\mathbb{M}}_{1}+\sum\limits_{\ell\leq n}{\mathbb{K}}_{\ell}
,

•

{\mathbb{K}}_{n}
is in
c\ell^{\kappa}_{\rm is}({\mathcal{K}})
,

•

{\mathbb{M}}_{1}+\sum\limits_{\ell<\omega}{\mathbb{K}}_{\ell}={\mathbb{M}}_{1}\oplus\bigoplus\limits_{\ell<\omega}{\mathbb{K}}_{\ell}
.

Set
{\mathbb{K}}=\bigoplus\limits_{\ell<\omega}{\mathbb{K}}_{\ell}.
It then follows that
{\mathbb{M}}_{1}\oplus{\mathbb{K}}\models\varphi((x_{i})_{i<\alpha})
. In view of Lemma
2.13
,

{\mathbb{M}}_{1}\models\varphi((x_{i})_{i<\alpha}).

Conversely, suppose that
{\mathbb{M}}_{1}\leq^{\rm pr}_{\mathcal{L}_{\infty,\kappa}(\tau_{({\bf R},{\bf S})})}{\mathbb{M}}_{2}
. Let us first state a few
simple facts:

(i)

Suppose
\alpha<\kappa

and
\bar{b}\in
{}^{\alpha}{\mathbb{M}}_{2}
. Then there exists

\bar{a}\in
{}^{\alpha}{\mathbb{M}}_{1}
such that

({\mathbb{M}}_{1},\bar{a})\equiv_{\mathcal{L}_{\infty,\kappa}(\tau_{({\bf R},{\bf S})})}({\mathbb{M}}_{2},\bar{b}).

To see this, let
\alpha<\kappa
and

\bar{b}\in
{}^{\alpha}{\mathbb{M}}_{2}
. Suppose by contradiction that there is no
\bar{a}\in{}^{\alpha}{\mathbb{M}}_{1}
such that
({\mathbb{M}}_{1},\bar{a})\equiv_{\mathcal{L}_{\infty,\kappa}(\tau_{({\bf R},{\bf S})})}({\mathbb{M}}_{2},\bar{b}).
Thus for each
\bar{a}\in{}^{\alpha}{\mathbb{M}}_{1}
we can find

\phi_{\bar{a}}(\bar{\nu})\in\mathcal{L}_{\infty,\kappa}(\tau_{({\bf R},{\bf S})})
such that

{\mathbb{M}}_{1}\models\neg\phi_{\bar{a}}(\bar{a})~~\&~~{\mathbb{M}}_{2}\models\phi_{\bar{a}}(\bar{b}).

Let
\phi(\bar{\nu})=\bigwedge\{\phi_{\bar{a}}(\bar{\nu}):\bar{a}\in{}^{\alpha}{\mathbb{M}}_{1}\}\in\mathcal{L}_{\infty,\kappa}(\tau_{({\bf R},{\bf S})}).
Then
{\mathbb{M}}_{1}\models~\forall\bar{\nu}\neg\phi(\bar{\nu})
,
while
{\mathbb{M}}_{2}\models\phi(\bar{b})
, a contradiction to our assumption.

(ii)

Suppose
\bar{a}_{1}\in{}^{\mathop{\rm\ell g}(\bar{a}_{1})}{\mathbb{M}}_{1},\bar{a}_{2}\in{}^{\mathop{\rm\ell g}(\bar{a}_{2})}{\mathbb{M}}_{1}
with
\bar{a}_{1}\unlhd\bar{a}_{2}
and
\bar{b}_{1}\in{}^{\mathop{\rm\ell g}(\bar{a}_{1})}{\mathbb{M}}_{2}
are such that
({\mathbb{M}}_{1},\bar{a}_{1})\equiv_{\mathcal{L}_{\infty,\kappa}(\tau_{({\bf R},{\bf S})})}({\mathbb{M}}_{2},\bar{b}_{1}).

There there is some
\bar{b}_{2}\in{}^{\mathop{\rm\ell g}(\bar{a}_{2})}{\mathbb{M}}_{2}
such that
\bar{b}_{1}\unlhd\bar{b}_{2}
and
({\mathbb{M}}_{1},\bar{a}_{2})\equiv_{\mathcal{L}_{\infty,\kappa}(\tau_{({\bf R},{\bf S})})}({\mathbb{M}}_{2},\bar{b}_{2}).

(iii)

Suppose
\alpha<\kappa,\bar{a}\in{}^{\alpha}{\mathbb{M}}_{1}
,
\bar{b}\in{}^{\alpha}{\mathbb{M}}_{2}
and

({\mathbb{M}}_{1},\bar{a})\equiv_{\mathcal{L}_{\infty,\kappa}(\tau_{({\bf R},{\bf S})})}({\mathbb{M}}_{2},\bar{b})
. Let
\bar{c}=\langle b_{i}-a_{i}:i<\alpha\rangle
and let
{\mathbb{K}}
be the submodule of
{\mathbb{M}}_{2}
generated by
\bar{c}
. Then
\bar{b}\in{\mathbb{M}}_{1}+{\mathbb{K}}
and

{\mathbb{M}}_{1}+{\mathbb{K}}={\mathbb{M}}_{1}\oplus{\mathbb{K}}.

(iv)

Suppose
\langle K_{\ell}:\ell<\omega\rangle
is an increasing sequence of submodules of
{\mathbb{M}}_{2}
such that for
each
\ell<\omega,{\mathbb{M}}_{1}+{\mathbb{K}}_{\ell}={\mathbb{M}}_{1}\oplus{\mathbb{K}}_{\ell}
. Then
{\mathbb{M}}_{1}+\sum\limits_{\ell<\omega}{\mathbb{K}}_{\ell}={\mathbb{M}}_{1}\oplus\bigoplus\limits_{\ell<\omega}{\mathbb{K}}_{\ell}
.

We are now ready to define a winning strategy for the game
\Game_{{\mathcal{K}},\kappa}^{{\mathbb{M}}_{1},{\mathbb{M}}_{2}}
. Fix a well-ordering
<^{*}
of
{\mathbb{M}}_{2}
.
To start set
A_{-1}=\emptyset
and
\bar{b}_{-1}=\langle\rangle.
At stage
n
, suppose
player I has chosen
A_{n}\subseteq{\mathbb{M}}_{2}
of size
<\kappa.
We may assume that
A_{n}\supseteq A_{n-1}.
Let
\bar{a}_{n}=\langle a_{n}(i):i<\mathop{\rm\ell g}(\bar{a}_{n})\rangle
enumerate
A_{n}
in the
<^{*}
-order in such a way that
A_{n-1}
is an initial segment of the enumeration.
By (i) and (ii), choose
\bar{b}_{n}=\langle b_{n}(i):i<\mathop{\rm\ell g}(\bar{a}_{n})\rangle\in{\mathbb{M}}_{2}
such that
\bar{b}_{n}\unrhd\bar{b}_{n-1}
and
({\mathbb{M}}_{1},\bar{a}_{n})\equiv_{\mathcal{L}_{\infty,\kappa}(\tau_{({\bf R},{\bf S})})}({\mathbb{M}}_{2},\bar{b}_{n}).
Let
{\mathbb{K}}_{n}
be the submodule of
{\mathbb{M}}_{2}
generated by
\{b_{n}(i)-a_{n}(i):i<\mathop{\rm\ell g}(\bar{a}_{n})\}.
Now (iii) and (iv) guarantee that items (a)
and (c) of Definition
3.15
are satisfied. This completes the proof.
∎

Claim 3.19
.

Let
\varphi
be in

\mathcal{L}^{\rm cpe}_{\infty,\infty}(\tau_{({\bf R},{\bf S})})
. The following holds:

(1)

Assuming
\varphi=\varphi(\bar{x})

we can find a simple formula
\varphi_{\ast}(\bar{z})\in\mathcal{L}_{\infty,\infty}(\tau_{({\bf R},{\bf S})})
satisfying:

(a)

if

{\mathbb{M}}_{1}\leq^{\rm{\mathord{\mathrm{pr}}}}_{\varphi_{\ast}}{\mathbb{M}}_{2}
and
\bar{x}\in{\mathbb{M}}_{2}
then

\langle x_{i}+{\mathbb{M}}_{1}:i<\mathop{\rm\ell g}(\bar{x})\rangle\in\varphi({\mathbb{M}}_{2}/{\mathbb{M}}_{1})

iff
\bar{x}\in\varphi({\mathbb{M}}_{2})+{\mathbb{M}}_{1}
.

(b)

if
\varphi(\bar{x})\in\mathcal{L}^{\rm cpe}_{\mu,\mu}(\tau_{({\bf R},{\bf S})})

and
\mu
is regular,
then

\varphi_{\ast}(\bar{z})\in\mathcal{L}_{\mu,\mu}(\tau_{({\bf R},{\bf S})})
.

(2)

Assume
{\mathbb{M}}_{2}\in c\ell_{\rm ds}({\mathcal{K}})
and
{\mathbb{M}}_{1}
is
{\mathcal{K}}
-nice. If
{\mathbb{M}}_{1}\oplus{\mathbb{M}}_{2}={\mathbb{M}}_{3}
, then

{\mathbb{M}}_{1}\leq^{\rm pr}_{\varphi}{\mathbb{M}}_{3}
.

(3)

If
\langle{\mathbb{M}}_{i}:i<\delta\rangle
is increasing,

{\mathbb{M}}_{i}\leq^{\rm pr}_{\varphi}{\mathbb{M}}
for all
i<\delta
and
\varphi\in\mathcal{L}_{\infty,\mathop{\mathrm{cf}}(\delta)}(\tau_{({\bf R},{\bf S})})
, then
\bigcup\limits_{i<\delta}{\mathbb{M}}_{i}\leq^{\rm pr}_{\varphi}{\mathbb{M}}
and
{\mathbb{M}}_{j}\leq^{\rm pr}_{\varphi}\bigcup\limits_{i<\delta}{\mathbb{M}}_{i}
for all
j<\delta
.

(4)

Assume
\varphi
is a simple formula of

\mathcal{L}_{\kappa,\kappa}(\tau_{({\bf R},{\bf S})})
.
Then

{\mathbb{M}}_{1}<^{\rm pr}_{\varphi}{\mathbb{M}}_{2}
iff for every
<\kappa
-generated submodule

{\mathbb{N}}\subseteq{\mathbb{M}}_{2}
we have

{\mathbb{M}}_{1}\leq^{\rm pr}_{\varphi}{\mathbb{M}}_{1}+{\mathbb{N}}
.

(5)

If
{\mathbb{M}}=\bigoplus\limits_{t\in I}{\mathbb{M}}_{t}
and

\varphi(x)\in\mathcal{L}_{\infty,\infty}^{p}(\tau_{({\bf R},{\bf S})})
, then

\varphi({\mathbb{M}})=\bigoplus\limits_{t\in I}\varphi({\mathbb{M}}_{t})
.

Proof.

(1). For simplicity, suppose that
\varphi=\varphi(x)
. By Lemma
2.11
, we can assume the formula

\varphi
is simple, so
let it be of the form

\varphi=\exists_{i<\alpha}y_{i}\bigwedge_{j<\beta}\varphi_{j}(\bar{y},x)

where each

\varphi_{j}(\bar{y},x)
is an atomic formula.
But the only atomic relation is equality, so by moving to one side,
without loss of generality
\varphi_{j}(\bar{y},x)
is of the form

\sigma_{j}(\bar{y},x)=0

for some term
\sigma_{i}
.
Let
\bar{z}=\langle z_{j}:j<\beta\rangle
and

\varphi_{\ast}(\bar{z})=(\exists x,\bar{y})[\bigwedge_{j<\beta}\sigma_{j}(\bar{y},x)=z_{j}].

We show that
\varphi_{\ast}(\bar{z})
is as required.
Clause (b) clearly holds. In order to prove clause (a), take
{\mathbb{M}}_{1}\leq^{\rm{\mathord{\mathrm{pr}}}}_{\varphi_{\ast}}{\mathbb{M}}_{2}
and
x\in{\mathbb{M}}_{2}
.
First,
assume that
x+{\mathbb{M}}_{1}\in\varphi({\mathbb{M}}_{2}/{\mathbb{M}}_{1})
. Then

{\mathbb{M}}_{2}/{\mathbb{M}}_{1}\models\exists\bar{y}\bigwedge_{j<\beta}\sigma_{j}(\bar{y},x+{\mathbb{M}}_{1})=0.

We can find
\bar{b}=(b_{i})_{i<\alpha}\in{\mathbb{M}}_{2}
such that

{\mathbb{M}}_{2}/{\mathbb{M}}_{1}\models~\sigma_{j}(\bar{b}+{\mathbb{M}}_{1},x+{\mathbb{M}}_{1})=0
for all
j<\beta
.

6
6

6

By
\bar{b}+{\mathbb{M}}_{1}
we mean
\langle b_{i}+{\mathbb{M}}_{1}:i<\alpha\rangle
.

By definition,
\sigma_{j}(\bar{b}+{\mathbb{M}}_{1},x+{\mathbb{M}}_{1})=c_{j}+{\mathbb{M}}_{1}
for some
c_{j}\in M_{1}
.
We set
\bar{c}:=\langle c_{j}:j<\beta\rangle
. Then

{\mathbb{M}}_{2}\models\varphi_{*}(\bar{c})
. Hence,

M_{2}\models\sigma_{j}(\bar{b},x)=c_{j}

for all
j<\beta
. We apply our assumption to see

{\mathbb{M}}_{1}\models\varphi_{*}(\bar{c})
, e.g., there are
\bar{b}^{\prime}=(b^{\prime}_{i})_{i<\alpha}
and
x^{\prime}
in
{\mathbb{M}}_{1}

such that

M_{1}\models\sigma_{j}(\bar{b}^{\prime},x^{\prime})=c_{j}

for all
j<\beta
.
Then
{\mathbb{M}}_{2}
satisfies the same formulas. In particular,

M_{2}\models\sigma_{j}((b_{i}-b^{\prime}_{i})_{i<\alpha},x-x^{\prime})=0,

which implies
M_{2}\models\varphi(x-x^{\prime}).
Thus
x\in\varphi({\mathbb{M}}_{2})+{\mathbb{M}}_{1}
.

Conversely, suppose that
x\in\varphi({\mathbb{M}}_{2})+{\mathbb{M}}_{1}.

Let
y\in{\mathbb{M}}_{1}
be such that
x-y\in\varphi({\mathbb{M}}_{2})
. Then

{\mathbb{M}}_{2}\models\varphi(x-y)
, and consequently there is
\bar{b}=((b_{i})_{i<\alpha})\in{\mathbb{M}}_{2}
such that

M_{2}\models\sigma_{j}(\bar{b},x-y)=0

for all
j<\beta
.
Set
\bar{z}:=\bar{0}.
Then
{\mathbb{M}}_{2}\models\varphi_{*}(\bar{z})
, and hence by our assumption
{\mathbb{M}}_{1}\models\varphi_{*}(\bar{z})
.
It follows that for some
\bar{b}^{\prime}=(b^{\prime}_{i})_{i<\alpha}
and
x^{\prime}
in
{\mathbb{M}}_{1}
and

M_{1}\models\sigma_{j}(\bar{b}^{\prime},x^{\prime})=0

for all
j<\beta
.
Thus,
{\mathbb{M}}_{2}
satisfies the same formulas. Therefore,

M_{2}\models\sigma_{j}((b_{i}-b^{\prime}_{i})_{i<\alpha},x-y-x^{\prime})=0

for all
j<\beta.

Since
\bar{b}^{\prime},y+x^{\prime}
are in
{\mathbb{M}}_{1}
, we have

{\mathbb{M}}_{2}/{\mathbb{M}}_{1}\models\sigma_{j}(\bar{b}+{\mathbb{M}}_{1},x+{\mathbb{M}}_{1})=0

for all
j<\beta
.
This implies
{\mathbb{M}}_{2}/{\mathbb{M}}_{1}\models\varphi(x+{\mathbb{M}}_{1})
. Hence,
x+{\mathbb{M}}_{1}\in\varphi({\mathbb{M}}_{2}/{\mathbb{M}}_{1})
.

(2). This is in Lemma
2.13
.

(3). Let us first show that
\bigcup_{i<\delta}{\mathbb{M}}_{i}\leq^{\rm pr}_{\varphi}{\mathbb{M}}
. To see this, let
\psi(\bar{x})
be a subformula of
\varphi

and
\bar{z}\in\bigcup_{i<\delta}{\mathbb{M}}_{i}.
There is
i_{*}<\delta
such that
\bar{z}\in{\mathbb{M}}_{i_{*}}
. Then for all
i_{*}\leq i<\delta
,

{\mathbb{M}}_{i}\models\psi(\bar{z})
iff
{\mathbb{M}}\models\psi(\bar{z})
.
It follows that

\bigcup_{i<\delta}{\mathbb{M}}_{i}\models\psi(\bar{z})\iff{\mathbb{M}}\models\psi(\bar{z}).

Let
j<\delta
,
\psi(\bar{x})
a subformula of
\varphi
and
\bar{z}\in{\mathbb{M}}_{j}
. We conclude from the above argument
that

{\mathbb{M}}_{j}\models\psi(\bar{z})\iff{\mathbb{M}}\models\psi(\bar{z})\iff\bigcup_{i<\delta}{\mathbb{M}}_{i}\models\psi(\bar{z}).

Thus
{\mathbb{M}}_{j}\leq^{\rm pr}_{\varphi}\bigcup_{i<\delta}{\mathbb{M}}_{i}
.

(4) It is easily seen that if
{\mathbb{M}}_{1}\leq^{\rm pr}_{\varphi}{\mathbb{M}}_{2}
then
{\mathbb{M}}_{1}\leq^{\rm pr}_{\varphi}{\mathbb{M}}_{1}+{\mathbb{N}}
for each
<\kappa
-generated submodule

{\mathbb{N}}
of

{\mathbb{M}}_{2}
.

Conversely, suppose that the above condition holds.
Let
\psi(\bar{x})
be a subformula of
\varphi
. Then it is a simple formula as well, thus it is of the form

\exists_{i<\alpha}v_{i}\bigwedge_{j<\beta}[\sigma_{j}(\bar{x},\bar{v})=0],

for some terms
\sigma_{j}.
Let
\bar{x}\in{\mathbb{M}}_{1}
. It is obvious that
if
{\mathbb{M}}_{1}\models\psi(\bar{x})
, then
{\mathbb{M}}_{2}\models\psi(\bar{x})
.
Now let
{\mathbb{M}}_{2}\models\psi(\bar{x})
. Thus we can find
\bar{b}\in{\mathbb{M}}_{2}
such
that

{\mathbb{M}}_{2}\models\bigwedge_{j<\beta}[\sigma_{j}(\bar{x},\bar{b})=0].

Let
{\mathbb{N}}
be the submodule generated by
\bar{b}
. Then

{\mathbb{M}}_{1}+{\mathbb{N}}\models\bigwedge_{j<\beta}[\sigma_{j}(\bar{x},\bar{b})=0]
. We combine this
along with
our assumption to conclude that
{\mathbb{M}}_{1}\models\psi(\bar{x})
.

(5). We proceed by induction on the complexity of the formula
\varphi
(see also Lemma
2.13
), and we leave the routine check to the reader.
∎

Let
\kappa
be an infinite cardinal and let
{\mathfrak{m}}=({\mathcal{K}},{\mathbb{M}}_{\ast},{{\mathfrak{E}}},{\bf R},{\bf S},{\bf T})
be a context.
Set
{\rm{\rm Mod}}_{{\mathfrak{m}},\kappa}:=\{{\mathbb{M}}:{\mathbb{M}}_{\ast}\leq^{\rm ads}_{{\mathcal{K}},\kappa}{\mathbb{M}}\}
.

Lemma 3.20
.

Let
{\mathfrak{m}}
be a context as above and let
\kappa
be an infinite cardinal.

(1)

If
{\mathbb{M}}_{1}\leq_{\kappa}{\mathbb{M}}_{2},
then
{\mathbb{M}}_{1}
is a sub-module of
{\mathbb{M}}_{2}.

(2)

\leq_{\kappa}
is a partial order on
{\rm{\rm Mod}}_{{\mathfrak{m}},\kappa}.

(3)

If
\kappa\leq\theta
and
{\mathbb{M}}\leq_{\theta}{\mathbb{N}}
then

{\mathbb{M}}\leq_{\kappa}{\mathbb{N}}
.

(4)

If
\langle{\mathbb{M}}_{i}:i<\delta\rangle
is a
\leq_{\kappa}
-increasing and continuous in
{\rm{\rm Mod}}_{{\mathfrak{m}},\kappa}
, then

(a)

\bigcup\limits_{i<\delta}{\mathbb{M}}_{i}\in{\rm{\rm Mod}}_{{\mathfrak{m}},\kappa},

(b)

For each
j<\delta,{\mathbb{M}}_{j}\leq_{\kappa}\bigcup\limits_{i<\delta}{\mathbb{M}}_{i}
.

(5)

If
{\mathbb{M}}_{1},{\mathbb{M}}_{2},{\mathbb{M}}_{3}\in{\rm{\rm Mod}}_{{\mathfrak{m}},\kappa}
are such that

{\mathbb{M}}_{1},{\mathbb{M}}_{2}\leq_{\kappa}{\mathbb{M}}_{3}
and
{\mathbb{M}}_{1}\subseteq{\mathbb{M}}_{2}
, then
{\mathbb{M}}_{1}\leq_{\kappa}{\mathbb{M}}_{2}
.

(6)
