See Definition
5.18
for the definition of
\hat{\bf f}_{n}.

\begin{array}[]{lr}{\bf E}_{n}:=\big\{{\bf h}^{{\mathfrak{e}},n}[{\mathbb{M}}](\hat{\bf f}_{n}):{\mathbf{f}}\in{\rm End}({\mathbb{M}})\mbox{ and there are }z_{n}({\mathbf{f}})\in{\mathbb{L}}^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n}\mbox{ and }\\
\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\alpha_{n}({\mathbf{f}})<\kappa\text{ such that }({\mathord{\mathrm{Pr}}}1)^{n}_{\alpha_{n}({\mathbf{f}}),z_{n}({\mathbf{f}})}[{\mathbf{f}},{\mathfrak{e}}]\big\}.\end{array}

Then

•

{\rm End}^{{\mathfrak{e}},\omega}({\mathbb{M}})=\bigcup\limits_{n<\omega}{\bf E}_{n}
,

•

{\bf E}_{n}\subseteq{\bf E}_{n+1}
,

•

z_{n}({\mathbf{f}})

is unique modulo
\varphi^{\mathfrak{e}}_{\omega}({\mathbb{N}}^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n})
.

(v)

{\bf E}_{n}
is a subring of

{\rm End}^{\mathfrak{e},\omega}({\mathbb{M}})
and the
mapping
\hat{{\mathbf{f}}}_{n}\mapsto{\bf h}^{\mathfrak{e},n}_{z_{n}({\mathbf{f}})}

is a homomorphism from

\begin{array}[]{lr}\{\hat{{\mathbf{f}}}_{n}:&{\mathbf{f}}\in{\rm End}({\mathbb{M}})\mbox{ and
}({\mathord{\mathrm{Pr}}}1)^{n}_{\alpha_{n}({\mathbf{f}}),z_{n}({\mathbf{f}})}\mbox{ for some }\\
&\alpha_{n}({\mathbf{f}})<\kappa,\ z_{n}({\mathbf{f}})\in{\mathbb{L}}^{\mathord{\mathrm{tr}}}_{n}\}\end{array}

into
{\bf{dE}}^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n}
with kernel

{\rm End}^{{\mathfrak{e}},n}_{<\lambda}({\mathbb{M}})
, i.e.,

\{{\mathbf{f}}\in{\rm End}^{{\mathfrak{e}},n}({\mathbb{M}}):z_{n}({\mathbf{f}})\in\varphi^{\mathfrak{e}}_{\omega}({\mathbb{N}}_{n})\}.

(vi)

The ring
{\bf S}
is naturally mapped into

{\rm End}({\mathbb{M}})
, for
each
\alpha\leq\omega
, there is a natural
homomorphism from
{\rm End}({\mathbb{M}})
to
{\rm End}^{{\mathfrak{e}},\alpha}({\mathbb{M}})
, where for

\alpha<\omega
has a natural mapping to
{\bf{dE}}
.
In particular,
{\bf S}
is naturally mapped
into
dE^{\mathfrak{e}}
.

Proof.

(i). In view of Lemma
5.20
(ii),
{\bf f}

induces a homomorphism from

{\mathfrak{B}}^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n}({\mathbb{M}}_{1})\to{\mathfrak{B}}^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n}({\mathbb{M}}_{2})
,
and we conclude from Lemma
5.16
that
z\in{\mathbb{L}}^{{\mathfrak{e}},\ast}_{n}
.
By definition,
{\bf h}^{{\mathfrak{e}},n}_{z}\in{\bf{dE}}^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n,\ast}
.

(ii). For some formula
\psi(x,y)\in\mathcal{L}_{\mu,\kappa}^{pe}(\tau_{({\bf R},{\bf S})})
, for all
{\mathbb{M}}
, and all
x,y\in\psi_{n}({\mathbb{M}})
we
have

{\bf h}^{{\mathfrak{e}},n}_{{\mathbb{M}},z}(x+\varphi_{\omega}({\mathbb{M}}))=y+\varphi_{\omega}({\mathbb{M}})\Leftrightarrow{\mathbb{M}}\models\psi(x,y).

Now, we define
\psi^{\prime}(x,y)
by the following role

{\mathbb{M}}\models\psi^{\prime}(x,y)\Leftrightarrow{\mathbb{M}}\models\psi(y,x).

Since
{\bf f}
is an automorphism,
\psi^{\prime}(x,y)
satisfies the assumptions (i)-(iii) of Lemma
5.22
(3),
and hence for some
z^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{L}}^{{\mathfrak{e}}}_{n}
and all
x,y\in\psi_{n}({\mathbb{M}})
,

{\bf h}^{{\mathfrak{e}},n}_{{\mathbb{M}},z^{\prime}}(x+\varphi_{\omega}({\mathbb{M}}))=y+\varphi_{\omega}({\mathbb{M}})\Leftrightarrow{\mathbb{M}}\models\psi^{\prime}(x,y).

It is now clear that

{\bf h}^{{\mathfrak{e}},n}_{{\mathbb{M}},z^{\prime}}
is the inverse of
{\bf h}^{{\mathfrak{e}},n}_{{\mathbb{M}},z}
. In particular,
{\bf h}^{{\mathfrak{e}},n}_{{\mathbb{M}},z}
is an
automorphism of
{\mathfrak{B}}^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n}({\mathbb{M}})
.

(iii)
+
(iv)
+
(v). For each
n
, set

\begin{array}[]{lr}{\bf F}_{n}=\{\hat{{\mathbf{f}}}_{n}:&{\mathbf{f}}\in{\rm End}({\mathbb{M}})\mbox{ and
}({\mathord{\mathrm{Pr}}}1)^{n}_{\alpha_{n}({\mathbf{f}}),z_{n}({\mathbf{f}})}\mbox{ for some }\\
&\alpha_{n}({\mathbf{f}})<\kappa,\ z_{n}({\mathbf{f}})\in{\mathbb{L}}^{\mathord{\mathrm{tr}}}_{n}\}.\end{array}

We will show that:

(1)

{\rm End}^{{\mathfrak{e}},\omega}({\mathbb{M}}_{\kappa})=\bigcup\limits_{n<\omega}{\bf E}_{n}
.

(2)

{\bf E}_{n}
is a subring of

{\rm End}^{\mathfrak{e},\omega}({\mathbb{M}})
.

(3)

the
assignment
\hat{{\mathbf{f}}}_{n}\mapsto{\bf h}^{\mathfrak{e},n}_{z_{n}({\mathbf{f}})}

yields a homomorphism

\varrho_{n}:{\bf F}_{n}\to{\bf{dE}}^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n}
with kernel

\{\hat{{\mathbf{f}}}_{n}\in{\rm End}^{{\mathfrak{e}},n}({\mathbb{M}}):z_{n}({\mathbf{f}})\in\varphi^{\mathfrak{e}}_{\omega}({\mathbb{N}}_{n})\}.

(4)

The assignment
{\bf h}^{\mathfrak{e},n}[{\mathbb{M}}](\hat{{\mathbf{f}}}_{n})\mapsto{\bf h}^{\mathfrak{e},n}_{z_{n}({\mathbf{f}})}
induces a homomorphism
{\bf E}_{n}\to{\bf{dE}}^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n}
.

To see (1), clearly we have
\bigcup\limits_{n<\omega}{\bf E}_{n}\subset{\rm End}^{{\mathfrak{e}},\omega}({\mathbb{M}}_{\kappa})
. To see the reverse inclusion take
{\bf g}\in{\rm End}^{{\mathfrak{e}},\omega}({\mathbb{M}}_{\kappa})
. Then for some
n<\omega
we have
{\bf g}={\bf h}^{\mathfrak{e},n}[{\mathbb{M}}](\hat{{\mathbf{f}}}_{n})
where
{\mathbf{f}}\in{\rm End}^{{\mathfrak{e}},n}({\mathbb{M}})
.
Here, we are going to use the a strongly semi-nice construction. In view of this assumption, we can find

\alpha_{n}({\mathbf{f}})<\lambda
and
z_{n}({\mathbf{f}})\in{\mathbb{L}}^{\mathord{\mathrm{tr}}}_{n}
such that the property
({\mathord{\mathrm{Pr}}}1)^{n}_{\alpha_{n}({\mathbf{f}}),z_{n}({\mathbf{f}})}
holds. By definition
{\bf g}\in{\bf E}_{n}
. This completes the proof of
(1)
.

(2)
. This is clear and we leave it to the reader.

(3)
. Note that
z_{n}({\mathbf{f}}_{1}\pm{\mathbf{f}}_{2})=z_{n}({\mathbf{f}}_{1})\pm z_{n}({\mathbf{f}}_{2})
modulo
\varphi_{\omega}({\mathbb{N}}_{n})
. This shows that
\varrho_{n}
is additive:

\varrho_{n}({\mathbf{f}}_{1}\pm{\mathbf{f}}_{2})={\bf h}^{n}_{z_{n}({\mathbf{f}}_{1}\pm{\mathbf{f}}_{2})}={\bf h}^{n}_{z_{n}({\mathbf{f}}_{1})}\pm{\bf h}^{n}_{z_{n}({\mathbf{f}}_{2})}=\varrho_{n}({\mathbf{f}}_{1})+\varrho_{n}({\mathbf{f}}_{2}).

Similarly, for any
r\in R
we have

\varrho_{n}(r{\mathbf{f}})=\varrho_{n}(r{\mathbf{f}})
. Here, we compute
\ker(\varrho_{n})
:

\varrho_{n}({\mathbf{f}})=0\Leftrightarrow{\bf h}^{n}_{z_{n}({\mathbf{f}})}={\bf h}^{n}_{0}\Leftrightarrow z_{n}({\mathbf{f}})\in\varphi_{\omega}({\mathbb{N}}_{n}),

i.e.,
\ker(\varrho_{n})=\{\hat{\bf f}_{n}\in{\bf F}_{n}:z_{n}({\mathbf{f}})\in\varphi_{\omega}({\mathbb{N}}_{n})\}
, as claimed.

(4)
. This is trivial.

Items (iv) and (v) follow immediately. The assignment

{\bf h}^{\mathfrak{e},n}[{\mathbb{M}}](\hat{\bf f}_{n})\mapsto{\bf h}^{\mathfrak{e},n}_{z_{n}({\mathbf{f}})}

defines a homomorphism from

{\rm End}^{{\mathfrak{e}},\omega}({\mathbb{M}})
into

{\bf{dE}}^{\mathfrak{e}}
with kernel

\bigcup\limits_{n<\omega}\{{\bf h}^{\mathfrak{e},n}[{\mathbb{M}}](\hat{\bf f}_{n})\in{\rm End}^{{\mathfrak{e}},\omega}({\mathbb{M}}):z_{n}({\mathbf{f}})\in\varphi^{\mathfrak{e}}_{\omega}({\mathbb{N}}_{n})\},

which is included in
{\rm End}^{{\mathfrak{e}},\omega}_{<\lambda}({\mathbb{M}}).

In sum, we have a natural embedding from the ring

{\rm End}^{{\mathfrak{e}},\omega}({\mathbb{M}})/{\rm End}^{{\mathfrak{e}},\omega}_{<\lambda}({\mathbb{M}})
into

{\bf{dE}}^{\mathfrak{e}}
.

It remains to prove the moreover part of (iii). To this end, let

{\bf S}
be any subring of
{\rm End}^{{\mathfrak{e}},\omega}({\mathbb{M}})/{\rm End}^{{\mathfrak{e}},\omega}_{<\lambda}({\mathbb{M}})
of cardinality
<\lambda
.
For each
s\in{\bf S}
find
n_{s}<\omega
and
{\bf f}_{s}\in{\rm End}({\mathbb{M}})
such that

s={\bf h}^{\mathfrak{e},n}[{\mathbb{M}}](\hat{({\bf f}_{s})}_{n_{s}})+{\rm End}^{{\mathfrak{e}},\omega}_{<\lambda}({\mathbb{M}})
. We look at the following club of
\kappa
:

C_{s}:=\{\alpha<\kappa:\mathop{\mathrm{Rang}}({\bf f}_{s}\restriction{\mathbb{M}}_{\alpha})\subseteq{\mathbb{M}}_{\alpha}\}.

For each

\alpha\in C_{s}\setminus S
, we define an endomorphism
\theta(s):{\mathbb{M}}/{\mathbb{M}}_{\alpha}\to{\mathbb{M}}/{\mathbb{M}}_{\alpha}

by the following role:

\theta(s)(x+{\mathbb{M}}_{\alpha})={\bf f}_{s}(x)+{\mathbb{M}}_{\alpha}.

It is now natural to take
\bigcap\limits_{s\in{\bf S}}C_{s}
as our required club, but we need to do a little more.
Indeed, it is not clear that if
\theta
is a homomorphism, as it may not preserve addition or multiplication.
To handle this, we shrink the above intersection further as follows.
Given
s_{1},\cdots,s_{m}\in{\bf S}
, we have

{\bf h}^{\mathfrak{e},n}[{\mathbb{M}}]((\hat{{\bf f}}_{s_{1}+\cdots+s_{m}})_{n_{s_{1}+\cdots+s_{m}}})-{\bf h}^{\mathfrak{e},n}[{\mathbb{M}}]((\hat{{\bf f}}_{s_{1}})_{n_{s_{1}}})-\cdots-{\bf h}^{\mathfrak{e},n}[{\mathbb{M}}]((\hat{{\bf f}}_{s_{m}})_{n_{s_{m}}})\in{\rm End}^{{\mathfrak{e}},\omega}_{<\lambda}({\mathbb{M}})

and

{\bf h}^{\mathfrak{e},n}[{\mathbb{M}}]((\hat{{\bf f}}_{s_{1}\cdots s_{m}})_{n_{s_{1}\cdots s_{m}}})-\left({\bf h}^{\mathfrak{e},n}[{\mathbb{M}}]((\hat{{\bf f}}_{s_{1}})_{n_{s_{1}}})\circ\cdots\circ{\bf h}^{\mathfrak{e},n}[{\mathbb{M}}]((\hat{{\bf f}}_{s_{m}})_{n_{s_{m}}})\right)\in{\rm End}^{{\mathfrak{e}},\omega}_{<\lambda}({\mathbb{M}}).

Thus we can find
\alpha(s_{1},\cdots,s_{m})<\kappa
such that

\mathop{\mathrm{Rang}}(\left({\bf f}_{s_{1}+\cdots+s_{m}}-{\bf f}_{s_{1}}-\cdots-{\bf f}_{s_{m}}\right)\restriction\psi^{\mathfrak{e}}({\mathbb{M}}))\subseteq{\mathbb{M}}_{\alpha(s_{1},\cdots,s_{m})}

and

\mathop{\mathrm{Rang}}(\left({\bf f}_{s_{1}\cdots s_{m}}-\left({\bf f}_{s_{1}}\circ\cdots\circ{\bf f}_{s_{m}}\right)\right)\restriction\psi^{\mathfrak{e}}({\mathbb{M}}))\subseteq{\mathbb{M}}_{\alpha(s_{1},\cdots,s_{m})}.

Let
\alpha(\ast)=\mathop{\mathrm{sup}}\{\alpha(s_{1},\cdots,s_{m}):m<\omega,~s_{1},\cdots,s_{m}\in{\bf S}\}<\kappa
.
Then
C=\bigcap\limits_{s\in{\bf S}}C_{s}\setminus(\alpha(\ast)+1)
is as required. The key point is that for
s_{1},\cdots,s_{m}\in S,

and
\alpha\in C,
modulo
{\mathbb{M}}_{\alpha}
, we have

{\bf f}_{s_{1}+\cdots+s_{m}}={\bf f}_{s_{1}}+\cdots+{\bf f}_{s_{m}}

and

{\bf f}_{s_{1}\cdots s_{m}}={\bf f}_{s_{1}}\circ\cdots\circ{\bf f}_{s_{m}}.

(vi). Let
s\in S
. The notation
{\bf f}_{s}
stands
for the multiplication map by
s
, e.g.,
{\bf f}_{s}\in{\rm End}({\mathbb{M}})
and it is defined by
{\bf f}_{s}(x)=xs
.
The assignment
s\mapsto{\bf f}_{s}
defines an embedding

S\longrightarrow{\rm End}({\mathbb{M}})
. Let
\alpha<\omega
.
The map
{\bf f}\mapsto\hat{{\bf f}}_{n}
yields a natural homomorphism
{\rm End}({\mathbb{M}})\to{\rm End}^{{\mathfrak{e}},n}({\mathbb{M}})
. For
\alpha:=\omega
, as
{\rm End}^{{\mathfrak{e}},\omega}({\mathbb{M}})

is the direct limit of
\langle{\rm End}^{{\mathfrak{e}},n}({\mathbb{M}}):n<\omega\rangle
,
and by the above argument, we have a natural homomorphism
{\rm End}({\mathbb{M}})\to{\rm End}^{{\mathfrak{e}},\omega}({\mathbb{M}})
. Finally,
the assignment
{\bf h}^{\mathfrak{e},n}[{\mathbb{M}}](\hat{\bf f}_{n})\mapsto{\bf h}^{n}_{z_{n}({\mathbf{f}})}
defines a homomorphism
{\rm End}^{{\mathfrak{e}},\omega}({\mathbb{M}})\to{\bf{dE}}
.
∎

Definition 5.26
.

By
{\rm End}^{{\mathfrak{e}},n}_{cpt}({\mathbb{M}})
we mean

\left\{h\in{\rm End}^{{\mathfrak{e}},n}:\mbox{the range of $h$ is compact for }({\mathfrak{e}},n)\mbox{ in }{\mathbb{M}}_{\lambda}\right\}.

Remark 5.27
.

In Lemma
5.25
we can replace

{\rm End}^{{\mathfrak{e}},n}_{<\lambda}({\mathbb{M}})

with

{\rm End}^{{\mathfrak{e}},n}_{cpt}({\mathbb{M}})
and drive the analogue statement.
Since this has no role in this paper, we leave the routine modification to the reader.

Lemma 5.28
.

Let
{\mathfrak{m}}:=(\mathcal{K},{\mathbb{M}}_{\ast},{{\mathfrak{E}}},{\bf R},{\bf S},{\bf T})
be a
\lambda
-context,
where
\lambda
is a regular cardinal such that
\lambda=\lambda^{\aleph_{0}}>|{\bf R}|+|{\bf S}|+\aleph_{0}+||{\mathbb{M}}_{*}||
and for all
\alpha<\lambda,~\alpha^{\aleph_{0}}<\lambda
.
Then there
is a bi-module
{\mathbb{M}}\geq_{\aleph_{0}}{\mathbb{M}}_{\ast}

satisfying

\|{\mathbb{M}}\|=\lambda=|\psi^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n}({\mathbb{M}})/\varphi^{\mathfrak{e}}_{\omega}({\mathbb{M}})|
such that
{\mathbb{M}}
has few direct decompositions in the
following sense:

(i)

if
{\mathbb{M}}=\mathop{{\bigoplus}}\limits_{t\in J}{\mathbb{M}}_{t}
and
{\mathfrak{e}}\in{{\mathfrak{E}}}
, then
for all but finitely many

t\in J
we have
\bigvee\limits_{n}[\psi^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n}({\mathbb{M}}_{t})\subseteq\varphi^{\mathfrak{e}}_{\omega}({\mathbb{M}}_{t})]
. In particular,

\bigvee\limits_{n}[\varphi^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n}({\mathbb{M}}_{t})=\varphi^{\mathfrak{e}}_{\omega}({\mathbb{M}}_{t})],

provided
{\mathfrak{e}}
is simple.

(ii)

Assume in addition that
|{\bf R}|+|{\bf S}|<2^{\aleph_{0}}
and that
{\mathbb{M}}={\mathbb{K}}_{\alpha}\oplus{\mathbb{P}}_{\alpha}
for
\alpha<(|{\bf R}|+|{\bf S}|+\aleph_{0})^{+}
. Then
for some

\alpha_{0}<\alpha_{1}
and some
n<\omega
we have
\psi^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n}({\mathbb{K}}_{\alpha_{\ell}})\subseteq\varphi^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n}({\mathbb{K}}_{\alpha_{1}})+\varphi^{\mathfrak{e}}_{\omega}({\mathbb{M}}),
where
\ell<2
. In particular, if
{\mathfrak{e}}
is simple, then

\varphi^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n}({\mathbb{K}}_{\alpha_{1}})+\varphi^{\mathfrak{e}}_{\omega}({\mathbb{M}})=\varphi^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n}({\mathbb{K}}_{\alpha_{2}})+\varphi^{\mathfrak{e}}_{\omega}({\mathbb{M}}).

(iii)

{\rm End}^{\mathfrak{e},\omega}({\mathbb{M}})/{\rm End}^{{\mathfrak{e}},\omega}_{<\lambda}({\mathbb{M}})
has
cardinality
\leq|{\bf R}|+|{\bf S}|+\aleph_{0}
.

Proof.

Let
\langle{\mathbb{M}}_{\alpha}:\alpha\leq\lambda\rangle
be a strongly semi-nice construction for

{\mathfrak{m}}
where
S\subset S^{\lambda}_{\aleph_{0}}
is stationary
such that
S^{\lambda}_{\aleph_{0}}\setminus S
is stationary as well. Let
{\mathbb{M}}:={\mathbb{M}}_{\lambda}
. We show
{\mathbb{M}}
is as required.

(i). Suppose not. Let

{\mathbb{M}}=\mathop{{\bigoplus}}\limits_{t\in J}{\mathbb{M}}_{t}
and
{\mathfrak{e}}\in{{\mathfrak{E}}}
be a counterexample to the claim.
Without loss of the generality,

J=\omega
and that for each
n<\omega,~\psi^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n}({\mathbb{M}}_{n})\nsubseteq\varphi^{\mathfrak{e}}_{\omega}({\mathbb{M}}_{n})
.
Define
{\bf f}:{\mathbb{M}}\to{\mathbb{M}}
in such a way that

{\bf f}(x)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}0&x\in{\mathbb{M}}_{2n},\\
x&x\in{\mathbb{M}}_{2n+1}\end{array}\right.

In other words,
{\bf f}
is the natural projection from
{\mathbb{M}}
onto
\bigoplus\limits_{n<\omega}{\mathbb{M}}_{2n+1}
.
Then for some
n(\ast)<\omega
,
\alpha<\lambda
and
z\in{\mathbb{L}}^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n(\ast)}

the property
({\mathord{\mathrm{Pr}}}1)^{n(\ast)}_{\alpha,z}[{\bf{\bf f}},{\mathfrak{e}}]

holds.
Let
\bar{\mathbb{G}}^{\ast}=\langle{\mathbb{G}}^{\ast}_{n}:n\geq n(\ast)\rangle
be a decreasing sequence of additive subgroups of
\varphi^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n(\ast)}({\mathbb{M}})
as in Lemma
4.17
. In particular,

(1)

If
n\geq n(\ast)
,
{\bf h}:{\mathbb{N}}^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n}\to{\mathbb{M}}
and
z_{n}:=g_{n(\ast),n}(z)
, then

{\bf f}({\bf h}(x^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n}))-{\bf h}(z_{n})\in{\mathbb{G}}_{n}^{\ast}
.

(2)

If
z^{*}_{\ell}\in\mathbb{G}^{\ast}_{\ell},
for
\ell\geq n(\ast),
then there exists
z^{*}\in\mathbb{G}^{\ast}_{n(\ast)}

such that
z^{*}-\sum\limits_{\ell=n(\ast)}^{n}z_{\ell}^{*}\in\varphi^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n+1}({\mathbb{M}})
.

Due to Lemma
4.19
(1) we know that there are
k,m<\omega
such that

(3)

{\mathbb{G}}^{\ast}_{m}\subseteq\mathop{{\bigoplus}}\limits_{\ell<k}{\mathbb{M}}_{\ell}+\varphi^{\mathfrak{e}}_{\omega}({\mathbb{M}}).

For each
n>n(\ast)+m+k
pick some
y_{n}\in\psi^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n}({\mathbb{M}}_{n})\setminus\varphi_{\omega}^{\mathfrak{e}}({\mathbb{M}}_{n})
.
Let also

{\bf h}_{n}:{\mathbb{N}}^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n}\to{\mathbb{M}}_{n}
be a bimodule homomorphism such that
y_{n}={\bf h}_{n}(x_{n}^{\mathfrak{e}})
.
We choose
n
large enough.
The assignment

x\mapsto({\bf h}_{n}(x),{\bf h}_{n+1}(g_{n}(x))\in{\mathbb{M}}_{n}\oplus{\mathbb{M}}_{n+1}\subset{\mathbb{M}}

defines a
map
{\bf h}:{\mathbb{N}}_{n}\to{\mathbb{M}}
. This unifies
{\bf h}_{n+1}
and
{\bf h}_{n}
.
By symmetry, we may assume that
n
is even.
It then follows from clause (3) that

{\bf h}(z_{n})=({\bf h}_{n}(z_{n}),{\bf h}_{n+1}(z_{n+1}))\in\varphi_{\omega}^{\mathfrak{e}}({\mathbb{M}}_{n})\subset\varphi_{\omega}^{\mathfrak{e}}({\mathbb{M}})

and

y_{n}-{\bf h}(z_{n+1})=y_{n}-({\bf h}_{n+1}(z_{n+1}),{\bf h}_{n+2}(z_{n+2}))\in\varphi_{\omega}^{\mathfrak{e}}({\mathbb{M}}_{n})\subset\varphi_{\omega}^{\mathfrak{e}}({\mathbb{M}}).

It follows from the first one that
{\bf h}_{n}(z_{n})
and
{\bf h}_{n+1}(z_{n+1})
are in
\varphi_{\omega}^{\mathfrak{e}}({\mathbb{M}})
.
By repetition,
{\bf h}_{n+2}(z_{n+2})
is in
\varphi_{\omega}^{\mathfrak{e}}({\mathbb{M}})
.
We plug these in the second containment to see

y_{n}\in\varphi_{\omega}^{\mathfrak{e}}({\mathbb{M}}).

This is a contradiction that we searched for it.

Here, we assume that
\mathfrak{e}
is simple. According to Lemma
5.8
,
\psi^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n}({\mathbb{M}}_{t})=\varphi^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n}({\mathbb{M}}_{t})
for all
n
and
t\in J
. Hence for the
n
as chosen above, we have

\varphi^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n}({\mathbb{M}}_{t})=\varphi^{\mathfrak{e}}_{\omega}({\mathbb{M}}_{t}),

as required.

(ii). For each
\alpha<(|{\bf R}|+|{\bf S}|+\aleph_{0})^{+}
, let
{\bf f}_{\alpha}
be the projection onto
{\mathbb{K}}_{\alpha}
, i.e.,

{\bf f}_{\alpha}(x)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}x&x\in{\mathbb{K}}_{\alpha},\\
0&x\in{\mathbb{P}}_{\alpha}\end{array}\right.

Pick
n_{\ast}(\alpha)<\omega
,
z_{\ast}(\alpha)\in{\mathbb{L}}^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n_{\ast}(\alpha)}
and

\beta_{\ast}(\alpha)
such that
({\mathord{\mathrm{Pr}}}1)^{n_{\ast}(\alpha)}_{\beta_{\ast}(\alpha),z_{\ast}(\alpha)}[{\bf f}_{\alpha},\mathfrak{e}]

holds. We combine Lemma
4.17
along with Lemma
4.19
(1) to find
m(\alpha)<\omega
and a compact and decreasing sequence
\langle\bar{\mathbb{G}}^{\ast}_{\ell}:\ell\geq m(\alpha)\rangle
of additive subgroups of
\varphi^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n(\ast)}({\mathbb{M}})
. Furthermore, in the light of Lemma
4.21
we can assume that
\mathbb{G}^{\ast}_{m(\alpha)}\subseteq\varphi^{\mathfrak{e}}_{\omega}({\mathbb{M}}).
There exists a stationary set
S
of
\alpha<(|{\bf R}|+|{\bf S}|+\aleph_{0})^{+}

such that for some
n<\omega
and some fixed
z\in{\mathbb{L}}^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n}
, and for all
\alpha\in S,
we have
n_{\ast}(\alpha)=n
and
z_{\ast}(\alpha)=z
. Let

\alpha_{0}<\alpha_{1}
be in
S
and
\ell<2
. It follows that:

\begin{array}[]{ll}y\in\psi^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n}({\mathbb{K}}_{\alpha_{\ell}})&\Rightarrow\exists{\bf h}:{\mathbb{N}}^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n}\to{\mathbb{K}}_{\alpha_{\ell}},\quad{\bf h}(x^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n})=y\\
&\Rightarrow{\bf f}(y)-{\bf h}(z_{n})\in{\mathbb{G}}^{\ast}_{n}\subset\varphi^{\mathfrak{e}}_{\omega}({\mathbb{M}})\\
&\Rightarrow y-{\bf h}(z_{n})\in\varphi^{\mathfrak{e}}_{\omega}({\mathbb{M}})\\
&\Rightarrow y\in\varphi^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n}({\mathbb{K}}_{\alpha_{\ell}})+\varphi^{\mathfrak{e}}_{\omega}({\mathbb{M}}).\end{array}

We proved that
\psi^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n}({\mathbb{K}}_{\alpha_{\ell}})\subseteq\varphi^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n}({\mathbb{K}}_{\alpha_{\ell}})+\varphi^{\mathfrak{e}}_{\omega}({\mathbb{M}})
.

Now suppose that
{\mathfrak{e}}
is simple. In view of Lemma
5.8
,
\psi^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n}({\mathbb{K}}_{\alpha_{\ell}})=\varphi^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n}({\mathbb{K}}_{\alpha_{\ell}})
.
It then follows that

\varphi^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n}({\mathbb{K}}_{\alpha_{\ell}})+\varphi^{\mathfrak{e}}_{\omega}({\mathbb{M}})=\varphi^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n}({\mathbb{K}}_{\alpha_{1-\ell}})+\varphi^{\mathfrak{e}}_{\omega}({\mathbb{M}}).

(iii). We apply the notation
introduced in Lemma
5.25
. Recall that

E_{n}
is defined by the natural image of

\begin{array}[]{lr}F_{n}:=\{{\mathbf{f}}\restriction(\varphi_{n}^{\mathfrak{e}}({\mathbb{M}}_{\lambda})/\varphi^{\mathfrak{e}}_{\omega}({\mathbb{M}}_{\lambda}):&{\mathbf{f}}\in{\rm End}({\mathbb{M}})\mbox{ and
}({\mathord{\mathrm{Pr}}}1)^{n}_{\alpha_{n}({\mathbf{f}}),z_{n}({\mathbf{f}})}\mbox{ for some }\\
&\alpha_{n}(F)<\lambda,\ z_{n}({\mathbf{f}})\in{\mathbb{L}}^{\mathord{\mathrm{tr}}}_{n}\}\end{array}

in
{\rm End}^{\bar{\varphi},\omega}({\mathbb{M}})
. Also,
the
mapping
{\mathbf{f}}\mapsto\bf{h}^{n}_{z_{n}({\mathbf{f}})}

induces a homomorphism from

F_{n}

into
{\bf{dE}}^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n}
with kernel

{\rm End}^{{\mathfrak{e}},n}_{<\lambda}({\mathbb{M}})
.
Since
{\rm End}^{{\mathfrak{e}},\omega}({\mathbb{M}})=\bigcup\limits_{n<\omega}{\bf E}_{n}
, it is enough to show that

\{\bf{h}^{n}_{z_{n}({\mathbf{f}})}:z_{n}({\mathbf{f}})\in{\mathbb{L}}^{\mathord{\mathrm{tr}}}_{n}\}
is of cardinality at most

\leq|{\bf R}|+|{\bf S}|+\aleph_{0}
. Thus it is enough
to show that the cardinality of
{\mathbb{N}}^{{\mathfrak{e}}}_{n}
is at most

\leq|{\bf R}|+|{\bf S}|+\aleph_{0}
. This holds, because

{\mathbb{N}}^{{\mathfrak{e}}}_{n}
as an
({\bf R},{\bf S})
bimodule is finitely generated. ∎

Remark 5.29
.

If we omit “
|{\bf R}|+|{\bf S}|<2^{\aleph_{0}}
”, we get by the same proof weaker
conclusions: with an “error term” which is included in a finitely
generated bimodule.

The following is supposed to be used together with any of the later lemmas
here as its conclusion is in their assumptions.

Lemma 5.30
.

Let
{\bf R}
be a ring which is not purely semisimple and let
{\bf S}:={\bf T}:=\langle 1\rangle_{\bf R}
. Let
{\mathcal{K}}={\mathcal{K}}[{\bf R},\mu]

be the family of
(\leq\mu_{1})
generated and
(\leq\theta)

presented bimodules where
\mu_{1}^{\theta}\leq\mu
and let
{\mathbb{M}}_{*}

be a bimodule of cardinality
<\mu
. Finally, let

{\mathfrak{E}}:={\mathfrak{E}}_{{\bf R},\mu}
be the set of
(<\kappa)
-simple
non trivial
{\mathfrak{e}}\in{\mathfrak{E}}_{({\bf R},{\bf S})}
wherever

(||{\bf R}||+\aleph_{0})^{<\kappa}\leq\mu
. The following assertions are true.

(a)

There is
{\mathfrak{e}}\in{{\mathfrak{E}}}
which is very nice.

(b)

If
(|{\bf R}|+\aleph_{0})^{\aleph_{0}}\leq\mu

then
{\mathfrak{e}}({\bar{\varphi}})\in{{\mathfrak{E}}}
for every very nice
\bar{\varphi}
.

(c)

{\mathfrak{m}}=({\mathcal{K}},{\mathbb{M}}_{*},{\mathfrak{E}},{\bf R},{\bf S},{\bf T})
is a non trivial context which is simple.

Proof.

Since
{\bf R}
is not purely semisimple, and in the light of Theorem
2.21
, we can find a sequence
\bar{\varphi}=\langle\varphi_{n}(x):n<\omega\rangle

as in Definition
5.2
. Then
\mathfrak{e}=\mathfrak{e}(\bar{\varphi})\in{\mathfrak{E}}
is very nice. This confirms (a).
Items (b) and (c) are clear.
∎

Lemma 5.31
.

Suppose
{\bf R}
is a ring,
T
is a complete first order theory of

{\bf R}
-modules which is not superstable,
{\bf S}={\bf T}=\langle 1\rangle_{\bf R}
and
\mu\geq|{\bf R}|+\kappa
.
Then there is a family

{\mathcal{K}}\cup\{{\mathbb{M}}_{\ast}\}
of
{\bf R}
-modules with
\mu
members such that:

(a)

{\mathbb{M}}_{\ast}\oplus\mathop{{\bigoplus}}\limits_{t\in I}{\mathbb{M}}_{t}
is model
of
T
whenever
{\mathbb{M}}_{t}\in{\mathcal{K}}
, moreover

{\mathbb{M}}_{\ast}\prec_{\mathcal{L}({\tau_{{\bf R}}})}{\mathbb{M}}_{*}\oplus\mathop{{\bigoplus}}\limits_{t\in I}{\mathbb{M}}_{t}.

(b)

For any
{\mathbb{N}}\in{\mathcal{K}}
we have
||{\mathbb{N}}||\leq\mu
.

(c)

If
2^{\mu_{1}}\leq\mu
and
\mu_{1}\geq||{\bf R}||+\kappa
, then every model
{\mathbb{N}}
of
T
satisfying

{\mathbb{M}}_{*}\prec_{\mathcal{L}({\tau_{{\bf R}}})}{\mathbb{M}}_{*}\oplus{\mathbb{N}}

belongs to
{\mathcal{K}}
.

(d)

Let
2^{\mu_{1}}\leq\mu
and
\mu_{1}\geq||{\bf R}||+\aleph_{0}
. Then every appropriate sequence
\langle{\mathbb{N}}_{n},g_{n},x_{n}:n<\omega\rangle
with
||{\mathbb{N}}_{n}||\leq\mu_{1}
belongs to
{\mathcal{K}}
.

(e)

Let
{\mathfrak{E}}
be the set of
(<\aleph_{0})
-simple
non trivial
{\mathfrak{e}}\in{\mathfrak{E}}_{({\bf R},{\bf S})}
such that each
{\mathbb{N}}_{n}^{\mathfrak{e}}
is in
\mathcal{K}
.
Then
{\mathfrak{m}}=({\mathcal{K}},{\mathbb{M}}_{*},{\mathfrak{E}},{\bf R},{\bf S},{\bf T})
is a
\mu
-context; note that a bimodule for
{\mathfrak{m}}
is
just an
{\bf R}
-module.

Proof.

Let
{\mathbb{M}}_{\ast}

be any
\aleph_{1}
-saturated model of

T
of size
\leq\mu
and set

{\mathcal{K}}:=\{{\mathbb{N}}:{\mathbb{N}}\mbox{ is an ${\bf R}$--module such that
}{\mathbb{M}}_{\ast}\prec_{{\mathcal{L}}(\tau_{\bf R})}{\mathbb{M}}_{\ast}\oplus{\mathbb{N}}\mbox{ and }2^{\|{\mathbb{N}}\|}\leq\mu\}.

It is easily seen that
\mathcal{K}
is as required. Since this has no role in the paper, we leave the routine details to the reader.
∎

The following easy lemma plays an essential role in the sequel:

Lemma 5.32
.

Let
{\mathfrak{m}}
be a
\lambda
-context and

{\bf S}
be a free
{\bf T}
-module with a base
\{c^{\ast}_{\beta}:\beta<\alpha\}
.
Let
\bar{\varphi}
be very nice,

\varphi_{n}=(\exists y_{0},\ldots,y_{k_{n}-1})\varphi^{\prime}_{n}
where

\varphi^{\prime}_{n}=\bigwedge\limits^{m_{n}-1}_{\ell=0}[a^{n}_{\ell}x-\sum\limits_{i=0}^{k_{n}-1}b^{n}_{\ell,i}y_{i}].

Let
{\mathfrak{e}}={\mathfrak{e}}({\bar{\varphi}})\in{\mathfrak{E}}^{\mathfrak{m}}
and
{\mathbb{N}}_{n}={\mathbb{N}}^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n}
(see Definition
5.6
). The following assertions hold:

(1)

Let
{\mathbb{N}}_{n,0}
be the
{\bf R}
-submodule of

{\mathbb{N}}_{n}
generated by
\{x,y_{i}:i<k_{m_{n}-1}\}
.
Then
{\mathbb{N}}_{n}
is the direct sum
\sum\limits_{\beta<\alpha}{\mathbb{N}}_{n,\beta}
and
{\mathbb{N}}_{n,0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle h_{\beta}}}{{\cong}}{\mathbb{N}}_{n,\beta}
, as

{\bf R}
-modules, where
{\mathbb{N}}_{n,\beta}

is the
{\bf R}
-module generated by
\{xc^{\ast}_{\beta}\}\cup\{y_{i}c^{\ast}_{\beta}:i<k_{m_{n}-1}\}
freely except the
equations
\varphi^{\prime}_{n}
and
h_{0}
is the identity.

(2)

As a
{\bf T}
-module,
\varphi_{n}({\mathbb{N}}_{n})/\varphi_{\omega}({\mathbb{N}}_{n})=\sum\limits_{\beta<\alpha}\varphi_{n}({\mathbb{N}}_{n,\beta})/\varphi_{\omega}({\mathbb{N}}_{n,\beta})
.

(3)

For any
z\in{\mathbb{L}}^{{\mathfrak{e}}}_{n}
, there
are
z_{\beta}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{n,0}\cap{\mathbb{L}}^{{\mathfrak{e}}}_{n}\cap\varphi^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n}({\mathbb{N}}_{n,0})

such that
z=\sum\limits_{\beta<\alpha}h_{\beta}(z_{\beta})
. Also,

{\bf h}_{z}^{\mathfrak{e},n}=\sum\limits_{\beta<\alpha}{\bf h}_{h_{\beta}(z_{\beta})}^{\mathfrak{e},n}
. In particular,
z
is
n
-nice if and only if each
z_{\beta}
is

n
-nice.

(4)

The rings
{\bf{de}}^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n}
and
{\bf S}

generate
{\bf{dE}}^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n}
. In fact each element of
{\bf{dE}}^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n}
has the form

\sum\limits_{\beta<\alpha}x_{\beta}b_{\beta}
, where
x_{\beta}\in{\bf{de}}^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n}
. Also we have

{\bf{dE}}^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n}={\bf{de}}^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n}\mathop{{\otimes}}\limits_{\bf T}{\bf S}
.

(5)

Let
\mathcal{I}_{n}
be a maximal ideal of
{\bf{de}}^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n}
.
Then
{\bf D}_{n}:={\bf{de}}^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n}/\mathcal{I}_{n}
is a field.

(6)

Let
{\bf T}^{\prime}:={\bf T}/(\mathcal{I}_{n}\cap{\bf T})
,

{\bf S}^{\prime}:={\bf S}/(\mathcal{I}_{n}\cap{\bf T})
and let
{\mathbb{M}}
be from a strongly semi-nice construction.
Then any set of equations on
{\bf S}
which has a solution in

{\rm End}_{\bf R}({\mathbb{M}})

has a solution in
{\bf D}_{n}\mathop{{\otimes}}\limits_{{\bf T}^{\prime}}{\bf S}^{\prime}
.

Proof.

(1)
. Since
{\bf S}
is free as a
{\bf T}
-module with the base
\{c^{\ast}_{\beta}:\beta<\alpha\}
, we have
{\bf S}=\bigoplus_{\beta<\alpha}{\bf T}c^{\ast}_{\beta}
.
Now, we apply this through the following natural identifications of
{\bf R}
-modules:

\begin{array}[]{ll}{\bf R}x{\bf S}\oplus\bigoplus_{i<k_{m}-1}{\bf R}y_{i}{\bf S}&=\bigoplus_{\beta<\alpha}{\bf R}xc^{\ast}_{\beta}\oplus\bigoplus_{i<k_{m}-1}\bigoplus_{\beta<\alpha}{\bf R}y_{i}c^{\ast}_{\beta}\\
&=\bigoplus_{\beta<\alpha}\left({\bf R}xc^{\ast}_{\beta}\oplus\bigoplus_{i<k_{m}-1}{\bf R}y_{i}c^{\ast}_{\beta}\right).\end{array}

It turns out from the previous displayed identification that

{\mathbb{N}}_{n}\cong\bigoplus_{\beta<\alpha}{\mathbb{N}}_{n,\beta},

as an
{\bf R}
-module.
Also for any
y\in{\mathbb{N}}_{n,0}
, we set
h_{\beta}(y):=yc_{\beta}
. This yields an
{\bf R}
-module isomorphism
{\mathbb{N}}_{n,0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle h_{\beta}}}{{\cong}}{\mathbb{N}}_{n,\beta}
.

(2)
. We apply Lemma

3.19

along with (1) to conclude that
\varphi_{n}({\mathbb{N}}_{n})=\bigoplus_{\beta<\alpha}\varphi_{n}({\mathbb{N}}_{n,\beta})
. Consequently,
\varphi_{n}({\mathbb{N}}_{n})/\varphi_{\omega}({\mathbb{N}}_{n})=\bigoplus_{\beta<\alpha}\varphi_{n}({\mathbb{N}}_{n,\beta})/\varphi_{\omega}({\mathbb{N}}_{n,\beta})
.

(3)
. Let
z\in{\mathbb{L}}^{{\mathfrak{e}}}_{n}
. Then,

z\in\varphi_{n}({\mathbb{N}}_{n})=\bigoplus_{\beta<\alpha}\varphi^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n}({\mathbb{N}}_{n,\beta})\cong\bigoplus_{\beta<\alpha}h_{\beta}^{\prime\prime}(\varphi^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n}({\mathbb{N}}_{n,0})).

Let
z_{\beta}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{n,0}
be such that

z=\sum\limits_{\beta<\alpha}h_{\beta}(z_{\beta})
. It is evident that
z_{\beta}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{n,0}\cap{\mathbb{L}}^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n}\cap\varphi^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n}({\mathbb{N}}_{n,0})
.

Now suppose that
{\bf g}:{\mathbb{N}}_{n}\to{\mathbb{M}}
is a bimodule homomorphism. Then

\begin{array}[]{ll}{\bf h}_{z}^{\mathfrak{e},n}({\bf g}(x_{n})+\varphi_{\omega}({\mathbb{M}}))&={\bf g}(z)+\varphi_{\omega}({\mathbb{M}})\\
&=\left(\sum\limits_{\beta<\alpha}{\bf g}(h_{\beta}(z_{\beta}))\right)+\varphi_{\omega}({\mathbb{M}})\\
&=\sum\limits_{\beta<\alpha}\left({\bf g}(h_{\beta}(z_{\beta}))+\varphi_{\omega}({\mathbb{M}})\right)\\
&=\sum\limits_{\beta<\alpha}{\bf h}_{h_{\beta}(z_{\beta})}^{\mathfrak{e},n}({\bf g}(x_{n})+\varphi_{\omega}({\mathbb{M}})).\end{array}

It follows that
{\bf h}_{z}^{\mathfrak{e},n}=\sum\limits_{\beta<\alpha}{\bf h}_{h_{\beta}(z_{\beta})}^{\mathfrak{e},n}
.

(4)
. Let
x\in{\bf{de}}^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n}
and
s\in{\bf S}
. Then
xs\in{\bf{dE}}^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n}
. This implies
the existence of a
{\bf T}
-linear map
{\bf{de}}^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n}\times{\bf S}\to{\bf{dE}}^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n}
.
By the universal property of tensor products,
there is a map
f:{\bf{de}}^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n}\otimes_{{\bf T}}{\bf S}\to{\bf{dE}}^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n}
.
Now, let
z\in{\bf{dE}}^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n}
. By clause (3), there are

z_{\beta}\in{\bf{de}}^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n}
and
s_{\beta}\in{\bf S}
such that

z=\sum\limits_{\beta}z_{\beta}s_{\beta}
. Moreover,
by its proof, we know that such a presentation is unique.
This shows that
f
is an isomorphism. Up to this identification,

{\bf{dE}}^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n}={\bf{de}}^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n}\mathop{{\otimes}}\limits_{\bf T}{\bf S}
.
Thanks to Lemma
5.24
we know the rings
{\bf{de}}^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n}
and
{\bf S}
commute with each other.

(5)
. As
\mathcal{I}_{n}
is a maximal ideal, it is clear that
{\bf D}_{n}
is a division ring. Since the ring
{\bf{de}}^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n}
is commutative (see Lemma
5.24
(2)) we deduce that
{\bf D}_{n}
is a field.

(6)
. Let

(\ast)_{1}

\sum_{j}s_{ij}X_{i}^{j}=0,

be a system of polynomial
equations with parameters
s_{ij}\in S
and indeterminates
\{X_{i}\}
. Suppose
these equations have a solution
{\mathbf{f}}\in{\rm End}({\mathbb{M}})
. This means that

(\ast)_{2}

\sum_{j}s_{ij}{\mathbf{f}}^{j}=0.

There are

z_{n}({\mathbf{f}})\in{\mathbb{L}}^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n}
and
\alpha_{n}({\mathbf{f}})<\lambda
such that

({\mathord{\mathrm{Pr}}}1)^{n}_{\alpha_{n}({\mathbf{f}}),z_{n}({\mathbf{f}})}[{\mathbf{f}},{\mathfrak{e}}]
.
Let

f_{n}:=\hat{{\bf f}}_{n}
.
Then

(\ast)_{3}

\sum_{j}s_{ij}f^{j}_{n}=0.

Recall from Lemma
5.25
(v) that the natural
mapping
\varrho_{n}:\hat{{\mathbf{f}}}_{n}\mapsto{\bf h}^{n}_{z_{n}({\mathbf{f}})}

is a homomorphism from

\begin{array}[]{lr}\{\hat{{\mathbf{f}}}_{n}:&{\mathbf{f}}\in{\rm End}({\mathbb{M}})\mbox{ and
}({\mathord{\mathrm{Pr}}}1)^{n}_{\alpha_{n}({\mathbf{f}}),z_{n}({\mathbf{f}})}\mbox{ holds for some }\\
&\alpha_{n}({\mathbf{f}})<\lambda,\ z_{n}({\mathbf{f}})\in{\mathbb{L}}^{\mathord{\mathrm{tr}}}_{n}\}\end{array}

into
{\bf{dE}}^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n}
with kernel included in

{\rm End}^{\mathfrak{e},n}_{<\lambda}({\mathbb{M}})
.
Let

\pi:{\rm End}^{{\mathfrak{e}},n}({\mathbb{M}})\to\frac{{\rm End}^{{\mathfrak{e}},n}({\mathbb{M}})}{{\rm End}^{{\mathfrak{e}},n}_{<\lambda}({\mathbb{M}})}

be the canonical map and let
g_{n}=\pi({\bf h}^{n}_{z_{n}({\mathbf{f}})})
.
Applying
\pi\circ\varrho_{n}
to both sides of
(\ast)_{3}
, we get

(\ast)_{4}

\sum_{j}s_{ij}g^{j}_{n}=0.

Since there is an embedding

\rho_{n}:\frac{{\rm End}^{{\mathfrak{e}},n}({\mathbb{M}})}{{\rm End}^{{\mathfrak{e}},n}_{<\lambda}({\mathbb{M}})}\hookrightarrow{\bf{dE}}^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n},

by setting
e_{n}:=\rho_{n}(g_{n}),
we have

(\ast)_{5}

\sum_{j}s_{ij}e^{j}_{n}=0
.

In view of (4) we see that
{\bf{dE}}^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n}={\bf{de}}^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n}\mathop{{\otimes}}\limits_{\bf T}{\bf S}
.
Let

\sigma:{\bf{de}}^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n}\mathop{{\otimes}}\limits_{\bf T}{\bf S}\to{\bf D}_{n}\mathop{{\otimes}}\limits_{{\bf T}^{\prime}}{\bf S}^{\prime}

be the natural map induced by
{\bf{de}}^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n}\twoheadrightarrow{\bf D}_{n}
and
{\bf S}\twoheadrightarrow{\bf S}^{\prime}
.
Set
t_{n}:=\sigma(e_{n})
. By applying
\sigma
to
(\ast)_{5}

we have

(\ast)_{6}

\sum_{j}s_{ij}t^{j}_{n}=0
.

This essentially says that the polynomial equations from
(\ast)_{1}
with parameters in
S

have a solution in
{\bf D}_{n}\mathop{{\otimes}}\limits_{{\bf T}^{\prime}}{\bf S}^{\prime}
as well.
This is what we want to prove.
∎

In what follows we will use the following two consequences
of Lemma
5.32
:

Corollary 5.33
.

Suppose that the following three items holds:

(a)

{\bf R}
is a ring which is not pure semisimple and let
{\bf T}
be the subring of
{\bf R}
generated by
1
,
i.e.,
{\bf T}\cong{\mathbb{Z}}/n{\mathbb{Z}}
, where
n:=\operatorname{char}({\bf R})
which is not
necessarily prime.

(b)

{\bf S}
is a ring containing
{\bf T}
such that
({\bf S},+)
is a free

{\bf T}
-module and suppose that for every
s\in{\bf S}\setminus\{0_{\bf S}\}

for some
{\mathbb{N}}\in{\mathcal{K}}\cup\{{\mathbb{M}}_{*}\}
we have

{\mathbb{N}}s\not=\{0_{\mathbb{N}}\}
, and

(c)

\lambda=\mathop{\mathrm{cf}}(\lambda)>||{\bf R}||+||{\bf S}||+\aleph_{0}

and
\alpha<\lambda\Rightarrow|\alpha|^{\aleph_{0}}<\lambda
.

Then we can find an
{\bf R}
-module
{\mathbb{M}}
of cardinality
\lambda
, and a
homomorphism
\bf{h}
from
{\bf S}
into
{\rm End}({\mathbb{M}})
such that:

(d)

\mathop{\mathrm{Ker}}({\bf h})=\{0\}
.

(e)

If
\Sigma
is a set of equations with parameters in

{\bf S}
such that
{\bf h}(\Sigma)
is solvable in

{\rm End}_{\bf R}({\mathbb{M}})
, then

\Sigma
is solvable in
{\bf D}\otimes{\bf S}
for some field
{\bf D}
.

(f)

If
s\in{\bf S}\setminus\{0_{{\bf S}}\}
and

{\mathbb{N}}\in{\mathcal{K}}
is such that
{\mathbb{N}}s\not=\{0_{\mathbb{N}}\}
, then
the image of
{\mathbb{M}}
under

{\bf h}(s)
has cardinality
\lambda
.

Corollary 5.34
.

Suppose
{\bf S}
is a ring extending
\mathbb{Z}
such that
({\bf S},+)
is free,
and let
{\bf R}
be a ring which is not pure semisimple. Let
{\bf D}
be a
field such that
p:=\operatorname{char}({\bf D})|\operatorname{char}({\bf R})
and set

{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}:={\mathbb{Z}}/p{\mathbb{Z}}
if
p>0
,
and
{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}:={\mathbb{Z}}
otherwise.
Let
\Sigma
be a set of
equations over
{\bf S}
which is not solvable in
{\bf D}\mathop{{\otimes}}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}}({\bf S}/p{\bf S})
. Then for
{\mathbb{M}}
which is strongly nicely constructed,

\Sigma
is not
solvable in
{\rm End}({\mathbb{M}})
.

Recall that a module is
\aleph_{0}
-free if each of its finitely
generated submodules are free. This yields the following statement:

Remark 5.35
.

In Corollary
5.34
, if
({\bf S},+)
is an
\aleph_{0}
-free

{\bf T}
-module, the similar conclusions are hold.

6.
All things together: Kaplansky test problems

We are now ready to answer Kaplansky test problems.

Notation 6.1
.

For the rest of this section we assume that

{\bf R}
is a ring which is not pure semisimple.

Theorem 6.2
.

Let
{\mathfrak{m}}
be a non trivial
\lambda
-context with

{\bf S}={\bf T}=\langle 1_{\bf R}\rangle_{\bf R}
where

\lambda=\mathop{\mathrm{cf}}(\lambda)>|\alpha|^{\aleph_{0}}+||{\mathfrak{m}}||

for
\alpha<\lambda
. Then there are

{\bf R}
-modules
{\mathbb{M}}
,

{\mathbb{M}}_{1}
and
{\mathbb{M}}_{2}
of cardinality
\lambda
such that:

(1)

{\mathbb{M}}\oplus{\mathbb{M}}_{1}\cong{\mathbb{M}}\oplus{\mathbb{M}}_{2}
, and

{\mathbb{M}}_{1}\not\cong{\mathbb{M}}_{2}
.

(2)

{\mathbb{M}}_{1}
,
{\mathbb{M}}_{2}
,
{\mathbb{M}}_{1}\oplus{\mathbb{M}}
and
{\mathbb{M}}_{2}\oplus{\mathbb{M}}
are
\leq_{\aleph_{0}}
–extensions of

{\mathbb{M}}^{\ast}
.

(3)

{\mathbb{M}}_{1}\equiv_{\mathcal{L}_{\infty,\lambda}}{\mathbb{M}}_{2}
.

Remark 6.3
.

Adopt the notation of Theorem
6.2
.

(a)

Note that (1) becomes trivial if we remove the “of cardinality
\lambda
”. To see this, take

{\mathbb{M}}
,

{\mathbb{M}}_{1}
and
{\mathbb{M}}_{2}
to be free
{\bf R}
-modules with

\|{\mathbb{M}}\|>\|{\mathbb{M}}_{1}\|>\|{\mathbb{M}}_{2}\|\geq|{\bf R}|+\aleph_{0}.

(b)

Recall that
{\mathbb{M}}_{1}\equiv_{\mathcal{L}_{\infty,\lambda}}{\mathbb{M}}_{2}
means for every sentence
\sigma\in\mathcal{L}_{\infty,\lambda},

{\mathbb{M}}_{1}\models\sigma\iff{\mathbb{M}}_{2}\models\sigma.

Proof.

(1)
. Let
{\bf T}
be the subring of
{\bf R}

which 1 (the unit) generates.

Step A)
: Here, we introduce the auxiliary ring
{\bf S}
:

Let
{\bf S}:=\frac{{\bf T}\langle{\mathcal{X}},{\mathcal{W}}_{1},{\mathcal{Y}},{\mathcal{W}}_{2}\rangle}{I}
where
{\bf T}\langle{\mathcal{X}},{\mathcal{W}}_{1},{\mathcal{Y}},{\mathcal{W}}_{2}\rangle
is the skew polynomial ring in noncommuting variables
\{{\mathcal{X}},{\mathcal{W}}_{1},{\mathcal{Y}},{\mathcal{W}}_{2}\}
with coefficients in the commutative ring
{\bf T}
, and
I
is its two-sided ideal
generated by:

(\ast)
:

{\mathcal{X}}{\mathcal{X}}={\mathcal{X}}
,

{\mathcal{Y}}{\mathcal{Y}}={\mathcal{Y}}
,

{\mathcal{X}}{\mathcal{W}}_{1}{\mathcal{W}}_{2}={\mathcal{X}}
,

{\mathcal{Y}}{\mathcal{W}}_{2}{\mathcal{W}}_{1}={\mathcal{Y}}
,

{\mathcal{X}}{\mathcal{W}}_{1}{\mathcal{Y}}={\mathcal{X}}{\mathcal{W}}_{1}
,

(1-{\mathcal{X}})(1-{\mathcal{Y}})=1-{\mathcal{X}}
,

{\mathcal{Y}}{\mathcal{X}}={\mathcal{Y}}
,

{\mathcal{Y}}{\mathcal{W}}_{2}{\mathcal{X}}={\mathcal{Y}}{\mathcal{W}}_{2}
.

In other words,
{\bf S}
is the ring generated by
{\bf T}\cup\{{\mathcal{X}},{\mathcal{W}}_{1},{\mathcal{Y}},{\mathcal{W}}_{2}\}
extending
{\bf T}
freely except equations
(\ast)

(to understand these equations see the definition of
{\mathbb{M}}^{\otimes}

as a bimodule below).

Step B)
: Let
\alpha<\beta<\gamma
be
additively indecomposable ordinals
18
18

18

Recall that an ordinal
\gamma
is additively indecomposable if for all ordinals

\alpha,\beta<\gamma
we have
\alpha+\beta<\gamma.
and let
{\mathbb{M}}
be an
{\bf R}
-module. We define a new
bimodule
{\mathbb{M}}^{\otimes}
related to
{\mathbb{M}}
and ordinals
\alpha,\beta,\gamma
.

For
i<\gamma
, let
{\mathbb{M}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle h_{i}}}{{\cong}}{\mathbb{M}}^{\otimes}_{i}
(where
{\mathbb{M}}_{i}^{\otimes}
is an

{\bf R}
-module) and set
{\mathbb{M}}^{\otimes}:=\mathop{{\bigoplus}}\limits_{i<\gamma}{\mathbb{M}}_{i}^{\otimes}
.
We expand
{\mathbb{M}}^{\otimes}
to an
({\bf R},{\bf S})
-bimodule. To this end, we take
x\in{\mathbb{M}}^{\otimes}_{i}
.
Due to the axioms of bimodules, it is enough to define
\{h_{i}(x){\mathcal{X}},h_{i}(x){\mathcal{Y}},h_{i}(x){\mathcal{W}}_{1},h_{i}(x){\mathcal{W}}_{2}\}
. We define these via the following rules:

h_{i}(x){\mathcal{X}}:=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}h_{i}(x)&\qquad i\geq\alpha,\\
0&\qquad i<\alpha,\end{array}\right.

h_{i}(x){\mathcal{Y}}:=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}h_{i}(x)&\qquad i\geq\beta,\\
0&\qquad i<\beta,\end{array}\right.

h_{i}(x){\mathcal{W}}_{1}:=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}h_{j}(x)&\mbox{ if for some }\epsilon,\ i=\alpha+\epsilon<\gamma,\ j=\beta+\epsilon<\gamma,\\
0&\mbox{ otherwise,}\end{array}\right.

and

h_{i}(x){\mathcal{W}}_{2}:=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}h_{j}(x)&\mbox{ if for some }\epsilon,\ i=\beta+\epsilon<\gamma,\ j=\alpha+\epsilon<\gamma,\\
0&\mbox{ otherwise.}\end{array}\right.

Let
\bar{\mathbb{M}}=\langle{\mathbb{M}}_{\alpha}:\alpha\leq\kappa\rangle
be a strongly semi-nice
construction. Recall that semi-nice
construction is a consequence of Section 3, and its strong form
was constructed in Section 4.

Let

{\mathbb{P}}:={\mathbb{M}}_{\kappa}
and let

{}_{\bf R}{\mathbb{P}}
be
{\mathbb{P}}
as an
{\bf R}
-module.

Step C)
: Here, we define the

{\bf R}
-modules
{\mathbb{M}}
,

{\mathbb{M}}_{1}
and
{\mathbb{M}}_{2}
of cardinality
\lambda
such that

{\mathbb{M}}\oplus{\mathbb{M}}_{1}\cong{\mathbb{M}}\oplus{\mathbb{M}}_{2}
.

To this end, recall from the second step that every element of
{\bf S}
may be considered as an
endomorphism of
{}_{\bf R}{\mathbb{P}}
.
Set
{}_{\bf R}{\mathbb{M}}^{1}:=({}_{\bf R}{\mathbb{P}}){\mathcal{X}}
and
{}_{\bf R}{\mathbb{M}}_{1}:=({}_{\bf R}{\mathbb{P}})(1-{\mathcal{X}})
.
We conclude from the formula
{\mathcal{X}}{\mathcal{X}}-{\mathcal{X}}=0

that
{}_{\bf R}{\mathbb{M}}^{1}\cap{}_{\bf R}{\mathbb{M}}_{1}=0
. Let us use from the formula

{\mathcal{X}}(1-{\mathcal{X}})=1
that

{}_{\bf R}{\mathbb{P}}={}_{\bf R}{\mathbb{P}}({\mathcal{X}}+(1-{\mathcal{X}}))={}_{\bf R}{\mathbb{P}}{\mathcal{X}}+(1-{\mathcal{X}}){}_{\bf R}{\mathbb{P}}={}_{\bf R}{\mathbb{M}}^{1}+{}_{\bf R}{\mathbb{M}}_{1}={}_{\bf R}{\mathbb{M}}^{1}\oplus{}_{\bf R}{\mathbb{M}}_{1}.

Let
{}_{\bf R}{\mathbb{M}}^{2}:=({}_{\bf R}{\mathbb{P}}){\mathcal{Y}}
and
{}_{\bf R}{\mathbb{M}}_{2}:=({}_{\bf R}{\mathbb{P}})(1-{\mathcal{Y}})
.
In the same vein, the above formulas lead us to the following decomposition

{}_{\bf R}{\mathbb{P}}={}_{\bf R}{\mathbb{M}}^{2}\oplus{}_{\bf R}{\mathbb{M}}_{2}.

In view of the equation
{\mathcal{X}}{\mathcal{W}}_{1}{\mathcal{Y}}={\mathcal{X}}{\mathcal{W}}_{1}
, we have

{\mathbb{M}}^{1}{\mathcal{W}}_{1}={}_{\bf R}{\mathbb{P}}{\mathcal{X}}{\mathcal{W}}_{1}={}_{\bf R}{\mathbb{P}}{\mathcal{X}}{\mathcal{W}}_{1}{\mathcal{Y}}\subset{}_{\bf R}{\mathbb{P}}{\mathcal{Y}}={\mathbb{M}}^{2}.

This yields a homomorphism from
{\mathbb{M}}^{1}
to
{\mathbb{M}}^{2}
, defined by the help of the following assignment

a\mapsto a{\mathcal{W}}_{1}.

Similarly,
{\mathcal{W}}_{2}
provides a
homomorphism from
{\mathbb{M}}^{2}
onto
{\mathbb{M}}^{1}
, defined via

b\mapsto b{\mathcal{W}}_{2}.

Thanks to the equations
{\mathcal{Y}}{\mathcal{W}}_{2}{\mathcal{W}}_{1}={\mathcal{Y}}
and
{\mathcal{Y}}{\mathcal{X}}={\mathcal{Y}}
,
it is easily seen that the multiplication maps by
{\mathcal{W}}_{1}
and
{\mathcal{W}}_{2}

are inverse to each other. This provides an
isomorphism from
{\mathbb{M}}^{1}
onto
{\mathbb{M}}^{2}
, so let
{}_{\bf R}{\mathbb{M}}:={}_{\bf R}{\mathbb{M}}^{1}\cong{}_{\bf R}{\mathbb{M}}^{2}
.

Step D)
: One has
{}_{\bf R}{\mathbb{M}}_{1}\not\cong{}_{\bf R}{\mathbb{M}}_{2}
.

Assume towards contradiction that
{}_{\bf R}{\mathbb{M}}_{1}\cong{}_{\bf R}{\mathbb{M}}_{2}
. Thus there are
f_{1}:{}_{\bf R}{\mathbb{M}}_{1}\to{}_{\bf R}{\mathbb{M}}_{2}

and
f_{2}:{}_{\bf R}{\mathbb{M}}_{2}\to{}_{\bf R}{\mathbb{M}}_{1}
such that
f_{1}f_{2}=1
and
f_{2}f_{1}=1
.
Recall that
{}_{\bf R}{\mathbb{P}}={}_{\bf R}{\mathbb{M}}^{1}\oplus{}_{\bf R}{\mathbb{M}}_{1}={}_{\bf R}{\mathbb{M}}^{2}\oplus{}_{\bf R}{\mathbb{M}}_{2}
. Define
{\mathcal{Z}}_{1}\in{\rm End}_{\bf R}({}_{\bf R}{\mathbb{P}})
by applying the following assignment

(a,b)\in{}_{\bf R}{\mathbb{M}}^{1}\oplus{}_{\bf R}{\mathbb{M}}_{1}\mapsto(a{\mathcal{W}}_{1},f_{1}(b))\in{}_{\bf R}{\mathbb{M}}^{2}\oplus{}_{\bf R}{\mathbb{M}}_{2}.

In the same vein, define
{\mathcal{Z}}_{2}\in{\rm End}_{\bf R}({}_{\bf R}{\mathbb{P}})
via

(a,b)\in{}_{\bf R}{\mathbb{M}}^{2}\oplus{}_{\bf R}{\mathbb{M}}_{2}\mapsto(a{\mathcal{W}}_{2},f_{2}(b))\in{}_{\bf R}{\mathbb{M}}^{1}\oplus{}_{\bf R}{\mathbb{M}}_{1}.

Clearly,
{\mathcal{Z}}_{1}{\mathcal{Z}}_{2}={\mathcal{Z}}_{2}{\mathcal{Z}}_{1}=1=\mathop{\mathrm{id}}_{\mathbb{P}}
.
It is also easy to check that:

\begin{array}[]{ll}{\mathcal{X}}{\mathcal{Z}}_{1}={\mathcal{X}}{\mathcal{Z}}_{1}{\mathcal{Y}},\\
(1-{\mathcal{X}}){\mathcal{Z}}_{1}=(1-{\mathcal{X}}){\mathcal{Z}}_{1}(1-{\mathcal{Y}}),\\
{\mathcal{Y}}{\mathcal{Z}}_{2}={\mathcal{Y}}{\mathcal{Z}}_{2}{\mathcal{X}},\\
(1-{\mathcal{Y}}){\mathcal{Z}}_{2}=(1-{\mathcal{Y}}){\mathcal{Z}}_{2}(1-{\mathcal{X}}).\end{array}

We use just one very simple
non trivial
{\mathfrak{e}}\in{{\mathfrak{E}}}^{{\mathfrak{m}}}
. According to Lemma
4.17
, there are

n(\ast)<\omega
,
z_{1}
and
z_{2}\in{\mathbb{L}}^{{\mathfrak{e}},\ast}_{n(\ast)}

such that the
equations above hold in the endomorphism ring of Abelian group

\varphi^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n(\ast)}({\mathbb{M}})/\varphi^{\mathfrak{e}}_{\omega}({\mathbb{M}})

for any
bimodule
{\mathbb{M}}
,
19
19

19

Recall from Lemma
5.8
that
\varphi^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n}({\mathbb{M}})\equiv\psi^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n}({\mathbb{M}})
holds for all
n<\omega.
when we replace
{\mathcal{Z}}_{1}
(resp.
{\mathcal{Z}}_{2}
)
by
{\bf h}^{{\mathfrak{e}},n(\ast)}_{{\mathbb{M}},z_{1}}
(resp.

{\bf h}^{{\mathfrak{e}},n(\ast)}_{{\mathbb{M}},z_{2}}
) and interpret

{\mathcal{X}},{\mathcal{Y}},{\mathcal{W}}_{1},{\mathcal{W}}_{2}\in{\bf S}
naturally. This holds in particular
for the bimodule
{\mathbb{M}}^{\otimes}

we defined in Step B).
So, the following equations hold:

\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathcal{X}{\bf h}^{{\mathfrak{e}},n}_{{\mathbb{M}}^{\otimes},z_{1}}=\mathcal{X}{\bf h}^{{\mathfrak{e}},n}_{{\mathbb{M}}^{\otimes},z_{1}}\mathcal{Y},\\
(1-\mathcal{X}){\bf h}^{{\mathfrak{e}},n}_{{\mathbb{M}}^{\otimes},z_{1}}=(1-\mathcal{X}){\bf h}^{{\mathfrak{e}},n}_{{\mathbb{M}}^{\otimes},z_{1}}(1-\mathcal{Y}),\\
\mathcal{Y}{\bf h}^{{\mathfrak{e}},n}_{{\mathbb{M}}^{\otimes},z_{2}}=\mathcal{Y}{\bf h}^{{\mathfrak{e}},n}_{{\mathbb{M}}^{\otimes},z_{2}}\mathcal{X},\\
(1-\mathcal{Y}){\bf h}^{{\mathfrak{e}},n}_{{\mathbb{M}}^{\otimes},z_{2}}=(1-\mathcal{Y}){\bf h}^{{\mathfrak{e}},n}_{{\mathbb{M}}^{\otimes},z_{2}}(1-\mathcal{X}).\end{array}

These equations in turn define certain decompositions of
\varphi^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n}({\mathbb{M}}^{\otimes})/\varphi^{\mathfrak{e}}_{\omega}({\mathbb{M}}^{\otimes})
which yield to the following isomorphism

\frac{\varphi^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n}\bigl(\sum_{i<\beta}{\mathbb{M}}^{\otimes}_{i}\bigl)}{\varphi^{\mathfrak{e}}_{\omega}\bigl(\sum_{i<\beta}{\mathbb{M}}^{\otimes}_{i}\bigr)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longrightarrow}}\frac{\varphi^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n}\bigl(\sum_{i<\alpha}{\mathbb{M}}^{\otimes}_{i}\bigl)}{\varphi^{\mathfrak{e}}_{\omega}\bigl(\sum_{i<\alpha}{\mathbb{M}}^{\otimes}_{i}\bigr)}.

The cardinality of left (resp. right) hand side is
|\beta|
(resp.
|\alpha|
). Thus if we choose
|\beta|>|\alpha|
, we get a contradiction that we searched for it.

(2)
. This is in the proof of
(1)
.

(3)
. This follows from (2) and Lemma
3.18
.
∎

We now prove the existence of
{\bf R}
-modules with the Corner pathology. We present such a thing by applying Corollary
5.33
.

Theorem 6.4
.

Let
m(\ast)>2
and let
\lambda>|{\bf R}|
be a cardinal of the form

\lambda=\left(\mu^{\aleph_{0}}\right)^{+}
. Then there is an
{\bf R}
-module
{\mathbb{M}}
of cardinality

\lambda

such that:

{\mathbb{M}}^{n}\cong{\mathbb{M}}\quad\emph{ifandonlyif}\quad m(\ast)-1\mbox{ divides }n-1.

Proof.

We divide the proof into nine steps.

Step A)
We first introduce
rings
{\bf S}_{0}
and
{\bf S}
. The ring
{\bf S}_{0}
is incredibly easy compared to
{\bf S}

and
{\bf S}
is essentially the ring of endomorphisms we would like.

To this end, let
{\bf T}
be the subring of

{\bf R}
which 1
generates. Let
{\bf S}_{0}
be the ring extending
{\bf T}

generated by
\{{\mathcal{X}}_{0},\ldots,{\mathcal{X}}_{m(\ast)-1},{\mathcal{W}},{\mathcal{Z}}\}
freely except the equations:

(\ast)_{1}
:

{\mathcal{X}}^{2}_{\ell}={\mathcal{X}}_{\ell}
,

{\mathcal{X}}_{\ell}{\mathcal{X}}_{m}=0
(
\ell\neq m
),

1={\mathcal{X}}_{0}+\ldots+{\mathcal{X}}_{m(\ast)-1}
,

{\mathcal{X}}_{\ell}{\mathcal{W}}{\mathcal{X}}_{m}=0
for
\ell+1\neq m\ \mod\ m(\ast)
,

{\mathcal{W}}^{m(\ast)}=1
,

{\mathcal{Z}}^{2}=1
,

{\mathcal{X}}_{0}{\mathcal{Z}}(1-X_{0})={\mathcal{X}}_{0}{\mathcal{Z}}
,

(1-{\mathcal{X}}_{0}){\mathcal{Z}}{\mathcal{X}}_{0}=(1-{\mathcal{X}}_{0}){\mathcal{Z}}
.

The meaning of these equations will become clear when we use them.

Similarly, we define
{\bf S}
, but in addition we require
\sigma=0
, where
\sigma
is a term in the language of rings,
when
{}_{\bf D}{\mathbb{M}}^{\ast}\sigma=0

for every field
\bf D
and where
{}_{\bf D}{\mathbb{M}}^{*}
is the
({\bf D},{\bf S}_{0})
-
bimodule as
defined below. Now “
{\bf S}

is a free
{\bf T}
-module” will be proved later.

For an integer
m
, the notation
[-\infty,m)
stands for
\{n:n\mbox{ is an integer }<m\}
and if

\eta\in{}^{[-\infty,m)}\omega
, then we set
\eta\restriction k:=\eta\restriction[-\infty,\mathop{\mathrm{min}}\{m,k\})
.

We look at the following sets:

\begin{array}[]{lll}W_{0}:=&\big\{\eta:&\eta\mbox{ is a function with domain of the form }[-\infty,n)\\
&&\mbox{and range }\subseteq\{1,\ldots,m(\ast)-1\},\mbox{ and such that}\\
&&\mbox{for every small enough }m\in{\mathbb{Z}},\ \eta(m)=1\big\},\end{array}

and
W_{1}:=W_{0}\times\{0,\ldots,m(\ast)-1\}
.

Let
\bf D
be a field such that if
{\bf T}
is finite and of cardinality
n
, then

\operatorname{char}({\bf D})
divides
n
.
So
{\bf D}\otimes{\bf S}
is the ring extending
{\bf D}
by adding

{\bf D},{\mathcal{X}}_{0},\ldots,{\mathcal{X}}_{m(\ast)-1},{\mathcal{W}},{\mathcal{Z}}

as non commuting variables freely except satisfying the equation in
(\ast)_{1}

and if
||{\bf T}||
is finite we divide
{\bf S}
by
p{\bf S}
where
p:=\operatorname{char}({\bf D})
.
So there is a homomorphism
g_{\bf D}
from
{\bf S}
to
{\bf D}\otimes{\bf S}

such that
\{0_{{\bf S}}\}=\cap\{\mathop{\mathrm{Ker}}(g_{\bf D}):{\bf D}\emph{asabove}\}
.

Let
{\mathbb{M}}^{\ast}={}_{\bf D}{\mathbb{M}}^{\ast}
be the left

\bf D
-module freely generated by

\{x_{\eta,\ell}:\eta\in W_{0},\ \ell<m(\ast)\}.

We make
{}_{\bf D}{\mathbb{M}}^{\ast}
to a right

({\bf D}\mathop{{\otimes}}{\bf S}_{0})
-module by defining
xz
for
x\in{}_{{\bf D}}{\mathbb{M}}^{*}
and
z\in{\bf S}_{0}
. It is enough to deal with

z\in\{{\mathcal{X}}_{m}:m<m(*)\}\cup\{{\mathcal{Z}},{\mathcal{W}}\}
. Let
x=\sum\limits_{\eta,\ell}a_{\eta,\ell}x_{\eta,\ell}
where

(\eta,\ell)
vary on

W_{0}
,
a_{\eta,\ell}\in{\bf D}
and
\{(\eta,\ell):a_{\eta,\ell}\neq 0\}

is finite. We define

(\sum\limits_{\eta,\ell}a_{\eta,\ell}x_{\eta,\ell})z:=\sum\limits_{\eta,\ell}a_{\eta,\ell}(x_{\eta,\ell}z).

Here, we define the action
of
\{{\mathcal{X}}_{m},{\mathcal{Z}},{\mathcal{W}}\}
on
x_{\eta,\ell}
as follows:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}x_{\eta,\ell}{\mathcal{X}}_{m}&:=&\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}x_{\eta,\ell}&\mbox{if }\ell=m,\\
0&\mbox{if }\ell\neq m,\end{array}\right.\\
\\
x_{\eta,\ell}{\mathcal{Z}}&:=&\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}x_{\eta{}^{\frown}\!\langle\ell\rangle,0}&\mbox{if }\ell>0,\\
x_{\eta\restriction[-\infty,n-1),\eta(n-1)}&\mbox{if }\ell=0,\mbox{
and }(-\infty,n)=\mathop{\mathrm{Dom}}(\eta),\end{array}\right.\\
\\
and\\
x_{\eta,\ell}{\mathcal{W}}&:=&x_{\eta,m}\ \mbox{ when }m=\ell+1\ \mod\ m(\ast).\end{array}

We get a
({\bf D},{\bf S})
-bimodule as the identities in the
definition of
{\bf S}_{0}
and
{\bf S}

holds. If
{\bf D}={\bf T}
, some by inspection (those of
(\ast)_{1}
), the rest by the
choice of
{\bf S}
. If
{\bf D}\neq{\bf T}
, by the restriction on
{\bf D}
.
Let

{}_{\bf D}{\mathbb{M}}^{\ast}_{\ell}:=\{\sum\limits_{\eta}d_{\eta,\ell}x_{\eta,\ell}:\ \eta\in W_{0}\mbox{ and }d_{\eta,\ell}\in{\bf D}\}.

So clearly
{}_{\bf D}{\mathbb{M}}^{\ast}=\mathop{{\bigoplus}}\limits_{\ell=0}^{m(\ast)-1}{}_{\bf D}{\mathbb{M}}^{\ast}_{\ell}
.

Step B)
In this step we introduce an
{\bf R}
-module
{\mathbb{M}}
such that

{\mathbb{M}}^{m(\ast)-1}\cong{\mathbb{M}}
.

Let
{\mathbb{P}}
be the
({\bf R},{\bf S})
-bimodule constructed in
Lemma
4.7
for
\lambda
. Here, we used the existence of seminice construction and its strong version.

We look at
{\mathbb{P}}_{\ell}:={\mathbb{P}}{\mathcal{X}}_{\ell}
. Let
i\neq j
. We use the formula
{\mathcal{X}}_{i}{\mathcal{X}}_{j}=0
to observe that
{\mathbb{P}}_{i}\cap{\mathbb{P}}_{j}=0
.
Since

\sum\limits^{m(\ast)-1}_{\ell=0}{\mathcal{X}}_{\ell}=1
we have

{}_{{\bf R}}{\mathbb{P}}=\sum\limits^{m(\ast)-1}_{\ell=0}{\mathbb{P}}{\mathcal{X}}_{\ell}=\mathop{{\bigoplus}}\limits^{m(\ast)-1}_{\ell=0}{}_{{\bf R}}{\mathbb{P}}_{\ell}.

Suppose
\ell+1\neq m\ \mod\ m(\ast)
. We combine the formula
{\mathcal{X}}_{\ell}{\mathcal{W}}{\mathcal{X}}_{m}=0
with the formula
\sum\limits^{m(\ast)-1}_{\ell=0}{\mathcal{X}}_{\ell}=1
to observe that

{\mathbb{P}}_{\ell}{\mathcal{W}}={\mathbb{P}}{\mathcal{X}}_{\ell}{\mathcal{W}}({\mathcal{X}}_{0}+\ldots+{\mathcal{X}}_{m(\ast)})={\mathbb{P}}_{\ell}{\mathcal{W}}{\mathcal{X}}_{\ell+1}\subset{\mathbb{P}}_{\ell+1},

i.e.,
{\mathcal{W}}:{}_{\bf R}{\mathbb{P}}_{\ell}\twoheadrightarrow{}_{\bf R}{\mathbb{P}}_{\ell+1}
is surjective.
Here, we use the relation
{\mathcal{W}}^{m(\ast)}=1
to equip
{\mathcal{W}}
as an embedding from

{\mathbb{P}}
onto
{\mathbb{P}}
. Hence,
{\mathcal{W}}\restriction{}_{\bf R}{\mathbb{P}}_{\ell}

is an isomorphism
from
{}_{\bf R}{\mathbb{P}}_{\ell}
onto
{}_{\bf R}{\mathbb{P}}_{\ell+1}
.
So

{}_{\bf R}{\mathbb{P}}_{m(\ast)-1}\cong\ldots\cong{}_{\bf R}{\mathbb{P}}_{1}\cong{}_{\bf R}{\mathbb{P}}_{0}.

Thanks to the formulas
{\mathcal{X}}_{0}{\mathcal{Z}}(1-X_{0})={\mathcal{X}}_{0}{\mathcal{Z}}
and
\sum\limits^{m(\ast)-1}_{\ell=0}{\mathcal{X}}_{\ell}=1
we conclude that

{\mathcal{Z}}
maps
{}_{\bf R}{\mathbb{P}}_{0}
into

{\mathbb{P}}{\mathcal{X}}_{0}{\mathcal{Z}}={\mathbb{P}}{\mathcal{X}}_{0}{\mathcal{Z}}(1-X_{0})={\mathbb{P}}{\mathcal{X}}_{0}{\mathcal{Z}}({\mathcal{X}}_{1}+\ldots+{\mathcal{X}}_{m(\ast)-1})\subset\mathop{{\bigoplus}}\limits^{m(\ast)-1}_{\ell=1}{\mathbb{P}}{\mathcal{X}}_{\ell}=\mathop{{\bigoplus}}\limits^{m(\ast)-1}_{\ell=1}{\mathbb{P}}_{\ell}\cong{\mathbb{P}}_{0}.

By the same vein,
{\mathcal{Z}}
maps
\mathop{{\bigoplus}}\limits^{m(\ast)-1}_{\ell=1}{\mathbb{P}}_{\ell}
into
{}_{\bf R}{\mathbb{P}}_{0}
. We are going to use the formula
{\mathcal{Z}}^{2}=1
to exemplifies
{\mathcal{Z}}
with the following isomorphism

\mathop{{\bigoplus}}\limits^{m(\ast)-1}_{\ell=1}{}_{\bf R}{\mathbb{P}}_{\ell}\cong{}_{\bf R}{\mathbb{P}}_{0}.

But, we have just shown

\mathop{{\bigoplus}}\limits^{m(\ast)-1}_{\ell=1}{}_{\bf R}{\mathbb{P}}_{\ell}\cong({}_{\bf R}{\mathbb{P}}_{0})^{m(\ast)-1}.

This completes the proof
of Step B).

So, it is enough to show

(*)_{2}
:

1<k<m(\ast)-1\quad\Rightarrow\quad{}_{\bf R}{\mathbb{P}}^{k}_{0}\not\cong{}_{{\bf R}}{\mathbb{P}}_{0}
.

Assume
k
is a counterexample. The desired contradiction will be presented in
Step I), see below. To this end we need some preliminaries.

Step C)
There exist a field
{\bf D}
and
{\mathcal{Y}}\in{\bf D}\mathop{{\otimes}}\limits_{\bf T}{\bf S}
satisfying the
following equations:

(*)_{3}
:

{\mathcal{Y}}\restriction{}_{\bf D}{\mathbb{M}}^{\ast}_{0}
is an
isomorphism from

{}_{\bf D}{\mathbb{M}}^{\ast}_{0}
onto
\mathop{{\bigoplus}}\limits_{\ell=1}^{k}{}_{\bf D}{\mathbb{M}}^{\ast}_{\ell}
,

{\mathcal{Y}}\restriction\mathop{{\bigoplus}}\limits_{\ell=1}^{k}{}_{\bf D}{\mathbb{M}}^{\ast}_{\ell}
is an
isomorphism from
\mathop{{\bigoplus}}\limits_{\ell=1}^{k}{}_{\bf D}{\mathbb{M}}^{\ast}_{\ell}
onto
{}_{\bf D}{\mathbb{M}}^{\ast}_{0}
,

{\mathcal{Y}}\restriction\mathop{{\bigoplus}}\limits^{m(\ast)}_{\ell=k+1}{}_{\bf D}{\mathbb{M}}^{\ast}_{\ell}
is the identity, and

{\mathcal{Y}}^{2}=1
.

Indeed, recall from
{}_{\bf R}{\mathbb{P}}^{k}_{0}\cong{}_{{\bf R}}{\mathbb{P}}_{0}
that
{}_{\bf R}{\mathbb{P}}
is equipped with an
endomorphism
{\mathbf{f}}
such that:

(*)_{4}
:

{\mathbf{f}}\restriction{}_{\bf R}{\mathbb{P}}_{0}
is an
isomorphism from

{}_{\bf R}{\mathbb{P}}_{0}
onto
{}_{\bf R}{\mathbb{P}}_{1}\oplus\ldots\oplus{}_{\bf R}{\mathbb{P}}_{k}
,

{\mathbf{f}}\restriction(_{\bf R}{\mathbb{P}}_{1}\oplus\ldots\oplus{}_{\bf R}{\mathbb{P}}_{k})
is an
isomorphism from
{}_{\bf R}{\mathbb{P}}_{1}\oplus\ldots\oplus{}_{\bf R}{\mathbb{P}}_{k}
onto
{}_{\bf R}{\mathbb{P}}_{0}
,

{\mathbf{f}}\restriction{}_{\bf R}{\mathbb{P}}_{j}
, for
k<j<m(\ast)
, is the identity, and

{\mathbf{f}}^{2}=1
.

Assuming
({\bf S},+)
is a free
{\bf T}
-module, according to Corollary
5.33
, there is a
field
\bf D
with the property that
p:=\operatorname{char}({\bf D})
divides
n:=\operatorname{char}({\bf R})
,
when
n>0
, and there exists
{\mathcal{Y}}\in{\bf D}\mathop{{\otimes}}\limits_{\bf T}{\bf S}
satisfying the
the desired equations.
This completes the proof of Step C).

In sum, we are left to show that
({\bf S},+)
is a free
{\bf T}
-module.
Note that each of
\{1_{S},{\mathcal{X}}_{0},\ldots,{\mathcal{X}}_{m(\ast)},{\mathcal{W}},{\mathcal{Z}\}}

map each generator
x_{\eta,\ell}

to another generator or zero, so this applies to any composition of
them, in fact, in quite specific way for
\rho
,
\nu\in\{1,\ldots,m(\ast)-1\}^{<\omega}
,
k
,
m<\omega
.
Let

{\mathcal{Y}}^{k,m}_{\rho,\nu}
be a
monomial operator in the generators of
{\bf S}
such that:

x_{\eta,\ell}{\mathcal{Y}}^{k,m}_{\rho,\nu}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}x_{\eta_{1}{}^{\frown}\!\nu,m}&\mbox{ if }\ell=k,\mbox{ and }\eta=\eta_{1}{}^{\frown}\!\rho\mbox{ for some }\eta_{1}\in W_{0}\\
0&\mbox{ otherwise.}\end{array}\right.

In order to define these operators, first we define
Y^{k,k}_{\rho,\langle\rangle}
and
{\mathcal{Y}}^{k,k}_{\langle\rangle,\nu}

by induction on
\mathop{\rm\ell g}(\rho)
and
\mathop{\rm\ell g}(\nu)
respectively. Also, negative powers of
{\mathcal{W}}

can be defined, because
{\mathcal{W}}
is invertible. Now,
we let

{\mathcal{Y}}^{k,m}_{\rho,\nu}:=Y^{k,k}_{\rho,\langle\rangle}{\mathcal{Y}}_{\langle\rangle,\nu}^{k,k}{\mathcal{W}}^{m-k}.

Step D)
: Set
\Omega=\{{\mathcal{Y}}^{k,m}_{\rho,\nu}:\ \rho,\nu\in[1,m(\ast))^{<\omega},\ k,m\in[0,m(\ast))
and if

\mathop{\rm\ell g}(\rho)>0
and
\mathop{\rm\ell g}(\nu)>0
, then
\rho(0)=\nu(0)]\}.
Then

\boxtimes_{1}

\Omega
generates
{\bf S}
as a
{\bf T}
-module

First note that if
{\mathcal{Y}}^{k,m}_{\rho_{1},\nu_{1}}
is not in the family,
then for some
(\varrho,\rho,\nu)

we have
Y^{k,m}_{\rho,\nu}

belongs to the family
and
(\rho,\nu)=(\varrho^{\frown}\rho_{1},\varrho{}^{\frown}\!\nu_{1})
. Hence

{\mathcal{Y}}^{k,m}_{\rho,\nu}={\mathcal{Y}}^{k,m}_{\rho_{1},\nu_{1}}
. So,
\Omega
generates all of
{\mathcal{Y}}^{k,m}_{\rho,\nu}
’s and thus we can use them.

Let
{\bf S}^{\prime}
be the sub-
{\bf T}
-module of
{\bf S}
generated by

{\bf T}
and the family above.
Now
{\bf T}\subseteq{\bf S}^{\prime}
because

(\dagger)_{1}

1=\sum\{Y_{\langle\rangle,\langle\rangle}^{m,m}:m=0,\ldots,m(*)-1\}.

To see this, we evaluate both sides at
x_{\eta,\ell}
.
The right hand side of
(\dagger)_{1}

is equal to

\begin{array}[]{ll}x_{\eta,\ell}\sum\{Y_{\langle\rangle,\langle\rangle}^{m,m}:m=0,\ldots,m(*)-1\}&=x_{\eta^{\frown}\langle\rangle,\ell}\sum\{Y_{\langle\rangle,\langle\rangle}^{m,m}:m=0,\ldots,m(*)-1\}\\
&=x_{\eta^{\frown}\langle\rangle,\ell}Y_{\langle\rangle,\langle\rangle}^{\ell,\ell}\\
&=x_{\eta^{\frown}\langle\rangle,\ell}\\
&=x_{\eta,\ell}.\end{array}

Since the left hand side of
(\dagger)_{1}
is the identity, we get the desired equality.

Next we show

{\mathcal{X}}_{m}\in{\bf S}^{\prime}
for each
m<m(*)
. It suffices to show that

(\dagger)_{2}

{\mathcal{X}}_{m}={\mathcal{Y}}^{m,m}_{\langle\rangle,\langle\rangle}.

In order to see
(\dagger)_{2}
, we evaluate both sides at
x_{\eta,\ell}
.
Let
n=\mathop{\rm\ell g}(\eta)+1
. First, assume that
\ell=m
. Then, the right hand side of
(\dagger)_{2}

is equal to

x_{\eta,\ell}{\mathcal{Y}}^{m,m}_{\langle\rangle,\langle\rangle}=x_{\eta,m}{\mathcal{Y}}^{m,m}_{\langle\rangle,\langle\rangle}=x_{\eta,m},

which is equal to
x_{\eta,\ell}{\mathcal{X}}_{m}
. Now, we show the claim when
\ell\neq m
. In this case both sides of
(\dagger)_{2}
are equal to zero, e.g.,
(\dagger)_{2}
is valid.

In order to show
{\mathcal{W}}\in{\bf S}^{\prime}
we bring the following claim:

(\dagger)_{3}

{\mathcal{W}}=\sum\{{\mathcal{Y}}^{\ell,m}_{\langle\rangle,\langle\rangle}:\ell=m+1\mod\ m(*)\}.

As before, it is enough to evaluate both sides of
(\dagger)_{3}
at
x_{\eta,\ell}
.
Let
m
be such that
\ell\equiv_{m(\ast)}m+1
.
The right hand side of
(\dagger)_{3}

is equal to

\begin{array}[]{ll}x_{\eta,\ell}\sum\{{\mathcal{Y}}^{\ell,m}_{\langle\rangle,\langle\rangle}:\ell=m+1\mod\ m(*)\}&=x_{\eta,\ell}{\mathcal{Y}}^{\ell,m}\\
&=x_{\eta,m}.\end{array}

By definition, this is equal to the left hand side of
(\dagger)_{3}
.

Finally, we claim that
{\mathcal{Z}}\in{\bf S}^{\prime}
. Indeed, it suffices to prove that

(\dagger)_{4}

{\mathcal{Z}}=\{{\mathcal{Y}}^{0,m}_{\langle m\rangle,\langle\rangle}:m=1,\ldots,m(*)-1\}+\sum\{{\mathcal{Y}}^{m,0}_{\langle\rangle,\langle m\rangle}:m=1,\ldots,m(*)-1\}.

Again we evaluate both sides of
(\dagger)_{4}
at
x_{\eta,\ell}
.
Let
n=\mathop{\rm\ell g}(\eta)+1
and first suppose that
\ell=0.
Then, the right hand side of
(\dagger)_{4}

is equal to

\begin{array}[]{ll}x_{\eta,0}\{{\mathcal{Y}}^{0,m}_{\langle m\rangle,\langle\rangle}:m=1,\ldots,m(*)-1\}&+x_{\eta,0}\sum\{{\mathcal{Y}}^{m,0}_{\langle\rangle,\langle m\rangle}:m=1,\ldots,m(*)-1\}\\
&=x_{\eta,0}\{{\mathcal{Y}}^{0,m}_{\langle m\rangle,\langle\rangle}:m=1,\ldots,m(*)-1\}\\
&=x_{\eta{\restriction}{n-1}{}^{\frown}\!\langle\eta(n)\rangle,0}{\mathcal{Y}}^{0,\eta(n)}\\
&=x_{\eta{\restriction}{n-1}{}^{\frown}\!\langle\eta(n)\rangle,\eta(n)}.\end{array}

This is equal to the left hand side of
(\dagger)_{4}
. Suppose now that
\ell>0
.
Then the right hand side
is equal to
x_{\eta{}^{\frown}\!\langle\ell\rangle,0}
. By definition this is equal to the left hand side of
(\dagger)_{4}
.

So far, we have proved that the subring
{\bf S}^{\prime}
includes
{\bf T},{\mathcal{X}}_{m}(m<m(*)),{\mathcal{Z}}

and
{\mathcal{W}}
and is a sub
{\bf T}
-module. To finish this step, it suffices
to prove that
{\bf S}^{\prime}
is closed under product. For this, it is enough to prove
that
{\bf S}^{\prime}
includes the product
{\mathcal{Y}}^{k^{1},m^{1}}_{\rho_{1},\nu_{1}}{\mathcal{Y}}^{k^{2},m^{2}}_{\rho_{2},\nu_{2}}
of any two members of
\Omega
.

Now if
m^{1}\neq k^{2}
or no one of
\nu_{1},\rho_{2}
is an end-segment
of the other then this is not the case. So easily the product is

Y^{k^{1},m^{2}}_{\rho_{3},\nu_{3}}
where:

•

if
\nu_{1}=\rho_{2}
, then
(\rho_{3},\nu_{3})=(\rho_{1},\nu_{2})
,

•

if

\nu_{1}=(\varrho^{1})^{\frown}\rho_{2}
, then
(\rho_{3},\nu_{3})=(\rho_{1},(\varrho^{1})^{\frown}\nu_{2})
,

•

if
\rho_{2}=(\varrho^{1})^{\frown}\nu_{1}
, then
(\rho_{3},\nu_{3})=((\varrho^{1})^{\frown}\nu_{1},\nu_{2})
.

So
\boxtimes_{1}
holds, which completes the proof
of Step D).

Step E)
The set
\Omega
from Step D)
is a free basis of
{\bf S}
as a
{\bf T}
-module.

Assume that
{\mathcal{X}}=\sum\{a^{k,m}_{\nu,\rho}{\mathcal{Y}}^{k,m}_{\nu,\rho}:\rho,k,\nu,m\}=0
for some finite set

\{(\rho,\nu,k,m):a^{k,m}_{\nu,\rho}\neq 0\}
.
We have to show that
a^{k,m}_{\nu,\rho}=0
, for all such
\rho,k,\nu,m
.

Let
k^{*}<\omega
and

\rho^{*}\in\{0,\ldots,m(*)-1\}^{<\omega}
be such that
\mathop{\rm\ell g}(\rho^{*})>\mathop{\mathrm{sup}}\{\mathop{\rm\ell g}(\rho):a^{k,n}_{\rho,\nu}\neq 0\}
. We look at
x_{\rho^{*},k^{*}}{\mathcal{X}}
. Let
A(k^{*},\rho^{*})
be the set of all
\nu
in the finite sequence above where
\rho^{*}=\rho^{*}_{\nu}
{}^{\frown}\nu
,
for some
\rho^{*}_{\nu}.

Then

\begin{array}[]{ll}x_{\rho^{*},k^{*}}{\mathcal{X}}&=\sum\limits_{\nu,\rho,k,m}a^{k,m}_{\nu,\rho}(x_{\rho^{*},k^{*}}{\mathcal{Y}}^{k,m}_{\nu,\rho})\\
&=\sum\limits_{\nu,\rho,m}\{a^{k^{*},m}_{\nu,\rho}(x_{\rho^{*},k^{*}}{\mathcal{Y}}^{k^{*},m}_{\nu,\rho}):\nu\in A(k^{*},\rho^{*})\}\\
&=\sum\limits_{\nu,\rho,m}\{a^{k^{*},m}_{\nu,\rho}x_{(\rho^{*}_{\nu})^{\frown}\rho,m}:\nu\in A(k^{*},\rho^{*})\}\\
&=0.\end{array}

Recall that
{\mathbb{M}}^{\ast}
is the left
\bf D
-module freely generated by
x_{\eta,\ell}
’s, so as we are free in choosing
k^{*},\rho^{*},

we can easily show that
for any
tuple
(\rho,\nu,k,n)
in the finite set above, for some suitable choice of
\rho^{*}
,

a^{k,n}_{\rho,\nu}
is the only component of
x_{(\rho^{*}_{\nu})^{\frown}\rho,m}
in the sum above, and hence
a^{k,n}_{\rho,\nu}=0
. This completes the proof of Step E).

Before we continue, let us introduce some notations and definitions.
Clearly,
{\mathcal{Y}}
can be represented as a finite sum of the form:

{\mathcal{Y}}=\sum\{d^{k,m}_{\rho,\nu}{\mathcal{Y}}^{k,m}_{\rho,\nu}:k,m,\rho,\nu\}.

Choose
n(*)

large enough such that
n(*)>\mathop{\mathrm{max}}\{\mathop{\rm\ell g}(\rho),\mathop{\rm\ell g}(\nu):d^{k,m}_{\rho,\nu}\neq 0\}
.
For any
u\subseteq\{(\eta,\ell):\eta\in W_{0},\ \ell<m(\ast)\}
, let
{\mathbb{N}}_{u}
be
the sub
{\bf D}
-module of
{}_{\bf D}{\mathbb{M}}^{\ast}
generated by

\{x_{\eta,\ell}:(\eta,\ell)\in u\}
.

For
\eta\in w_{0}
and
\ell=1,\ldots,m(\ast)
set

{}_{\bf D}{\mathbb{M}}^{\ast}_{[1,k]}:=\mathop{{\bigoplus}}\limits^{k}_{j=1}{\mathbb{M}}^{\ast}_{\ell}
,
and let

\begin{array}[]{ll}w_{\eta,\ell}&:=\{(\nu,m)|(\nu,m)=(\eta,\ell)\mbox{ or }\eta^{\frown}\langle\ell\rangle\trianglelefteq\nu\mbox{ and }m<m(*)\},\\
u_{\ell}&:=\{(\eta,m)|m=\ell,\ \eta\in w_{0}\},\\
w^{m}_{\eta,\ell},&:=\ w_{\eta,\ell}\cap u_{m},\\
w^{[1,n]}_{\eta,\ell}&:=w_{\eta,\ell}\cap\bigcup\limits_{m\in[1,n]}u_{m}.\end{array}

Step F)

For any
\eta\in w_{0}
and
\ell<m(\ast)
, there is a finite subset
u\subseteq w^{0}_{\eta,\ell}
such that

(\alpha)

{\mathcal{Y}}
maps
{\mathbb{N}}_{w^{0}_{\eta,\ell}\setminus u}

into
{\mathbb{N}}_{w_{\eta,\ell}}

in fact into
{\mathbb{N}}_{w^{[1,k]}_{\eta,\ell}}

(\beta)

if
v
is finite and
u\subseteq v\subseteq w^{0}_{\eta,\ell}
then

{\mathbb{N}}_{w^{0}_{\eta,\ell}\setminus v}{\mathcal{Y}}
is a
{\bf D}
-vector subspace of
{\mathbb{N}}_{w^{0}_{\eta,\ell}}{\mathcal{Y}}

of cofinite dimension.

(\gamma)

for any finite subsets

v_{1}
,
v_{2}
of
w^{0}_{\eta,\ell}

extending
u
the following equalities are true:

\begin{array}[]{l}\dim({\mathbb{N}}_{w^{0}_{\eta,\ell}}/{\mathbb{N}}_{w^{0}_{\eta,\ell}\setminus v_{1}})-\dim({\mathbb{N}}_{w^{[1,k]}_{\eta,\ell}}/({\mathbb{N}}_{w^{0}_{\eta,\ell}\setminus v_{1}}{\mathcal{Y}}))\\
=\dim({\mathbb{N}}_{w^{0}_{\eta,\ell}}/{\mathbb{N}}_{w^{0}_{\eta,\ell}\setminus v_{2}})-\dim({\mathbb{N}}_{w^{[1,k]}_{\eta,\ell}}/({\mathbb{N}}_{w^{0}_{\eta,\ell}\setminus v_{2}}{\mathcal{Y}})),\end{array}

where the dimensions are computed as
{\bf D}
-vector spaces.

Indeed, the case
\ell=0
is easy.
Recalling the representation of
{\mathcal{Y}}
and the choice of

n(\ast)<\omega
, if we set

u=\{(\nu,m)\in w_{\eta,\ell}:\ |\mathop{\mathrm{Dom}}(\nu)\setminus\mathop{\mathrm{Dom}}(\eta)|<n(\ast)\},

then we have

(\nu,m)\in w_{\eta,\ell}\setminus u\quad\Rightarrow\quad x_{\nu,m}{\mathcal{Y}}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{w_{\eta,\ell}}.

Note that
u
is finite and clause
(\alpha)
holds.
As
{\mathcal{Y}}{\mathcal{Y}}=1

etc., we can show
that
(\beta)
holds. For checking the
equalities in clause
(\gamma)
, let
v_{3}:=v_{1}\cup v_{2}
. Due to the transitivity of
equality, it is enough to
prove the required equality for pairs
(v_{1},v_{3})
and
(v_{2},v_{3})
. This enable us
to assume
without loss of generality that
v_{1}\subseteq v_{2}
. Also, we stipulate
v_{0}=\emptyset
.

Now

{\mathbb{N}}_{w^{0}_{\eta,\ell}\setminus v_{2}}\subseteq{\mathbb{N}}_{w^{0}_{\eta,\ell}\setminus v_{1}}\subseteq{\mathbb{N}}_{w^{0}_{\eta,\ell}\setminus w_{0}}={\mathbb{N}}_{w^{0}_{\eta,\ell}},

and

\dim({\mathbb{N}}_{w^{0}_{\eta,\ell}}/{\mathbb{N}}_{w^{0}_{\eta,\ell}\setminus v_{i}})=|v_{i}|.

Manipulating the equalities in clause
(\alpha)
, they are equivalent to

(*)

\dim({\mathbb{N}}_{w^{0}_{\eta,\ell}}/{\mathbb{N}}_{w^{0}_{\eta,\ell}\setminus v_{1}})-\dim({\mathbb{N}}_{w^{0}_{\eta,\ell}}/{\mathbb{N}}_{w^{0}_{\eta,\ell}\setminus v_{2}})=

\dim({\mathbb{N}}_{w^{[1,k]}_{\eta,\ell}}/({\mathbb{N}}_{w^{0}_{\eta,\ell}\setminus v_{1}}{\mathcal{Y}}))-\dim({\mathbb{N}}_{w^{[1,k]}_{\eta,\ell}}/({\mathbb{N}}_{w^{0}_{\eta,\ell}\setminus v_{2}}{\mathcal{Y}})).

Since
{\mathbb{N}}_{w^{0}_{\eta,\ell}\setminus v_{2}}\subseteq{\mathbb{N}}_{w^{0}_{\eta,\ell}\setminus v_{1}}\subseteq{\mathbb{N}}_{w^{0}_{\eta,\ell}}
, the left hand side
of
(*)
is
equal to

\dim({\mathbb{N}}_{w^{0}_{\eta,\ell}\setminus v_{1}}/{\mathbb{N}}_{w^{0}_{\eta,\ell}\setminus v_{2}}).

Also,
{\mathbb{N}}_{w^{0}_{\eta,\ell}\setminus v_{2}}{\mathcal{Y}}\subseteq{\mathbb{N}}_{w^{0}_{\eta,\ell}\setminus v_{1}}{\mathcal{Y}}\subseteq{\mathbb{N}}_{w^{[1,k]}_{\eta,\ell}}

(the first inclusion holds
as
{\mathbb{N}}_{w^{0}_{\eta,\ell}\setminus v_{2}}\subseteq{\mathbb{N}}_{w^{0}_{\eta,\ell}\setminus v_{1}}
and the second one holds by clause
(\beta)
and
u\subseteq v_{1}\cap v_{2}
).
Hence the right hand side of

(\ast)
is equal to

\dim({\mathbb{N}}_{w^{0}_{\eta,\ell}\setminus v_{1}}{\mathcal{Y}}/{\mathbb{N}}_{w^{0}_{\eta,\ell}\setminus v_{2}}{\mathcal{Y}}).

Hence
(\ast)
is reduced in proving

(\ast)^{\prime}

\dim({\mathbb{N}}_{w^{0}_{\eta,\ell}\setminus v_{1}}/{\mathbb{N}}_{w^{0}_{\eta,\ell}\setminus v_{2}})=\dim({\mathbb{N}}_{w^{0}_{\eta,\ell}\setminus v_{1}}/({\mathbb{N}}_{w^{0}_{\eta,\ell}\setminus v_{2}}{\mathcal{Y}}))
.

Since
{\mathcal{Y}}
is an isomorphism from
{\mathbb{N}}_{w^{0}_{\eta,\ell}\setminus v_{2}}
onto
{\mathbb{N}}_{w^{0}_{\eta,\ell}\setminus v_{2}}{\mathcal{Y}}
,
it implies the validity of
(\ast)^{\prime}
.
This completes the proof.

Let
{\bf n}_{\eta,\ell}

be the natural number so
that for every large enough finite subset
v\subseteq w_{\eta,\ell}

{\bf n}_{\eta,\ell}:=\dim\left(\frac{{\mathbb{N}}_{w^{0}_{\eta,\ell}}}{{\mathbb{N}}_{w^{0}_{\eta,\ell}\setminus v}}\right)-\dim\left(\frac{{\mathbb{N}}_{w^{[1,k]}_{\eta,\ell}}}{{\mathbb{N}}_{w^{0}_{\eta,\ell}\setminus v}{\mathcal{Y}}}\right).

In view of Step F), this is a well-defined notion, which does not depend on the choice of
v
.

Step G)
If
\eta
,
\nu\in w_{0}
and
\ell\in\{0,1,\ldots,m(\ast)-1\}
, then
{\bf n}_{\eta,\ell}={\bf n}_{\nu,\ell}
.

To see this, we define a function
f:w_{\eta,\ell}\longrightarrow w_{\nu,\ell}
by

f(\eta{}^{\frown}\!\rho,m)=(\nu{}^{\frown}\!\rho,m)
for any

\rho\in\{0,\ldots,m(*)-1\}^{<\omega}
.
This function is one-to-one and onto,
and it induces an isomorphism from

{\mathbb{N}}_{w^{[0,m(\ast))}_{\eta,\ell}}
onto

{\mathbb{N}}_{w_{\nu,\ell}^{[0,m(\ast))}}
. It almost commutes with all of our
operations (the problems are
“near”
\eta
and
\nu
). So choose
v_{1}\subseteq w^{0}_{\eta,\ell}
large enough, as
required in the definition of
{\bf n}_{\eta,\ell}
,
{\bf n}_{\nu,\ell}

and to
make
{\mathbb{N}}_{w^{0}_{\eta,\ell}\setminus v_{1}}{\mathcal{Y}}\subseteq{\mathbb{N}}_{w^{[1,k]}_{\eta,\ell}}
, and let

v_{2}=f(v_{1})
. Now check the desired claim.

So, we shall write

{\bf n}_{\ell}
instead of
{\bf n}_{\nu,\ell}
. By Step G), this is well-defined.

Step H)
The following equations are valid:

{\bf n}_{\ell}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}0&\mbox{if }\ell\in[1,k]\\
{\bf n}_{0}+{\bf n}_{1}+\ldots+{\bf n}_{m(\ast)-1}&\mbox{if }\ell\in[k+1,m(\ast))\emph{or}\ell=0\end{array}\right.

To prove this, first note that
w_{\eta,\ell}=\{(\eta,\ell)\}\cup\bigcup\limits_{m<m(*)}w_{\eta{}^{\frown}\!\langle\ell\rangle,m}
. In order to apply Step F), we fix a pair
(\eta,\ell)

and we choose
a finite subset
v\subseteq w^{0}_{\eta,\ell}
large enough as there.
Let
(\eta{}^{\frown}\!\langle\ell\rangle,m)
be
such that

\{(\eta,\ell)\}\cup\{(\eta{}^{\frown}\!\langle\ell\rangle,m):\ m<m(\ast)\}\subseteq v.

Now we compute
{\bf n}_{\eta,\ell}
which is equal to
{\bf n}_{\ell}
,
and
{\bf n}_{\eta{}^{\frown}\!\langle\ell\rangle,m}
which is equal to
={\bf n}_{m}

for
m<m(*)
and we shall get the equality.

Now set

{\bf n}^{1}_{v,\eta,\ell}:=\dim\left(\frac{{\mathbb{N}}_{w^{0}_{\eta,\ell}}}{{\mathbb{N}}_{w^{0}_{\eta,\ell}\setminus v}}\right)-\sum\limits_{m<m(*)}\dim\left(\frac{{\mathbb{N}}_{w^{0}_{\eta{}^{\frown}\!\langle\ell\rangle,m}}}{{\mathbb{N}}_{w^{0}_{\eta{}^{\frown}\!\langle\ell\rangle,m}\setminus v}}\right).

By definition, we have
{\mathbb{N}}_{w^{0}_{\eta,\ell}}=\bigoplus\limits_{m<m(*)}{\mathbb{N}}_{w^{0}_{\eta{}^{\frown}\!\langle\ell\rangle,m}}\oplus{\bf D}x_{\eta,\ell}
. Using this equality, we can easily see that

(\otimes_{1})

{\bf n}^{1}_{v,\eta,\ell}
is equal to
1

when
\eta\in w_{0},\ell<m(\ast)

and
v\subseteq w_{0}
is finite large enough (we can replace
v
by

v\cap w^{0}_{\eta,\ell}
of course).

In particular, the following definition makes sense:

(\otimes_{2})

{\bf n}^{2}_{v,\eta,\ell}:=\dim\left(\frac{{\mathbb{N}}_{w^{[1,k]}_{\eta,\ell}}}{{\mathbb{N}}_{w^{[1,k]}_{\eta,\ell}\setminus v}}\right)-\sum\limits_{m<m(*)}\dim\left(\frac{{\mathbb{N}}_{w^{[1,k]}_{\eta{}^{\frown}\!\langle\ell\rangle,m}}}{{\mathbb{N}}_{w^{[1,k]}_{\eta{}^{\frown}\!\langle\ell\rangle,m}\setminus v}}\right).

Suppose
\eta\in w_{0},\ell<m(\ast)
and
v\subseteq w_{0}

is finite and large enough.
In view of definition, and as the argument of
(\otimes_{1})
we present the following three implications:

(\otimes_{2.1})\quad\ell=0\ \Rightarrow\ {\bf n}^{2}_{v,\eta,\ell}=0
,

(\otimes_{2.2})\quad\ell\in[1,k]\ \Rightarrow\ {\bf n}^{2}_{v,\eta,\ell}=1
,

(\otimes_{2.3})\quad\ell\in[k+1,m(*))\Rightarrow\ {\bf n}^{2}_{v,\eta,\ell}=0
.

Next, we show that

(\otimes_{3})

{\bf n}^{1}_{v,\eta,\ell}-{\bf n}^{2}_{v,\eta,\ell}={\bf n}_{\ell}-\sum\limits_{m<m(*)}{\bf n}_{m}
.

Indeed, we have

\begin{array}[]{ll}{\bf n}^{1}_{v,\eta,\ell}-{\bf n}^{2}_{\nu,\eta,\ell}&={\rm dim}(\frac{{\mathbb{N}}_{w^{0}_{\eta,\ell}}}{{\mathbb{N}}_{w^{0}_{\eta,\ell}\setminus v}})-{\rm dim}(\frac{{\mathbb{N}}_{w^{[1,k]}_{\eta,\ell}}}{w^{[1,k]}_{\eta,\ell}\setminus v})\\
&-\sum\limits_{m<n(*)}{\rm dim}(\frac{{\mathbb{N}}_{w^{0}_{\eta{}^{\frown}\!\langle\ell\rangle,m}}}{{\mathbb{N}}_{w^{0}_{\eta^{{}^{\frown}\!}{\langle\ell\rangle,m}}\smallsetminus v}})\\
&+\sum\limits_{m<n(*)}{\rm dim}(\frac{{\mathbb{N}}_{w^{0}_{\eta^{{}^{\frown}\!}{\langle\ell\rangle,m}}}}{{\mathbb{N}}_{w^{[1,k]}_{\eta^{{}^{\frown}\!}{\langle\ell\rangle,m}}\smallsetminus v}})\\
&={\bf n}_{\eta,\ell}-\sum\limits_{\ell<m(*)}{\bf n}_{\eta^{{}^{\frown}\!}{\langle\ell\rangle,m}}\\
&={\bf n}_{\ell}-\sum\limits_{m<m(*)}{\bf n}_{m}.\end{array}

Now we combine
(\otimes_{1})
,
(\otimes_{2.1})
and
(\otimes_{2.2})

along with
(\otimes_{2.3})
to deduce the following formula.

(\otimes_{4})

{\bf n}^{1}_{v,\eta,\ell}-{\bf n}^{2}_{v,\eta,\ell}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}0&\mbox{if }\ell\in[1,k]\\
1&\mbox{otherwise }\end{array}\right.

Step H) follows from
(\otimes_{3})+(\otimes_{4})
.

Step I)
In this step, we drive our desired contradiction.
To see this, recall from Step H) that

\begin{array}[]{ll}\sum\limits^{m(\ast)-1}_{\ell=0}{\bf n}_{\ell}&=({\bf n}_{0}+\ldots+{\bf n}_{m(*)-1})\\
&+\sum\limits^{k}_{\ell=1}({\bf n}_{0}+\ldots+{\bf n}_{m(\ast)-1}-1)+\sum\limits^{m(\ast)-1}_{\ell=k+1}({\bf n}_{0}+\ldots+{\bf n}_{m(\ast)-1})\\
&=m(\ast)\sum\limits^{m(\ast)-1}_{\ell=0}{\bf n}_{\ell}-k.\end{array}

So,

-(m(\ast)-1)\left(\sum^{m(\ast)-1}_{\ell=1}{\bf n}_{\ell}\right)=-k,

i.e.,

\sum^{m(\ast)-1}_{\ell=1}{\bf n}_{\ell}=\frac{k}{m(\ast)-1}.

Now, the left hand side is an integer, while as
1<k<m(\ast)-1
,
the right hand side is not an integer, a contradiction.

This completes the proof.
∎

An additional outcome is a slight improvement of the above pathological property.

Corollary 6.5
.

Assume
\lambda=(\mu^{\aleph_{0}})^{+}>|{\bf R}|
and let
0<m_{1}<m_{2}-1
. Then there is an
{\bf R}
-module
{\mathbb{M}}
of cardinality
\lambda
such
that:

{\mathbb{M}}^{n^{1}}\cong{\mathbb{M}}^{n^{2}}\mbox{ if and only if }m_{1}<n^{1}\ \&\ m_{1}\leq n^{2}\ \&\ [(m_{2}-m_{1})|(n^{1}-n^{2})]

(
|
means divides).

Proof.

Let
m(\ast):=m_{2}-m_{1}
and let
{\bf S}_{1}
be the ring constructed in the proof of
Theorem
6.4
with respect to
m(\ast)
. Let also
{\mathbb{M}}^{\ast}
be the

({\bf R},{\bf S}_{1})
-bimodule constructed there. In particular, it is equipped with a structure of bimodule over
({\bf R},{\bf S}_{1})
. Choose cardinals

\lambda_{m_{1}}>\lambda_{m_{1}-1}>\ldots>\lambda_{0}>\|{\mathbb{M}}^{*}\|+\|{\bf R}\|+\|{\bf S}_{1}\|+\aleph_{0}.

Let
I:=\bigcup\limits_{m\leq m_{1}}\prod\limits_{\ell=0}^{m-1}\lambda_{\ell}

and define a function

f:I\longrightarrow I
by the following rules:

f(\eta):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\eta\restriction k&\mbox{ if }\eta\in I,\mbox{ and }\mathop{\rm\ell g}(\eta)=k+1\\
\eta&\mbox{ if }\eta=\langle\rangle.\end{array}\right.

Let
{\mathbb{M}}^{\otimes}
be a bimodule which is

\mathop{\oplus}\limits_{\eta\in I}{\mathbb{M}}^{\otimes}_{\eta}
with
{\mathbb{M}}^{\otimes}_{\eta}\cong{\mathbb{M}}^{\ast}

for each
\eta\in I
and let
h_{\eta}:{\mathbb{M}}^{\ast}\longrightarrow{\mathbb{M}}^{\otimes}_{\eta}
be such an isomorphism.

For every endomorphism
{\mathcal{Y}}
of
{\mathbb{M}}^{\ast}

we define an endomorphism
{\mathcal{Y}}^{\otimes}\in{\rm End}_{{\bf R}}({\mathbb{M}}^{\otimes})

of
{\mathbb{M}}^{\otimes}
as an
{\bf R}
-module as follows. We are going to define the action of

{\mathcal{Y}}^{\otimes}
on each
{\mathbb{M}}^{\otimes}_{\eta}
(
\eta\in I
). To this end, we take
x\in{\mathbb{M}}^{\otimes}_{\eta}
and define the action via:

x{\mathcal{Y}}^{\otimes}:=h_{f(\eta)}\left((h^{-1}_{\eta}(x){\mathcal{Y}})\right).

Let
{\bf S}^{\otimes}
be the subring of the ring of endomorphisms of

{\mathbb{M}}^{\otimes}
as an
{\bf R}
-module generated by

\{1^{\otimes},{\mathcal{X}}^{\otimes}_{0},{\mathcal{X}}^{\otimes}_{1},\ldots,{\mathcal{X}}^{\otimes}_{m(\ast)},{\mathcal{W}}^{\otimes},{\mathcal{Z}}^{\otimes}\}.

Note also that
1^{\otimes}
is not a unit in
{\bf S}^{\otimes}
.
Now we continue as in the proof of Theorem
6.2
, with
{\mathbb{M}}^{\otimes}

here instead of
{\mathbb{M}}
there, and
{\bf S}^{\otimes}
here instead of
{\bf S}
there. We leave the details to the reader.
∎

Theorem 6.6
.

Let
\lambda=(\mu^{\aleph_{0}})^{+}>|{\bf R}|
be a regular cardinal.
Then there are
{\bf R}
-modules
{\mathbb{M}}_{1}

and
{\mathbb{M}}_{2}
of cardinality
\lambda
such that:

(1)

{\mathbb{M}}_{1}
,
{\mathbb{M}}_{2}
are not isomorphic,

(2)

{\mathbb{M}}_{1}
is isomorphic to a direct summand of
{\mathbb{M}}_{2}
,

(3)

{\mathbb{M}}_{2}
is isomorphic to a direct summand of
{\mathbb{M}}_{1}
.

Proof.

Let
{\bf T}
be the subring of
{\bf R}
which
1
generates.
As before, we need to choose a ring
{\bf S}

(essentially
the ring of
endomorphisms
we would like).

Step A)
: To make things easier, we first introduce
a ring
{\bf S}_{0}
which is easy compared to
{\bf S}
.

Let
A_{1}
(resp.
A_{-1})
be the set of even
(resp. odd) integers and let
f
be the following function:

f(i):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}i+1&\mbox{if }i\geq 0,\\
i-1&\mbox{if }i<0.\end{array}\right.

Thus,
f
maps
A_{1}
(resp.
A_{-1}
) into
A_{-1}
(resp.
A_{1}
). Also,
A_{1}\setminus\mathop{\mathrm{Rang}}(f\restriction A_{-1})=\{0\}
and
A_{-1}\setminus\mathop{\mathrm{Rang}}(f\restriction A_{1})=\{-1\}
. Let
i
vary on the
integers. Let
{\bf S}_{0}
be the ring extending
{\bf T}

generated freely by
\{{\mathcal{X}}_{1},{\mathcal{X}}_{-1},{\mathcal{W}}_{1},{\mathcal{W}}_{-1},{\mathcal{Z}}_{1},{\mathcal{Z}}_{-1}\}
. Let

{\bf D}
be a field such that if
\|{\bf T}\|
is finite, then the
characteristic of
{\bf D}
is finite and divides
\|{\bf T}\|
. Then, we set

{\bf S}_{\bf D}^{*}={\bf D}\mathop{{\otimes}}\limits_{\bf T}{\bf S}_{0}
(see Theorem
6.4
).

We are going to define a right
({\bf D}\mathop{{\otimes}}\limits_{\bf T}{\bf S}_{0})
–
module
{\mathbb{M}}^{\ast}_{{\bf D}^{\ast}}
.
We first define a left
{\bf D}
-module structure over
{\mathbb{M}}:=\sum\{{\bf D}x_{i}:i\in{\mathbb{Z}}\}
with

b(\sum\limits_{i}a_{i}x_{i})=\sum\limits_{i}(ba_{i})x_{i}
where
a_{i}
and
b
are in
{\bf D}
. As mentioned, we
like to make
{\mathbb{M}}
a right
({\bf D}\mathop{{\otimes}}\limits_{\bf T}{\bf S}_{0})
–module which
will be called
{\mathbb{M}}^{*}_{\bf D}
.
Let
x\in{\mathbb{M}}
and
c\in{\bf D}\mathop{{\otimes}}\limits_{\bf T}{\bf S}_{0}
.
To define
xc
, as

{\bf D}
and
{\bf S}_{0}
commute, it is enough to define
it for
x=x_{i}
and
c\in\{{\mathcal{X}}_{1},{\mathcal{X}}_{-1},{\mathcal{W}}_{1},{\mathcal{W}}_{-1},{\mathcal{Z}}_{1},{\mathcal{Z}}_{-1}\}
.
So let

x_{i}{\mathcal{X}}_{1}:=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}x_{i}&\mbox{if }i\in A_{1},\\
0&\mbox{if }i\in A_{-1},\end{array}\right.

x_{i}{\mathcal{X}}_{-1}:=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}0&\mbox{if }i\in A_{1},\\
x_{i}&\mbox{if }i\in A_{-1},\end{array}\right.

x_{i}{\mathcal{W}}_{1}:=x_{f(i)},

x_{i}{\mathcal{W}}_{-1}:=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}x_{f^{-1}(i)}&\mbox{if }i\in{\mathop{\mathrm{Rang}}(f)},\\
0&\mbox{otherwise,}\end{array}\right.

x_{i}{\mathcal{Z}}_{1}:=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}x_{i}&\mbox{if }i\in A_{1}\cap({\mathbb{Z}}\setminus\{0\}),\\
0&\mbox{otherwise,}\end{array}\right.

and

x_{i}{\mathcal{Z}}_{-1}:=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}x_{i}&\mbox{if }i\in A_{-1}\cap({\mathbb{Z}}\setminus\{0\}),\\
