0&\mbox{otherwise.}\end{array}\right.

These make
{\mathbb{M}}^{\ast}_{\bf D}
a right

{\bf S}^{*}_{\bf D}
–module. Let
g^{\ast}_{{\bf D}}
be the natural ring homomorphism from
{\bf S}_{0}
into
{\bf S}^{*}_{\bf D}
. By using

g^{\ast}_{{\bf D}}
, the
{\bf S}^{*}_{\bf D}
–module
{\mathbb{M}}^{\ast}_{\bf D}
becomes a right
{\bf S}_{0}
-module.

Step B)
: In this step we define the auxiliary ring
{\bf S}
.

Let
{\bf S}
be the ring
(with 1, associative
but not necessarily commutative) extending
{\bf T}

generated by
{\mathcal{X}}_{1}
,
{\mathcal{X}}_{-1}
,

{\mathcal{W}}_{1}
,
{\mathcal{W}}_{-1}
,
{\mathcal{Z}}_{1}
,
{\mathcal{Z}}_{-1}

freely except the following equations (to
understand them see below):

(\ast)

\sigma=0
if
\sigma
is a term
21
21

21

i.e. in the language
of rings, in the variables
{\mathcal{X}}_{1},{\mathcal{X}}_{-1},{\mathcal{W}}_{1},{\mathcal{W}}_{-1},{\mathcal{Z}}_{1},{\mathcal{Z}}_{-1}
,

{\mathbb{M}}^{\ast}_{\bf D}\sigma=0
for
{\mathbb{M}}^{\ast}_{\bf D}
as defined in Step A),
for every field
{\bf D}
such that if
{\bf T}
is of finite cardinality
n
, then

\operatorname{char}({\bf D})|n
.

Let us state some of the relations, which follow from
(\ast)
, and we will use them more in the rest of our argument.

(\star)_{1}
:

{\mathcal{X}}^{2}_{1}={\mathcal{X}}_{1}
,

{\mathcal{X}}^{2}_{-1}={\mathcal{X}}_{-1}
,

{\mathcal{X}}_{1}+{\mathcal{X}}_{-1}=1
,

{\mathcal{X}}_{1}{\mathcal{X}}_{-1}={\mathcal{X}}_{-1}{\mathcal{X}}_{1}=0

{\mathcal{Z}}^{2}_{1}={\mathcal{Z}}_{1}
,
{\mathcal{Z}}_{1}{\mathcal{X}}_{1}={\mathcal{Z}}_{1}={\mathcal{X}}_{1}{\mathcal{Z}}_{1}

{\mathcal{X}}_{-1}{\mathcal{W}}_{1}=({\mathcal{X}}_{-1}{\mathcal{W}}_{1}){\mathcal{X}}_{1}{\mathcal{Z}}_{1}

{\mathcal{X}}_{1}{\mathcal{Z}}_{1}{\mathcal{W}}_{-1}=({\mathcal{X}}_{1}{\mathcal{Z}}_{1}{\mathcal{W}}_{-1}){\mathcal{X}}_{-1}

{\mathcal{X}}_{-1}{\mathcal{W}}_{1}{\mathcal{W}}_{-1}={\mathcal{X}}_{-1}
and

{\mathcal{X}}_{1}{\mathcal{Z}}_{1}{\mathcal{W}}_{-1}{\mathcal{W}}_{1}={\mathcal{Z}}_{1}={\mathcal{X}}_{1}{\mathcal{Z}}_{1}
.

Let us show, for example, that

{\mathcal{X}}_{1}+{\mathcal{X}}_{-1}=1
. We need to show the left hand side is the identity
map when we consider it as an endomorphism of
{\mathbb{M}}^{\ast}_{\bf D}
. To
this end, we evaluate
{\mathcal{X}}_{1}+{\mathcal{X}}_{-1}
at any generator of
{\mathbb{M}}^{\ast}_{\bf D}
, say
x_{i}
. Recall that

x_{i}{\mathcal{X}}_{1}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}x_{i}&\mbox{if }i\in A_{1},\\
0&\mbox{if }i\in A_{-1},\end{array}\right.

x_{i}{\mathcal{X}}_{-1}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}0&\mbox{if }i\in A_{1},\\
x_{i}&\mbox{if }i\in A_{-1},\end{array}\right.

Thus,
x_{i}({\mathcal{X}}_{1}+{\mathcal{X}}_{-1})=x_{i}
, as claimed. The other relations will follow in the same way.

Step C)
: Here, we introduce the
{\bf R}
-modules
{\mathbb{M}}_{1}

and
{\mathbb{M}}_{2}
of cardinality
\lambda
such that:

(i)

{\mathbb{M}}_{1}
is isomorphic to a direct summand of
{\mathbb{M}}_{2}
and

(ii)

{\mathbb{M}}_{2}
is isomorphic to a direct summand of
{\mathbb{M}}_{1}
.

Let
\langle{\mathbb{M}}_{\alpha}:\alpha\leq\lambda\rangle
be a strongly semi-nice construction for

(\lambda,{{\mathfrak{m}}},S,\lambda)
and let
{\mathbb{M}}:={\mathbb{M}}_{\lambda}
. Recall that such a thing exists.
Let
{\mathbb{M}}_{1}:={\mathbb{M}}{\mathcal{X}}_{1}
and

{\mathbb{M}}_{-1}:={\mathbb{M}}{\mathcal{X}}_{-1}
.
Since
{\mathcal{X}}_{1}{\mathcal{X}}_{-1}={\mathcal{X}}_{-1}{\mathcal{X}}_{1}=0
, we have

{\mathbb{M}}_{1}\cap{\mathbb{M}}_{-1}=0
.
Thanks to the formula
{\mathcal{X}}_{1}+{\mathcal{X}}_{-1}=1
,

{\mathbb{M}}={\mathbb{M}}({\mathcal{X}}_{1}+{\mathcal{X}}_{-1})={\mathbb{M}}{\mathcal{X}}_{1}+{\mathbb{M}}{\mathcal{X}}_{-1}={\mathbb{M}}_{1}+{\mathbb{M}}_{-1}={\mathbb{M}}_{1}\oplus{\mathbb{M}}_{-1}.

Then

{\mathbb{M}}_{1}
and
{\mathbb{M}}_{-1}
are equipped with
{\bf R}
-module structure, and there is the identification
{\mathbb{M}}={\mathbb{M}}_{1}\oplus{\mathbb{M}}_{-1}
.
We
shall show that
{\mathbb{M}}_{1}
,
{\mathbb{M}}_{-1}
are as required in
Theorem
6.6
(with respect to
{\mathbb{M}}_{1}
and
{\mathbb{M}}_{2}
).

The relations
{\mathcal{Z}}^{2}_{1}={\mathcal{Z}}_{1}
and
{\mathcal{Z}}_{1}{\mathcal{X}}_{1}={\mathcal{Z}}_{1}={\mathcal{X}}_{1}{\mathcal{Z}}_{1}
imply that

{\mathbb{M}}_{1}={\mathbb{M}}_{1}(1-{\mathcal{Z}}_{1})\oplus{\mathbb{M}}_{1}{\mathcal{Z}}_{1},

i.e.,
{\mathbb{M}}_{1}{\mathcal{Z}}_{1}

is a direct summand of
{\mathbb{M}}_{1}
.
Since
{\mathcal{X}}_{-1}{\mathcal{W}}_{1}=({\mathcal{X}}_{-1}{\mathcal{W}}_{1}){\mathcal{X}}_{1}{\mathcal{Z}}_{1}
, we have

{\mathbb{M}}_{-1}{\mathcal{W}}_{1}={\mathbb{M}}{\mathcal{X}}_{-1}{\mathcal{W}}_{1}={\mathbb{M}}({\mathcal{X}}_{-1}{\mathcal{W}}_{1}){\mathcal{X}}_{1}{\mathcal{Z}}_{1}\subset{\mathbb{M}}{\mathcal{X}}_{1}{\mathcal{Z}}_{1}={\mathbb{M}}_{1}{\mathcal{Z}}_{1}.

Thus,

{\mathcal{W}}_{1}
maps
{\mathbb{M}}_{-1}

into
{\mathbb{M}}_{1}{\mathcal{Z}}_{1}
.
By using the formula
{\mathcal{X}}_{1}{\mathcal{Z}}_{1}{\mathcal{W}}_{-1}=({\mathcal{X}}_{1}{\mathcal{Z}}_{1}{\mathcal{W}}_{-1}){\mathcal{X}}_{-1}
we observe that

{\mathbb{M}}_{1}{\mathcal{Z}}_{1}{\mathcal{W}}_{-1}={\mathbb{M}}{\mathcal{X}}_{1}{\mathcal{Z}}_{1}{\mathcal{W}}_{-1}={\mathbb{M}}{\mathcal{X}}_{1}{\mathcal{Z}}_{1}{\mathcal{W}}_{-1}{\mathcal{X}}_{-1}\subset{\mathbb{M}}{\mathcal{X}}_{-1}={\mathbb{M}}_{-1}.

In other words,

{\mathcal{W}}_{-1}
maps
{\mathbb{M}}_{1}{\mathcal{Z}}_{1}

into
{\mathbb{M}}_{-1}
. We are going to combine the formula
{\mathcal{X}}_{-1}{\mathcal{W}}_{1}{\mathcal{W}}_{-1}={\mathcal{X}}_{-1}
along with

{\mathcal{X}}_{1}{\mathcal{Z}}_{1}{\mathcal{W}}_{-1}{\mathcal{W}}_{1}={\mathcal{Z}}_{1}={\mathcal{X}}_{1}{\mathcal{Z}}_{1}
to deduce that
the multiplication maps by
{\mathcal{W}}_{1}
and
{\mathcal{W}}_{-1}
are the inverse of each other. This implies that
{\mathbb{M}}_{-1}
is
isomorphic to a direct summand of
{\mathbb{M}}_{1}
(as left
{\bf R}
-modules).
Similarly, we obtain:

{\mathbb{M}}_{-1}={\mathbb{M}}_{-1}(1-{\mathcal{Z}}_{-1})\oplus{\mathbb{M}}_{-1}{\mathcal{Z}}_{-1}.

So,
{\mathbb{M}}_{-1}{\mathcal{Z}}_{-1}
is
a direct summand of
{\mathbb{M}}_{-1}
and
{\mathbb{M}}_{-1}{\mathcal{Z}}_{-1}

is isomorphic to
{\mathbb{M}}_{1}
. By the same argument,
{\mathbb{M}}_{1}
is isomorphic
to a direct summand of
{\mathbb{M}}_{2}
as left
{\bf R}
-modules. In summary, we showed that

\bullet

{\mathbb{M}}_{1}\cong{\mathbb{M}}_{1}(1-{\mathcal{Z}}_{1})\oplus{\mathbb{M}}_{-1}
and

\bullet

{\mathbb{M}}_{-1}\cong{\mathbb{M}}_{-1}(1-{\mathcal{Z}}_{-1})\oplus{\mathbb{M}}_{1}.

This completes the proof of Step C).

It remains to show that
{\mathbb{M}}_{1}\not\cong{\mathbb{M}}_{-1}
. Suppose on the contrary
that they are isomorphic, and we get a contradiction, which is presented at Step H) below.
Let
\mathfrak{e}\in{\mathfrak{E}}^{{\mathfrak{m}}}
be simple.

Step D)
: There is a solution
{\mathcal{Y}}\in{\bf{dE}}^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n}
to the following equations:

(\ast)_{2}
:

{\mathcal{X}}_{1}{\mathcal{Y}}{\mathcal{X}}_{-1}={\mathcal{X}}_{1}{\mathcal{Y}}
,

{\mathcal{X}}_{-1}{\mathcal{Y}}{\mathcal{X}}_{1}={\mathcal{X}}_{-1}{\mathcal{Y}}
,

{\mathcal{Y}}{\mathcal{Y}}=1
.

To see this, recall that
{\mathbb{M}}_{1}\cong{\mathbb{M}}_{-1}

and
{\mathbb{M}}={\mathbb{M}}_{1}\oplus{\mathbb{M}}_{-1}
.
Let
h
be an isomorphism from
{\mathbb{M}}_{1}
onto
{\mathbb{M}}_{-1}
.
Define
{\mathbf{f}}:{\mathbb{M}}\to{\mathbb{M}}
by

(a,b)\in{\mathbb{M}}={\mathbb{M}}_{1}\oplus{\mathbb{M}}_{-1}\implies{\mathbf{f}}(a,b)=(h^{-1}(b),h(a)).

So,

{\mathbf{f}}\in{\rm End}_{\bf R}({\mathbb{M}})
and it satisfies
{\mathbf{f}}\restriction{\mathbb{M}}_{1}=h
and
{\mathbf{f}}\restriction{\mathbb{M}}_{-1}=h^{-1}
. The member in
{\bf{dE}}^{\mathfrak{e}}_{n}
which
{\mathbf{f}}
induces solves the equations in
(\ast)_{2}
. This completes the proof of Step D).

In the light of Corollary
5.33
, it is enough to prove
the following two items:

(a)

in
{\bf D}\mathop{{\otimes}}\limits_{\bf T}{\bf S}_{0}
there is
no solution to

(\ast)_{2}
, in particular, there is no such
{\mathcal{Y}}
. Note that
{\bf S}^{*}_{{\bf D}}

have the same characteristic as
{\bf D}
.

(b)

{\bf S}_{0}
is a free
{\bf T}
–module.

Clearly
{\bf S}
is a
{\bf T}
-module, generated by the set of monomials in

\{{\mathcal{X}}_{1},{\mathcal{X}}_{-1},{\mathcal{W}}_{1},{\mathcal{W}}_{-1},{\mathcal{Z}}_{1},{\mathcal{Z}}_{-1}\}.

Our aim is to show that
{\bf S}
is a free
{\bf T}
-module;
in fact we shall exhibit explicitly
a free basis. For
\ell\in\{1,-1\}
,
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
,
n\geq 0
,
n\geq-k
, we define an endomorphism
{\mathbf{f}}^{\ell}_{k,n}

of
{\mathbb{M}}^{\ast}_{\bf D}
by

{\mathbf{f}}^{\ell}_{k,n}(x_{i}):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}x_{f^{k}(i)}&\mbox{ if }f^{-n}(i)\mbox{ is well defined and }x_{i}\in A_{\ell},\\
0&\mbox{{otherwise}}\end{array}\right.

It is easy to see that it is an endomorphism of

{\mathbb{M}}^{\ast}_{\bf D}
as a left
{\bf D}
-module. We will
define a monomial

{\mathcal{Y}}^{\ell}_{k,n}
, in the following way. For every monomial
\sigma

let
\sigma^{0}

be
1=\mathop{\mathrm{id}}_{{\mathbb{M}}^{\ast}_{\bf D}}
and remember
n\geq-k
so
n+k\geq 0
. Now set

{\mathcal{Y}}^{\ell}_{k,n}={\mathcal{X}}_{\ell}({\mathcal{W}}_{-1})^{n}{\mathcal{W}}^{n+k}_{1}.

It is easy to see that the operation of
{\mathcal{Y}}^{\ell}_{k,n}

on
{\mathbb{M}}^{\ast}_{\bf D}
by right multiplication,
is equal to
{\mathbf{f}}^{\ell}_{k,n}
.

Let

\mathcal{G}:=\{{\mathcal{Y}}^{\ell}_{k,n}:(\ell,k,n)\in w\}

where

w=\{(\ell,k,n):\ \ell\in\{1,-1\},\ k\in{\mathbb{Z}},\ n\geq 0,\ k+n\geq 0\}.

In the next step, we show that

\mathcal{G}

generates
{\bf S}
as a
{\bf T}
-module.

Step E)
: The set

\mathcal{G}

generates
{\bf S}
as a
{\bf T}
-module.

It is enough to show that for every monomial
\sigma
, some equation
\sigma=\sum a^{\ell}_{n,k}{\mathcal{Y}}^{\ell}_{k,n}
holds in
{\bf S}
,
where
\{(\ell,n,k):a^{\ell}_{n,k}\neq 0\}
is finite and
a^{\ell}_{k,n}\in{\bf T}
, i.e., it holds in the ring of
endomorphism of
{\mathbb{M}}^{\ast}_{\bf D}
. We prove this by induction on the length of the monomial
\sigma
.

If the length is zero,
\sigma
is 1. Recall from
(\star)_{1}
that
1={\mathcal{X}}_{1}+{\mathcal{X}}_{-1}
.
By definition,
{\mathcal{X}}_{\ell}={\mathcal{Y}}^{\ell}_{0,0}
; so
1={\mathcal{Y}}^{1}_{0,0}+{\mathcal{Y}}^{-1}_{0,0}
as required.

If the length is
>0
, by the induction hypothesis it is enough to prove the following:

(\ast)

Let
\tau\in\{{\mathcal{X}}_{1},{\mathcal{X}}_{-1},{\mathcal{W}}_{1},{\mathcal{W}}_{-1},{\mathcal{Z}}_{1},{\mathcal{Z}}_{-1}\}
. Then

{\mathcal{Y}}^{\ell(\ast)}_{k(\ast),n(\ast)}\tau

is equal to some

\sum\limits_{\ell,k,n}a^{\ell}_{k,n}{\mathcal{Y}}^{\ell}_{k,n}
.

Indeed, it is enough to check equality on the generators of

{\mathbb{M}}^{\ast}_{\bf D}
, that is the
x_{i}
’s. The proof of
(\ast)
is divided
into three cases:

Case 1:

{\mathcal{Y}}^{\ell(\ast)}_{k(\ast),n(\ast)}{\mathcal{W}}_{\ell}
is:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}{\mathcal{Y}}^{\ell(\ast)}_{k(\ast)+1,n(\ast)}&\mbox{ if }&\ell=1\\
{\mathcal{Y}}^{\ell(\ast)}_{k(\ast)-1,n(\ast)}&\mbox{ if }&\ell=-1\mbox{ and }\ k(\ast)+n(\ast)>0,\\
{\mathcal{Y}}^{\ell(\ast)}_{k(\ast)-1,n(\ast)+1}&\mbox{ if }&\ell=-1\mbox{ and }\ k(\ast)+n(\ast)=0.\end{array}

First assume that
\ell=1
. Then

\begin{array}[]{ll}{\mathcal{Y}}^{\ell(\ast)}_{k(\ast),n(\ast)}{\mathcal{W}}_{\ell}&={\mathcal{X}}_{1}({\mathcal{W}}_{-1})^{n}{\mathcal{W}}^{n+k}_{1}{\mathcal{W}}_{1}\\
&={\mathcal{X}}_{1}({\mathcal{W}}_{-1})^{n}{\mathcal{W}}^{n+k+1}\\
&={\mathcal{Y}}^{\ell(\ast)}_{k(\ast)+1,n(\ast)}.\end{array}

Now assume that
\ell=-1
and
k(\ast)+n(\ast)>0
. If
i\in\mathop{\mathrm{Rang}}(f)
,
then
x_{i}{\mathcal{W}}_{1}{\mathcal{W}}_{-1}=x_{i}
. From
this,

x_{i}{\mathcal{Y}}^{\ell(\ast)}_{k(\ast),n(\ast)}{\mathcal{W}}_{\ell}=x_{i}{\mathcal{Y}}^{\ell(\ast)}_{k(\ast)-1,n(\ast)}.

If
i\notin\mathop{\mathrm{Rang}}(f)
, then
i\in\{0,1\}
. It is easy to see that

x_{0}{\mathcal{Y}}^{\ell(\ast)}_{k(\ast),n(\ast)}{\mathcal{W}}_{-1}=x_{0}{\mathcal{Y}}^{\ell(\ast)}_{k(\ast)-1,n(\ast)}

and

x_{-1}{\mathcal{Y}}^{\ell(\ast)}_{k(\ast),n(\ast)}{\mathcal{W}}_{-1}=x{-1}{\mathcal{Y}}^{\ell(\ast)}_{k(\ast)-1,n(\ast)}.

Thus, the functions
{\mathcal{Y}}^{\ell(\ast)}_{k(\ast),n(\ast)}{\mathcal{W}}_{-1}
and
{\mathcal{Y}}^{\ell(\ast)}_{k(\ast)-1,n(\ast)}
are the same.
Finally, assume that
\ell=-1
and
k(\ast)+n(\ast)=0
. Then

\begin{array}[]{ll}{\mathcal{Y}}^{\ell(\ast)}_{k(\ast),n(\ast)}{\mathcal{W}}_{-1}&={\mathcal{X}}_{-1}({\mathcal{W}}_{-1})^{n}{\mathcal{W}}^{n+k}_{1}{\mathcal{W}}_{-1}\\
&={\mathcal{X}}_{-1}({\mathcal{W}}_{-1})^{n}{\mathcal{W}}_{-1}\\
&={\mathcal{X}}_{-1}({\mathcal{W}}_{-1})^{n+1}\\
&={\mathcal{Y}}^{\ell(\ast)}_{k(\ast)-1,n(\ast)+1}.\end{array}

This completes the argument.

Case 2:

{\mathcal{Y}}^{\ell(\ast)}_{k(\ast),n(\ast)}{\mathcal{X}}_{\ell}
is:

\mbox{zero }\quad\mbox{ if }\quad[\ell(\ast)=\ell\iff k(\ast)\mbox{ odd}],

{\mathcal{Y}}^{\ell(\ast)}_{k(\ast),n(\ast)}\mbox{if }\quad[\ell(\ast)=\ell\iff k(\ast)\mbox{ even}].

The proof of this is similar to Case 1.

Case 3:

{\mathcal{Y}}^{\ell(\ast)}_{k(\ast),n(\ast)}{\mathcal{Z}}_{\ell}
is:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}{\mathcal{Y}}^{\ell(\ast)}_{k(\ast),n(\ast)}&\mbox{ if }&n(\ast)+k(\ast)>0\mbox{
and }[\ell(\ast)=\ell\iff k(\ast)\mbox{ even}],\\
Y^{\ell(\ast)}_{k(\ast),n(\ast)+1}&\mbox{ if }&n(\ast)+k(\ast)=0\mbox{ and }[\ell(\ast)=\ell\iff k(\ast)\mbox{ even}],\\
\mbox{zero}&\mbox{ if }&[\ell(\ast)=\ell\iff k(\ast)\mbox{ odd}].\end{array}

It is enough to check equality on the generators of

{\mathbb{M}}^{\ast}_{\dot{\bf D}}
, that is the
x_{i}
’s. The proof is again similar to the proof of Case 1.

Step F)
: The set
\mathcal{G}=\{{\mathcal{Y}}^{\ell}_{k,n}:(\ell,k,n)\in w\}

generates
{\bf S}
freely as a
{\bf T}
-module.

In the light of Step E) we see that
\mathcal{G}
generates
{\bf S}
as a
{\bf T}
-module. Towards contradiction
suppose that
0=\sum\{a^{\ell}_{k,n}{\mathcal{Y}}^{\ell}_{k,n}:(\ell,k,n)\in w\}
in
{\bf S}

where
w\subseteq w^{\ast}
is finite,
a^{\ell}_{k,n}\in{\bf T}
and not
all of them are zero. If
n=|{\bf T}|
is finite, we take the field
\bf D
such that
\operatorname{char}({\bf D})|n
, and some
a^{\ell}_{k,n}

is not zero in
{\bf D}
. Hence,
0=\sum\{a^{\ell}_{k,n}:(\ell,k,n)\in w\}
as an endomorphism of
{\mathbb{M}}^{\ast}_{\bf D}
,
a left
{\bf D}
-module where
a^{\ell}_{k,n}\in{\bf D}
and
w\subseteq w^{\ast}
is finite. We
shall prove that
a^{\ell}_{k,n}=0
for every
(\ell,k,n)\in w
.

If
i\in A_{1}
and
i\geq 0
, then

\begin{array}[]{rlc}0&=x_{i}(\sum\limits_{(\ell,k,n)\in w}a^{\ell}_{k,n}{\mathcal{Y}}^{\ell}_{k,n})\\
&=\sum\limits_{(\ell,k,n)\in w}a^{\ell}_{k,n}(x_{i}{\mathcal{Y}}^{\ell}_{k,n})\\
&=\sum\limits_{(\ell,k,n)\in w}\{a^{\ell}_{k,n}x_{i+k}:\ell=1,\ \mbox{ and }n\leq i\}\\
&=\sum\limits_{j\geq 0}(\sum\limits_{(1,k,n)\in w}\{a^{1}_{k,n}:\ \ i\geq n,\ i+k=j\})x_{j}\\
&=\sum\limits_{j\geq 0}(\sum\limits_{(1,j-i,n)\in w}\{a^{1}_{j-i,n}:\ i\geq n,\ \})x_{j}.\end{array}

Hence for every
i\in A_{1}
,
i\geq 0
and
j\geq 0
we have

(\ast)^{a}_{i,j}
:

0=\sum\{a^{1}_{j-i,n}:\ n\geq 0,\ n\leq i\mbox{ and
}n+(j-i)\geq 0\}
.

Similarly, for
i\in A_{-1}
,
i\geq 0
(equivalently,
i>0
as
i\in A_{-1}\ \Rightarrow\ i\neq 0
) and
j\geq 0
we can prove

(\ast)^{b}_{i,j}
:

0=\sum\{a^{-1}_{j-i,n}:\ n\geq 0,\ n\leq i\mbox{ and
}n+(j-i)\geq 0\}
.

Similarly, for
i\in A_{1}
,
i<0
,

\begin{array}[]{rlc}0&=x_{i}(\sum\limits_{(\ell,k,n)\in w}a^{\ell}_{k,n}{\mathcal{Y}}^{\ell}_{k,n})\\
&=\sum\limits_{(\ell,k,n)\in w}a^{\ell}_{k,n}(x_{i}{\mathcal{Y}}^{\ell}_{k,n})\\
&=\sum\limits_{(1,k,n)\in w}\{a^{1}_{k,n}x_{i-k}:\mbox{ }-i>n\}\\
&=\sum\limits_{j<0}(\sum_{(1,i-j,n)\in w}\{a^{1}_{i-j,n}:\mbox{ }n<-i\})x_{j}.\end{array}

Hence for every
i\in A_{1}
,
i<0
and
j<0
we have

(\ast)^{c}_{i,j}
:

0=\sum\{a^{1}_{i-j,n}:n\geq 0
and
n+(i-j)\geq 0

and
n<-i\}
.

Similarly, for every
i\in A_{-1}
,
i<0
and
j<0

(\ast)^{d}_{i,j}
:

0=\sum\{a^{-1}_{i-j,n}:\ n\geq 0\mbox{ and }n+(i-j)\geq 0\mbox{ and }n<-i\}
.

Choose, if possible,
(k,m)
such that:

(1)

(1,k,m)
belongs to
w
,

(2)

a^{1}_{k,m}\neq 0
,

(3)

m
is minimal under (1)+(2).

Note that
m\geq 0
by the definition of

w
. First assume that
m
is even. Let
i=m
and
j=i+k
. So
i\in A_{1}
(being even),
i\geq 0
and

j=m+k
is
\geq 0
as
(1,k,m)\in w
. In the equation
(\ast)^{a}_{i,j}

the term
a^{1}_{k,m}
appears in the sum, and for every other term
a^{1}_{k_{1},m_{1}}
which appears in the sum, we have
m_{1}<m
(and
k_{1}=k
), and hence by
(3) above is zero.
So it follows that
a^{1}_{k,m}
is zero, a contradiction.

If
m
is odd, we get a similar contradiction using
(\ast)^{c}_{i,j}
. Let

i=-m-1
and
j=i-k
. Note that
m\geq 0
, hence
i<0
and
i
is even as
m
is odd,
so
i\in A_{1}
. Also
j=i-k=-m-1-k\leq-1<0
(recalling
k+m\geq 0
as

(1,k,m)\in w^{*}
). In the equation

(\ast)^{c}_{i,j}
, the term
a^{1}_{i-j,n}=a^{1}_{k,n}
appears in the sum if and only if

0\leq n<-i=m+1
, and
n+(i-j)=n+k\geq 0
(but if the later fails,

a^{1}_{k,m}
is not defined) so
a^{1}_{k,m}
appears, and if another term

a^{1}_{k_{1},m_{1}}
occurs then
m_{1}\leq m
(and
k_{1}=k
). Hence
m_{1}<m
and so

a^{1}_{k_{1},m_{1}}=0
. Necessarily
a^{1}_{k,m}
is zero, a contradiction.

So
a^{1}_{k,n}=0
whenever it is defined.

Similarly
a^{-1}_{k,n}=0
whenever it is defined (use
(\ast)^{b}_{i,j}+(\ast)^{d}_{i,j}
). So,
{\bf S}_{0}
is a free module over
{\bf T}
, as required.

Step G)
: In this step we get a contradiction that
we searched for it. This will show that
{\mathbb{M}}_{1}\not\cong{\mathbb{M}}_{-1}
.

To this end, recall
that there are finitely many nonzero
a^{\ell}_{k,n}\in{\bf D}

such that

\boxtimes
:

{\mathcal{Y}}=\sum\left\{a^{\ell}_{k,n}{\mathcal{Y}}^{\ell}_{k,n}:\ n\geq 0\mbox{ and }k+n\geq 0\mbox{ and }\ell\in\{1,-1\}\right\}.

Let
n(\ast)<\omega
be such that

a^{\ell}_{k,n}\neq 0\quad\Rightarrow\quad|k|,n<n(\ast).

For
\ell=1,-1
let

\begin{array}[]{lr}{\mathbb{M}}^{\mathord{\mathrm{pos}}}_{\ell}:=\big\{\sum\limits_{i\geq 0}d_{i}x_{i}:&d_{i}\in{\bf D},\mbox{ all but finitely many of }d_{i}^{\prime}s\mbox{ are zero }\\
&\mbox{ and }d_{i}\neq 0\ \Rightarrow\ i\in A_{\ell}\big\},\\
{\mathbb{M}}^{\rm neg}_{\ell}:=\big\{\sum\limits_{i<0}d_{i}x_{i}:&d_{i}\in{\bf D},\mbox{ all but finitely many of }d_{i}^{\prime}s\mbox{ are zero }\\
&\mbox{ and }d_{i}\neq 0\ \Rightarrow\ i\in A_{\ell}\big\}.\end{array}

Clearly as a
{\bf D}
-module

{\mathbb{M}}^{\ast}_{\bf D}={\mathbb{M}}^{\mathord{\mathrm{pos}}}_{1}\oplus{\mathbb{M}}^{\mathord{\mathrm{pos}}}_{-1}\oplus{\mathbb{M}}^{\rm neg}_{1}\oplus{\mathbb{M}}^{\rm neg}_{-1}.

Let
{\mathcal{Y}}^{\mathord{\mathrm{pos}}}_{\ell}:={\mathcal{Y}}\restriction{\mathbb{M}}^{\mathord{\mathrm{pos}}}_{\ell}
and

{\mathcal{Y}}^{\rm neg}_{\ell}:={\mathcal{Y}}\restriction{\mathbb{M}}^{\rm neg}_{\ell}
for
\ell\in\{1,-1\}
.

Now each
{\mathcal{Y}}^{\ell}_{k,n}
maps
{\mathbb{M}}^{\mathord{\mathrm{pos}}}={\mathbb{M}}^{\mathord{\mathrm{pos}}}_{1}\oplus{\mathbb{M}}^{\mathord{\mathrm{pos}}}_{-1}
to itself,
and
{\mathbb{M}}^{\rm neg}={\mathbb{M}}^{\rm neg}_{1}\oplus{\mathbb{M}}^{\rm neg}_{-1}
to itself, and hence by

\boxtimes
above also
{\mathcal{Y}}
does it. According to
(\ast)_{2}
from Step B) we have

{\mathcal{X}}_{1}{\mathcal{Y}}{\mathcal{X}}_{-1}={\mathcal{X}}_{1}{\mathcal{Y}}
. Hence

{\mathbb{M}}_{1}{\mathcal{Y}}={\mathbb{M}}{\mathcal{X}}_{1}{\mathcal{Y}}={\mathbb{M}}{\mathcal{X}}_{1}{\mathcal{Y}}{\mathcal{X}}_{-1}\subset{\mathbb{M}}{\mathcal{X}}_{-1}={\mathbb{M}}_{-1},

i.e.,
{\mathcal{Y}}
maps
{\mathbb{M}}_{1}
to
{\mathbb{M}}_{-1}
. Thus, by the
previous sentence,
{\mathcal{Y}}
maps
{\mathbb{M}}^{\mathord{\mathrm{pos}}}_{1}
into

{\mathbb{M}}^{\mathord{\mathrm{pos}}}_{-1}
, and
{\mathbb{M}}^{\rm neg}_{1}

into
{\mathbb{M}}^{\rm neg}_{-1}
, i.e.,
{\mathcal{Y}}^{\mathord{\mathrm{pos}}}_{1}
(resp.
{\mathcal{Y}}^{\rm neg}_{1}
) is into

{\mathbb{M}}^{\mathord{\mathrm{pos}}}_{-1}
(resp.
{\mathbb{M}}^{\rm neg}_{-1}
).

Similarly by
(\ast)_{2}
we have
{\mathcal{X}}_{-1}{\mathcal{Y}}{\mathcal{X}}_{1}={\mathcal{X}}_{-1}{\mathcal{Y}}
, hence
{\mathcal{Y}}
maps

{\mathbb{M}}^{\mathord{\mathrm{pos}}}_{-1}
into
{\mathbb{M}}^{\mathord{\mathrm{pos}}}_{1}
and
{\mathbb{M}}^{\rm neg}_{-1}

into
{\mathbb{M}}^{\rm neg}_{1}
. Also, the
mappings
{\mathcal{Y}}^{\mathord{\mathrm{pos}}}_{1}
,
{\mathcal{Y}}^{\mathord{\mathrm{pos}}}_{-1}
,
{\mathcal{Y}}^{\rm neg}_{1}
,

{\mathcal{Y}}^{\rm neg}_{-1}
are
endomorphisms of
{\bf D}
-modules. As
{\mathcal{Y}}^{2}=1
(again by
(\ast)_{2}
)
we conclude that

{\mathcal{Y}}^{\mathord{\mathrm{pos}}}_{1}
and
{\mathcal{Y}}^{\mathord{\mathrm{pos}}}_{-1}
are the inverse of each
other, so both of them are
isomorphisms. Similarly for
{\mathcal{Y}}^{\rm neg}_{1}
and
{\mathcal{Y}}^{\rm neg}_{-1}
.

Let

\begin{array}[]{lr}{\mathbb{M}}^{\mathord{\mathrm{stp}}}_{1}:=\{\sum\limits_{i>0}d_{i}x_{i}:&d_{i}\in{\bf D},\mbox{ all
but finitely many $d_{i}$'s are zero }\\
&\mbox{and }d_{i}\neq 0\ \Rightarrow\ i\in A_{1}\}.\end{array}

Clearly
{\mathbb{M}}^{\mathord{\mathrm{stp}}}_{1}
is a sub
{\bf D}
-module of
{\mathbb{M}}^{\mathord{\mathrm{pos}}}_{1}
.
Note that
x_{0}\in{\mathbb{M}}^{\mathord{\mathrm{pos}}}_{1}\setminus{\mathbb{M}}^{\mathord{\mathrm{stp}}}_{1}
. This yields the
difference between
{\mathbb{M}}^{\mathord{\mathrm{stp}}}_{1}
and
{\mathbb{M}}^{\mathord{\mathrm{pos}}}_{1}
.

Let

\begin{array}[]{lr}{\mathbb{N}}:=\{\sum\limits_{i>n(\ast)}d_{i}x_{i}:&d_{i}\in{\bf D},\mbox{ all but finitely many of }d_{i}^{\prime}s\mbox{ are zero }\\
&\mbox{and }d_{i}\neq 0\ \Rightarrow\ i\in A_{1}\}.\end{array}

Let
H^{\mathord{\mathrm{pos}}}:{\mathbb{M}}_{1}^{\mathord{\mathrm{stp}}}\longrightarrow{\mathbb{M}}_{1}^{\rm neg}

be defined by
x_{i}H^{\mathord{\mathrm{pos}}}=x_{-i}
and
H^{\rm neg}:{\mathbb{M}}^{\rm neg}_{-1}\longrightarrow{\mathbb{M}}^{\mathord{\mathrm{stp}}}_{-1}

be defined by

x_{i}H^{\rm neg}=x_{-i}
. Both of them are isomorphisms of

{\bf D}
-modules. Note that
{\mathcal{Y}}^{\mathord{\mathrm{pos}}}_{1}
is an isomorphism from

{\mathbb{M}}^{\mathord{\mathrm{pos}}}_{1}
onto
{\mathbb{M}}^{\mathord{\mathrm{pos}}}_{-1}
and

H^{\mathord{\mathrm{pos}}}{\mathcal{Y}}^{\rm neg}_{1}H^{\rm neg}
is an
isomorphism from
{\mathbb{M}}^{\mathord{\mathrm{stp}}}_{1}
onto
{\mathbb{M}}^{\mathord{\mathrm{pos}}}_{-1}
. Note that

{\mathbb{M}}^{\mathord{\mathrm{stp}}}_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle H^{\mathord{\mathrm{pos}}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\mathbb{M}}^{\rm neg}_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\mathcal{Y}}^{\rm neg}_{1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\mathbb{M}}^{\rm neg}_{-1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle H^{\rm neg}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\mathbb{M}}^{\mathord{\mathrm{pos}}}_{-1}.

We show that

{\mathcal{Y}}^{\mathord{\mathrm{pos}}}_{1}\restriction{\mathbb{N}}=(H^{\mathord{\mathrm{pos}}}{\mathcal{Y}}^{\rm neg}_{1}H^{\rm neg})\restriction{\mathbb{N}}.

To see this, it is enough to check equality on the generators of

{\mathbb{N}}
, that is over the
x_{i}
’s where
i
is even and it is larger than
n(\ast)
.
In particular, by choosing
n(\ast)
large enough, we may assume that
i\gg 0
.
Recall that

{\mathcal{Y}}=\sum\left\{a^{\ell}_{k,n}{\mathcal{Y}}^{\ell}_{k,n}:\ n\geq 0\mbox{ and }k+n\geq 0\mbox{ and }\ell\in\{1,-1\}\right\}.

By definition
x_{i}{\mathcal{X}}_{-1}=0
. Then

\begin{array}[]{ll}x_{i}(H^{\mathord{\mathrm{pos}}}{\mathcal{Y}}^{\rm neg}_{1}H^{\rm neg})&=x_{-i}(\sum\{a^{\ell}_{k,n}{\mathcal{Y}}^{\ell}_{k,n}\})H^{\rm neg}\\
&=\sum a^{\ell}_{k,n}x_{-i}({\mathcal{X}}_{1}{\mathcal{W}}_{-1}^{n}{\mathcal{W}}^{n+k}_{1})H^{\rm neg}\\
&=\sum a^{1}_{k,n}(x_{-i}{\mathcal{W}}_{-1}^{n}{\mathcal{W}}^{n+k}_{1})H^{\rm neg}\\
&=\sum a^{1}_{k,n}(x_{f^{-n}(-i)}{\mathcal{W}}^{n+k}_{1})H^{\rm neg}\\
&=\sum a^{1}_{k,n}(x_{f^{n+k}(f^{-n}(-i))})H^{\rm neg}\\
&=\sum a^{1}_{k,n}(x_{f^{k}(-i)})H^{\rm neg}\\
&=\sum a^{1}_{k,n}(x_{-i-k)})H^{\rm neg}\\
&=\sum a^{1}_{k,n}x_{i+k}.\end{array}

On
the other hand,

(x_{i}){\mathcal{Y}}^{\mathord{\mathrm{pos}}}_{1}\restriction{\mathbb{N}}=\sum a^{1}_{k,n}x_{i+k}
. Thus we have

{\mathcal{Y}}^{\mathord{\mathrm{pos}}}_{1}\restriction{\mathbb{N}}=(H^{\mathord{\mathrm{pos}}}{\mathcal{Y}}^{\rm neg}_{1}H^{\rm neg})\restriction{\mathbb{N}}
, as claimed.

Let
{\mathbb{N}}^{\ast}:=\mathop{\mathrm{Rang}}({\mathcal{Y}}^{\mathord{\mathrm{pos}}}_{1}\restriction{\mathbb{N}})
. Then

{\mathbb{N}}^{\ast}=\mathop{\mathrm{Rang}}((H^{\mathord{\mathrm{pos}}}{\mathcal{Y}}^{\rm neg}_{1}H^{\rm neg})\restriction{\mathbb{N}})
.
So, as
{\mathcal{Y}}^{\mathord{\mathrm{pos}}}_{1}
is an
isomorphism from
{\mathbb{M}}^{\mathord{\mathrm{pos}}}_{1}
onto
{\mathbb{M}}^{\mathord{\mathrm{pos}}}_{-1}
,
and
{\mathbb{N}}\subseteq{\mathbb{M}}^{\mathord{\mathrm{pos}}}_{1}
we
have that
{\mathbb{N}}^{\ast}
is a sub
\bf D
-module of

{\mathbb{M}}^{\mathord{\mathrm{pos}}}_{-1}
. This means that
{\mathbb{M}}^{\mathord{\mathrm{pos}}}_{-1}/{\mathbb{N}}^{\ast}
is isomorphic to
{\mathbb{M}}^{\mathord{\mathrm{pos}}}_{1}/{\mathbb{N}}
(as
\bf D
-modules).

But
H^{\mathord{\mathrm{pos}}}{\mathcal{Y}}^{\rm neg}_{1}H^{\rm neg}
is an isomorphism from

{\mathbb{M}}^{\mathord{\mathrm{stp}}}_{1}
onto

{\mathbb{M}}^{\mathord{\mathrm{pos}}}_{-1}
and
{\mathbb{N}}\subseteq{\mathbb{M}}^{\mathord{\mathrm{stp}}}_{1}
,
and it maps
{\mathbb{N}}
onto
{\mathbb{N}}^{\ast}

(see above), so
{\mathbb{M}}^{\mathord{\mathrm{stp}}}_{1}/{\mathbb{N}}
is isomorphic to

{\mathbb{M}}^{\mathord{\mathrm{pos}}}_{-1}/{\mathbb{N}}^{\ast}
. By
the previous paragraph we get

{\mathbb{M}}^{\mathord{\mathrm{stp}}}_{1}/{\mathbb{N}}\cong{\mathbb{M}}^{\mathord{\mathrm{pos}}}_{-1}/{\mathbb{N}}^{\ast}\cong{\mathbb{M}}^{\mathord{\mathrm{pos}}}_{1}/{\mathbb{N}}.

On the one hand,
{\mathbb{M}}^{\mathord{\mathrm{pos}}}_{1}/{\mathbb{N}}
is a free

\bf D
-module, as
\{x_{2i}+{\mathbb{N}}:0\leq 2i\leq n(\ast)\}
is a free basis for it and also
{\mathbb{M}}^{\mathord{\mathrm{stp}}}_{1}/{\mathbb{N}}

is a free
\bf D
-module with the base
\{x_{2i}+{\mathbb{N}}:0<2i\leq n(\ast)\}
. On the other hand, the number of their generators differ by
1
. This is a contradiction that we searched for it.

The theorem follows.
∎
